F22(*.M AND GARDEN.

LORD SELBORNE'S ADVICE.

The conditions of farming vary so much in the different localities that. I cannot attempt to make any general recommendation for your guidance as to the means you should adopt for increasing the production of food on your hold- ing. That must be left to your judgment and to the advice of your friends and neighbours. But I suggest that you should consider whether you cannot attain the object in view by one or more of the following methods : (a) By ploughing up those of your poorest permanent grass fields which are suit- able for conversion, and increasing your arable land. (b) By shortening the period for which your existing arable land is kept under clover or rotation grasses. tc) By improving your remaining grass land so that it will carry more stock.

(d) By reducing your acreage of bare fallow wherever possible. _ _

I realise that increased production cannot be obtained without a great effort, and that far- mers have to face many special difficulties in these times. It is my desire to help you to overcome these difficulties by all the means in my power.

"Give up your labourers to the Army, hand over your spare cash to the tax-collector, but mind you keep the cultivation of the farm at its highest point, and plough more land than heretofore."

This is Lord Selborne's advice, says "Farm and Home." The farmers think he is quite right in saying he is asking them to "do the impossible." But they wish he had described how he himself would set out to follow the advice lie gives.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN AND THE COMING SHORTAGE OF LABOUR.

What he said to the farmers was, "Use every shift you can for labour, for I warn you quite fairly and squarely that you are to lose many more of your men in the coming few months." These are the words with which Lord Selborne is warning the country as to the immediate future.

He had recently, he said, seen in Surrey what he believed nobody had ever seen in England before—he' saw a woman ploughing. Having seen it he took his courage in both hands. It was no use saying it was impossible for women to plough, because there were many women at the plough in Europe to-day. He used that as an extreme illustration of the straits to which farmers might be put; but he wanted those present who were the leaders of agriculture to teach the people and' to make it clear that a man might play his part to win the war al- though he never left his farm. There were all the labourers and the dwellers in our villages— men upon whom the call in proportion was as great ,as on the landowners or 'farmers. He would say to them, "Make your gardens and allotments produce more than they have ever done before." Women of every class must assist. The squire's wife, and the farmer's, and the parson's wife, the wife and the daughter of the labourers, each in turn could make a con- tribution to agriculture in this year of war, and so work for victory, just as husband, -son, or brother, in the Fleet or in the trenches. He would make a special appeal to the wives and daughters of men who were fighting, because they were well .cared for by the nation. They had not been left, as were the German women, in grinding poverty, while the men were fight- ing the battles. It was not right that a woman in this country should :live. in *greater 'luxury than she did before her husband or son went away to fight; she should do her part just as the men. She must go on to the land if the farmer asked her, at a fair wage for a fair day's work. In the course of the proceedings criticism of some of Lord Selborne's proposals was uttered. It was argued that without a Government guarantee it would be financially risky to work more arable land, especially with the present shortage of labour, and that it would be diffi- cult if not impossible to get. women to under- take to work on the land.

Lord Se'borne, in reply, said that he did not ask any man to sacrifice his capital, but he asked him to sacrifice everything els., includ- ing leisure and prejudices. In regard to food production he was not talking only of wheat, but he meant also milk and cheese, oats and potatoes, and all food production. "You say," he added, "that it is no good going to the women and asking them to work upon the land. Well, I do not believe it. Lord Kitch- ener told me the other day that there was nothing he had done in making his new armies which a dozen people of the first authority had not told him was impossible. Well, I ask you to do the impossible."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OCTOBER OUTLOOK.

The reports furnished by the Crop Reporters of the Board on agricultural conditions in England and Wales state that the weather of September, which was everywhere a very fine month, proved excellent for the completion of the harvest. The cereal crops were all secured in very good condition, though there is still some outstanding corn in the hilly districts. The dry weather arrested the progress of dis- ease among the potatoes; and it is very fre- quently reported that the disease affected only the haulms without reaching the tubers. Most counties, however, mention its existence, al- though it appears to be little more prevalent than usual, except perhaps in Lancashire and some parts of the south-east of England. A certain amount of autumn cultivation has been done, varying very much according to the locality. In many parts it is rather backward owing to the ground being too hard, and in other districts owing to the harvest having been rather late; on the whole, work is per- haps rather`behindhand. In only very few instances is any wheat reported to have been sown by the end of September. • Pastures in some districts were getting rather bare at the end of the month, but generally there was plenty of keep. Live stock have done well generally. Labour has been every- where more or less scarce, but the fine weather, by reducing the imbiber of lost days, has caused the deficiency to be less felt than would have been the case with a wet harvest. In many cases women have helped in the fields. In some districts some apprehension is felt as to the sufficiency of labour for autumn cultiva- tion.

A LATE SHOW OF BLOOMS.

I have cut about 30 blooms this, last week-end of Druschki, Lyon, Harry Kirk, C. Testout, Lady Ashtown, Betty, and Jos. Hill. The blooms on C. Testout now are about five inches across.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
BULBS FOR THE GARDEN.

Now is the time to plant bulbs, and grand sorts can be obtained from the local seedsmen. The prices may be a shade higher on account of the war. Most of the bulbs come from Holland, but equally as good bulbs or narcissi and daffodils are grown in England. These class of plants require no manure, and all thrive in a light rich soil with leaf mould and a sprinkling of lime and bone-meal added. The Double White Narcissi or Alba Odorato Pleno is the only one that likes a heavy or clayey soil, so in light soils plant this bulb about 9in. deep. Good varieties to grow are :

Daffodils.—Emperor and Goldext Spur (yellows), Empress, Victoria and Horsefeldi (bicolours), Madame M. de Graaf (white). Narcissi .—Sir Watkin, Barn i Conspicuus, Cynosure, Mrs. Lantry, Katherine Spurrell and Figaro. Poets Narcissi.—Horace, Ornatus, Poetarum and Pheasant Eye. Double Narcissus.—Sulphur Phoenix and Alba Odorato Pheno.

Narcissus Poetaz.—The finest of all for cut- ting, a cross between Ornatus and Polyanthus Narcissi, giving three to five large- blooms on a stem almost as big as Pheasant Eye, nicely scented and easy to grow. The best varieties are Elvira Aspasia and Irene. Tulips.—The single tulips are the best, and good varieties to grow are: Keizerkroon, Crim- son King, Duchess de Parma, Prince of Austria, Thos. Moore, Golden Prince, Cottage Maid, La Reine, La Belle Alliance, Artus and Cramoise Brilliant.

Double Tulips.—Murillo, Crown of Gold and Rubra Maxima. When planting tulips put a small handful of sand under each bulb, and plant about Din, apart. Hyacinths.—Use the second size for bedding and top size for forcing, good sorts are: Baroness van Thuyl, La Grandesse, La Inno- cence (whites), Marie, Grand Matre, Pineman, Queen of the Blues (blue), Gen. Pellisier, King of the Belgians (red), Gertrude (rose), Gigantea (blush), Yellow Hammer (yellow). Spanish and English Iris.—These are our cheapest and handsomest of flowers, yet one rarely see them in our gardens. They are termed the "Poor Man's Orchid," and must not be confused with the German Flag Iris, greatly seen in nearly every garden. English Iris are like Spanish only larger and a shade later, and a bigger bulb. All are beautiful, sl there is no necessity to name them; plant 3in. deep. . Early Gladioli.—These are grand for cutting and are very cheap, plant about 4 to Gin. deep in good soil, with a sprinkling of sand around the bulbs to preserve them till they get going. These, like irises, look best in clumps of 12 bulbs, and all are beautiful, The Bride being a grand variety. Other bulbs for edging are Snowdrops, Crocus, Grape Hyacinths (blue and white) and Blue Squills.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
RASPBERRY SEMPER FIDELIS.

In a garden we visited lately we found several rows of this raspberry fruiting freely, the fruits being quite equal to those borne in July, the quantity of green fruit indicating that, given mild weather, raspberries would be forthcoming for some time. As this variety fruits at the points of the current year's shoots, the old wood may be entirely cut away in the spring. Young vigorous shoots will push up from the base during the summer, and on these the fruit in the autumn is borne. To make mire of suc- cess a rich, moist soil is necessary, with, when the fruit is swelling, a good mulch of rotten manure, and plenty of water should the season be dry. The plants referred to as bearing so well were heavily mulched with moss litter that, after having passed through the stable, was allowed to lie in a heap for some time.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
BEDDING PLANTS.

It is not now safe to leave unprotected any bedding plants, as zonal pelargoniums, the cut- tings of which have been struck in the open air. These should be potted up at once, or if they have been rooted in boxes or pots they should, if room can be found for them, be placed in the greenhouse, or 'care taken that they are pro- tected at night until room can be found for them in a structure the temperatikre of which can be kept at about 45 degrees. To guard against loss by frost calceolaiia cuttings should also be got in, the' best place for them being a cold frame. In the bottom of the. cold frame place some rotten leaves, and on this 4 inches of loam to which some leaf Soil and sand have been added, finishing with an inch layer of sand into which the cuttings can be dibbled. Keep close for a week until the cuttings have begun to callus.. It is an easy matter to protect these cuttings with bracken or litter should severe weather follow. Tufted pansies may be treated in the same way.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREASING HARDY PLANTS.

I Many of our hardy plants are now furnished ' with healthy young shoots, which; if taken off and inserted in handlights or a frame, will soon root. If handlights are used these • should be placed on a north or partially shaded border into which has been worked. a mixture of sand and leaf soil. Into these may be dibbled the cuttings of such as -pentstemons, snapdragons (which have been very fine this year), phloxes, and many others. Phloxes, pyrethrums, del- phiniums, chrysanthemum maximum, stenactis, &c., that are apt to ,become overcrowded, may be divided and replanted. thus allowing the off- sets to get established before the winter.. In the division of such plants as are mentioned above one word of warning is necessary, and , that is to select only the growths that are found round the outer edges of the clumps, throwing • away the woody and useless centre parts. In dividing it is always advisable to lift the various chimps bodily and shake away all the soil, as in this way the root system is laid bare, and the breaking up can be more easily carried out. If the plants have been growing in the same place for some years fresh quarters should be found for the young plants. If room is scarce and the necessity 'arises to use the same place, then see to it—that the ground for the new occupants is deeply trenched and well manured previous to replanting.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDA OCTOBER 16, 1915.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

IN t; 11,1,11'01,1.

Lieutenant Gordon Miln And A Roast

Beef Experiment.

From the Gallipoli Peninsula, under date of September Ist, Lieut. Gordon Miln, of the Cheshires, says :—" I have just come out of the trenches after fifteen clays' hard work there, and a slight attack of dysentery is the result. Although the sickness gets hold of one suddenly, it goes away just as quickly, which is a bless- ing." In a later communication, dated September 7th, he states :—" Ever since we came to our new quarters we have been toiling day and night. Last night I was detailed to take 100 men to the beach to unload a cargo, and we had some work to do when we got there. A tug brought a lighter Close to the shore, and then we had two hours'. solid work carting boxes up from the water's edge to the Army Service Corps Stores. We had also some carcases of frozen meat to haul up, so we were very tired when we landed back at 2.30 a.m. We had to start again on our dug-outs at 10 a.m., improving them for winter quarters, so that is what we get every day and night. Our dug-outs are situated on the top of a cliff by the water's edge, and these we dig out all day, whilst at night we go down to the beach for unloading, or go up inland to make trenches. We hear various rumours of Turkey suing for peace, but we do not put too much trust in them, much as we should like to see the business finished In fact, the place we are in at present is destine) for the winter quarters of the Army in this par- ticular part of the Peninsula. I shall have to close soon, as I am obliged to take a digging party to the trenches and have to see to the preparation of a meal beforehand. We have rigged up an oven to-night and are experiment-

ingwith roast beef, which will be a great luxury to us. We have iust received an addition to our company this morning of 25 men from Birkenhead to fill up some of our gaps."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM GALLIPOLI.

Drummer J. Clayton's Graphic Description Of His Experiences.

Drummer John Clayton, of the South Lancashires—whose age is 17 years, and who lives at Haydock-street, Warrington, writes from the Convalescent Depot Citadel, Cairo, a graphic letter to Mr. S. W. Jeffery, headmaster of the Wycliffe Council Schools. He says:—

Lost The Toss. "It is now four months since we left Eng- land, and three months since we set foot on the Gallipoli Peninsula. Two days after our land-

"601N6 TO STRAFFE THEM."

"I am still in the trenches," says a writer to the Orford Parish Magazine, "and I am just going to observe .some of our shooting. We are just going to straffe them, and I have not another second to spare now."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
"YOU WOULD LAUGH "

" Tommy " Waxes Merry Over Turks'

Gunnery-Work.

Mrs. W. Stokes, of Lord Nelson-street, has received the following letter from her husband, Gunner Stokes, who is serving with the R.F.A. in Gallipoli:— " It is very hot here in the daytime, but it is nice and cool in the night. The flies are awful; they do not give us any peace, and they make things more uncomfortable. We have arrived at the scene of the fighting at last. We are in the firing line now, and the shells and bullets are going over, so I can tell you it is a case of keeping low. We are in a pretty good position, and not in much danger unless the enemy land a shell right on our gun, and then they would shift us very quickly. You would laugh to see them trying to bring our areo- planes down; but I don't think they could hit a town. There is one good thing about it, and that is we are getting some very good food, and there .seems to be plenty of water, so that is something to be thankful for."

REINFORCEMENTS!

Trench Opinion On Bands For

'`` Slackers."

Private W. Whittle, of the Ist King's Liver- pool Regiment, who lives at 394, Knutsford-road, Warrington, but is now "with the British Expeditionary Force," writes "Would you mind finding me a corner in your paper, which I receive every week? "Well, I am tired of reading about the bands parading round the streets to get recruits. I say this: If they won't answer Kitchener's call, then fetch them!' I have been out here since March, and have been in two big fights, which have cost many lives, and we want reinforce- ments to back us up. It would be a great pleasure for the Warrington boys out here to see some of the old faces standing by them to back them up and to fight side by side. "The weather is getting rather cold out here. and the people of Warrington must consider what it means if we don't get these slackers to join in with us. They will just be like a non- union man trying to work by•the side of Union men and getting it thrown into his face every time they speak. "If they want a band to play to them let them come in the trenches and they will hear the music, as our pal says, that is. Corporal Tombs, V.C. We have a band which Lord Derby gave to us, after our great victory in May last at

"Will you young men of Warrington just buck yourselves up and don't be like a lot of little school lads? Answer the call to-day. Don't wait till to-morrow, it never coms.-1 remains, yours, W. Whittle."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
" WELL REPAID ! "

German Gunnery Efforts Repaid With

Interest.

Writing to a friend in Warrington, Gunner H. Thomson—a native of the town and a member of the Young Men's Bible Cass at the Working Men's Mission—announces that he is attached to a Howitzer Battery of the Ist Canadian Division, and that he was, when posting his letter a few days ago, "in the best of health; indeed, 'in the pink of condition." He adds that, from what he has heard, there must be quite a lot of young fellows from the Mission out at the Front. "And," he goes on, "before this lot is over every available man will be needed. The sights of ruin and destruc- tion out here are enough to make any young fellow feel like doing his best to exact revenge —revenge for the cruelties and barbarities that have been perpetrated. . . . The fellows I am with now are a decent lot of chaps, and it would surprise many of the folks at home to see how we pull together. The Germans send us plenty of shells, but, believe me, they are well repaid—our battery sees to that. I believe that our bombs 'put the wind-up the Huns."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
WINTER CAMPAIGN.

Pri‘ate .1. McMahon Tells Graphically Of Some Hardships.

Private John McMahon, of the Ist Leinsters, now in France, in peace times of Carlisle-street, Widnes, single, and an employe of the Brough- ton Copper Yard—first enlisted fifteen years ago. On the war outbreak he was called up with the Resprve, and at once sent out. To his mother, Mr.§: Alice Reddican, of 31, Watkin- street, Warrington, he writes : '• , Hard Conditions.

"I only came out of the trenches yesterday for a rest, and I can,, tell your we needed it. We were covered over with clay from head to foot, it rained every day'; we were over the knees in water and we had no sleep for seven days. We don't get much food in the trenches, but we get plenty when we are resting; we will be resting about a week before we go back. "We had about 32 killed this last time. We were only 30 yards from the German trenches; we used to be %outing at them every night. They are called the Prussian Guards: nearly every one was over six feet—a fine lot of men to look at.

"It is grand out here at night time to see the German limelights; they keep throwing them up all night, you would think you were in a picture palace.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
Plashed Germans Good Way Back.

"We have pushed the Germans a good way back; if we keep on we will soon have them out of France, and then they would give in as they won't fight on their own ground. There are some grand sights out here. There are cities and big towns blown to the ground. They dropped a shell into a barn my company was in, and killed 36. I happened to be out at the time, looking to see if I could get anything. I had a German helmet, but I lost it since; but I will have one before I come home. There are only three of us here that came out 12 months last August; some big fine fellows do come out here, but they can't stick the hardships—they keep going into hospital and getting Sent home. "I never felt better in my life since I came out here. I could have got a pass to come home for five days, but I wouldn't take it, it was no use coming home, for I would no sooner be at home than I would have to come back— and any way, I had my time last August. I have done twelve years since 1903, but we have to do an extra year in war time.

Lot of Frost Now.

"We are getting a lot of frost this last week, and I can tell you it is cold in the trenches. We are not allowed a blanket or any kind of clothes except our top coats, and they are nearly worn out.

"Last week ten of us and a Sergeant were sent for water, and when we were coming back a shell dropped in among us and blew the Sergeant away and wounded six of the others. I ran out and carried one' in and bandaged him up as best I could till the doctor came; then I went to look for the Sergeant, but of course he was in an awful state—we had to put Jura in a sack and bury him. I see a lot of sights like this, but we are used to them now. The officer said he would recommend me, because if the Germans had happened to drop another shell I would have gone after the Sergeant. I heard the poor fellow I bandaged died a couple of days after. - Nearly all the young soldiers get hit when they come out here; the Germans know when a fresh regiment is in the trenches and they try all kinds of games on then', they know it is no use trying it on us as we know them. I often pity the new soldiers coming out."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE EDUCATION RATE.

Revision Of The Estimates With A View To Economy.

1, At a Meeting of the iCheshire Education Com- mittee, at Crewe, reference was made to the desire to levy a rate exceeding 2(1. in the pound for higher education purposes in Cheshire.. Although the limitlad been given at 2d. in the Pound the, Cheshire authority had recently levied a rate of 21(1. in the pound. This, how- ever, was found to be insufficient, and steps were taken to obtain official sanction for an in- crease. The Clerk said he had received a letter stating that the Local Government Board had been in communication with the Board of Education on the matter and had not yet received their 'final reply. The letter added: "The Board may state, however, that it was improbable the application of the Cheshire County Council can be acceded to as it stands."

It was decided, however, to try to bring the estimate within a 2d. rate. It was stated that a further revision of the estimates for evening class education resulted in a reduction of the estimated expenditure by £4,217 on the amount of £20,547 originally sanctioned.

ing there we took our place in the firing line, I being a stretcher-bearer as all the rest of our drummers were. We had plenty of work to do from that day onward. The work was of a decidedly strenuous and risky kind, owing to the nature of the ground and above all that deadly enemy, the sniper. Our Drum-Major was our first casualty; shot through the stomach, he succumbed shortly after. • "We were getting nicely settled down when we had been a month on the Peninsula, when we had orders to pack up. It transpired that we were to make a new landing at Sulva Bay. We reached our destination about midnight, and under cover of darkness found a temporary resting place in a sort of horseshoe-shaped gully, facing the sea. My three mates and my- self tossed up: Who should sleep on the stretcher, and of course I lost. Soldier-crammed Gully.

"We were up early next morning, and we could see that the gully was literally crammed with soldiers. I thought what a good target it would be for the Turks if they got to know—as after- wards they did with the aid of one of their aeroplanes. They shelled us all day from all directions, and we stretcher-bearers had a glorious time of it; I don't think. Two of my mates got hit while tending to the wounded, and two of us had to do the best we could.

"At midnight we svere ordered to leave and follow in the wake of a Colonial division. We halted at dawn and snatched a few minutes' rest, then started offeagain. All the way one could see the dead scattered about.

There must be -someone praying very hard for my welfare at home, that I should come through such daily dangers without a scratch."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
Only Stretcher-Bearer Left.

"As we were advancing our men kept get- ting hit here and there, and I was always busy running about. At last my mate, who was carrying the stretcher with me, got hit in the wrist by a sniper. I bandaged him up and then went off with the stretcher myself. At last we halted in a ridge which was captured from the enemy the day before, and the Com- pany-Sergeant-Major sent three men out of the company to me in place of my three mates who had been hit.

"That night I felt terribly ill and weak after the work of the past few days, and the doctor told me that I was suffering from dysentery. So he gave me a red ticket and sent me down to the beach, where I was eventually taken to the hospital ship which sailed for Alexandria the next day, and from there I went to Cairo, where I now am. There are some wonderful sights round here. There is Joseph's Well close to a very ancient mosque. and also the well-known Spinx and Pyramids, and numerous other things." PROUD WARRINGTONIAN.

" It Is A Great Honour To Be Out" In France.

Pte. J. Ridgeway, R.F.A., whose parents live at 30, Wellington-street, and who is attached to a brigade ammunition column in France, writing home recently, says :—" I note that you went to the Park the other Sunday and that you saw many hundreds there. I wonder how many medically fit young men there were amongst them, who, yet, are frightened to come forward. • • • • I am very glad that I did enlist, for it is a big honour to be out here. When I get home I shall do a bit of choking off' if I see any likely lads walking about. . . . We caught it hot last Sunday night. A Tea-table Surprise.

"We were all sat down to our tea, and after. we had been at• the table about ten minutes siP or seven shells dropped over into our camp and took the edge off our appetites. Talk about fun!, You should have seen the way we fell flat on the floor. Fortunately there ,wag' no damage done. . . . We could not 'see the shells coming, but we could hear them whistling through the air." -

Private Ridgeway, prior to "joining up,' worked at Messrs. Hutchins' Tannery, Howley.

AMATEUR SCULPTORS.

Life-likeness Of Dickens In Trench Chalk.

Life in the trenches, writes an Orford soldier in the Orford Parish , Magazine, is very monotonous, and is relieved by some of the tioldiers cutting statues of men and animals out. of the chalk through which we have to dig, as we are in a great chalk field. I saw a statue of. Charles Dickens and it was a perfect life- likeness of him; also a carving in chalk, diamond shape, with the likeness of the 'Virgin Mary in the centre, and on the bevelled :sides these words,." Fear God, Honour the Xing." It brings one back to civil life again to think there are other things besides war. We were working in the trenches occupied by some of the Gordon

-

Highlanders the other night, and the corporal of the platoon, after seeing his men to their respective posts, took out his Bible and began to read, and was still doing so when I had occasion to pass about an hour or so later. Things are very quiet at this part of the line, but I am afraid it is the calm before the storm.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
BACK TO FRANCE.

Rev. T. T. James Makes A Personal Statement.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR-TIME HARVEST.

On Wednesday evening the Rev. T. T. James, pastor of the Wycliffe Congregational Church, left Warrington on a journey to France. There, by special permission of his congregation, he is serving with the Y.M.C.A. among the troops until after Christmas. It will be remembered that already he has served for three months on similar work, going away early in June. Addressing a large harvest thanksgiving assenibly at the Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening, Mr. James said : Appeal to His Church.

"I feel that I should like to say a word or two by way of personal statement at this ser- vice to-night. As most of you are aware, I am hoping to be back in France again this week. Very heartily I want to thank the officers and members of this church who have made that possible. These are trying and difficult days for all . our . churches, and inconvenient in many ways it is bound to be for you to have your minister away. That you will do everything that ldte and loyalty, regularity in attendance and faithfulness in Christian work can achieve to minimise that inconvenience I feel well assured. That God. will give you days of bless- ing, and that amidst all the strenuous anxieties of these critical times you may be able to give a faithful witness to the reality of His grace, will be my earnest prayer. And as you know that we are praying for you out there, I know you will pray here at home that in the midst of con- flict many alad may discover the secret of God's love. These have been happy days that I have had with you, happy days in this clutch, and happy days at home; and I thank you all very heartily for the real welcome you have given me. We must look forward yet to the best days of all.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
The Great Reaper.

"This day has been our harvest festival. That it can have come to us this year with no sense of overhanging clouds it seems impossible to imagine. We cannot meet here without remem- bering those for whom this harvest time is shadowed and darkened by bitter calamity. Not only is the burden of anxiety and bereavement heavy in our own land, but there are countries where ruined homesteads and shattered cities stand amidst the once smiling fields. There is a bitter harvest being reaped in blood; there is a reaper—death—whose sheaves are made of the broken lives of men. We shall pray with all our hearts that before another year has passed, and before another golden summer has changed to autumn, the last sword will be sheathed, and the last gun be silent on the battlefields of Europe, and that God will have brought us out of this nightmare and tragedy of strife. Give to us peace in our time, 0 Lord.'"

SOUTH LANCS.' CATCHWORD.

"The Job's A Good 'Cu," Says A Regimental Poet.

Sergeant Hasler, of the Bth South Lancashire Regiment, 27, Napier-street—promoted sergeant since last he sent some verses to "The Examiner "—has forwarded us the following lines, written by Private T. C. Currie, of the same battalion :

"THE JOB'S A GOOD 'UN."

Have you ever taken notice how a catchword or

a song, Can become the only phrase that folks will say? Take for instance "Tipperary,' well, 'twas just a catchy strain, Yet it's sung in many languages to-day. Now we've got a little catchword in the South

Lancs. all our own, It expresses our opinion in this way, If you've got a good thing coming, in the Lancs. you'll always hear .rust three words. In fact you've only got to say "THE JOB'S A GOOD 'UN."

It was invented in the Depot when the war had just begun, It has stuck to us until the present day. 'Twas invented when from dinner you did " through the window climb, And the Corporal he sped you on your way, When he asked you if you wanted Duff, and you said "Yes, I do," He would give you Duff, and then you'd come away; He kept shouting out "Now roll up there; there's plenty here for all " : And the Rookies would chime in with him and say __

===== 0002601_19151016_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE JOB'S A GOOD 'UN."

Well, we took the phrase to —mouth, and we woke that city up When our band along its streets began to play. All the girls there started smiling, and there's thousands of them there, And they welcomed us in such a hearty way. We were soldiers down from Lancashire to spend a winter there. There was many a townsman there, both old and grey. Who said," If I were a young chap, well, it's in that Corps I'd list. That's the Corps who always seem these words to say "THE JOB'S A GOOD 'ITN."

It's a phrase that's going to Berlin, though we may not take it there, Yet we'll help to send it best part of the way, It's a phrase the Potsdam Devil yet will hear, but won't enjoy, When our guns smash up his dreams of world- wide sway, When our Kitchener has given Billy what he needs so much, And has sent him to his ,knees for peace to pray, Why, our Wives and Kids and Sweethearts, who wait anxiously behind, Will tlrftnk God upon their knees and then they'll say "THE JOB'S A GOOD 'UN."

When they're dishing out the Honours we will get a decent share, We'll have Mons and Neuve Chapelle to start away; We'll have quite a lot of others to augment our present list, And the present list's a long one by the way. We expect the King will tell us "South Lanes. I am proud of you; "You have borne your Flag through many a heavy day. "If the, boys who grow to manhood do their part as well as you, "Britannia can whisper, come what may, "THE JOB'S A GOOD 'UN."

Sympathy.

A large touring automobile containing a man and his wife met a load of hay in a very narrow road. The woman declared that the farmer must back out, but her husband contended that she was unreasonable.

"But you can't back the automobile so far," she said, "and I don't intend to move for any- body. Besides, he should have seen us." The husband pointed out that this was ira- possible, owing to an abrupt turn in the road. "I don't care," she insisted. "I won't move if I have to stay here all night." Her husband was starting to argue the mat- ter, when the farmer, who had been sitting quietly on the hay, internosed : "Never mind, sir!" he exclaimed, with a sigh. "I'll try to back out. I've got one just like. her at home."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
SHORTAGE OF MEN.

Should Women Distribute Themselves Over Empire?

. Some of the problems in eugenics that will arise after the war were dealt with by Dr. Saleeby in the course of a lecture in London on Saturday night. We had in this country, he remarked, a great excess of women, and that excess was being accentuated by the war, and yet some people proposed that after the war the soldiers should be sent to the colonies, as there would not be enough employment here. And in the colonies there was a dearth of women. They could do nothing more absurd than send away millions of men from this country, where there was already such an enormous relative excess of females. That excess created an urgent problem before the war, and it was made more urgent every day the war lasted.

Obviously, the course to pursue was to try to get the whole of the Imperial population dis- tributed with a numerical equality.of the sexes. The alternative would be to flatter the Turk and adopt polygamy as a better expedient than Regent-street day and night exemplified. But believing, as he did, in the validity of the mono- gamistic principle, and that all parts of the Empire were important for the Empire and for its ideals, the modification of our inter-Imperial emigration, with the object of getting a numerical equality of the sexes. ought to be fundamental of our Imperial policy. To make the disproportion worse in the way suggested would be madness.

Germany was ruining her race now. We had lost 80,000, but she had probably lost not less than six times that number, and we knew that she was using consumptives, asthmatics, and men with false hands, and was running through her healthy manhood.

PALATiNE ARTILLERY.

Fund For Their Supply With

Comforts.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
APPEAL AND LEAD, FROM

LORD DERBY.

In the terms of the following lette.r, to which we are asked to 'give publicity, Lord Derby makes an appeal for the raising of a fund which has for its object the provisioning of the County Palatine Artillery, which has been raised ex- clusively in Lancashire, with comforts when they go to the Front, as they are shortly to do. The administration of the fund will be in the hands of Mr. H. P. P. Lane, the Chief Con- stable of the 'County, who expresSed the hope, in a Covering communication, that in addition to gifts of money which will make good any deficiency in the supply of articles, local work- ing committees in the various areas may be either formed or, where already in existence, induced to contribute the requisite articles so as to create a constant flow through his hands and enable him to meet all demauds. It is desired to impress upon the generously disposed that the woollen comforts inolude such means of protection against cold and damp as helmets and sleeveless waistcoats, and that such socks as are sent in should be rather loosely, not tightly, knit. Also, that the term '`-cigarettes" covers tobacco in its various forms. The material for the fund will be collected at the County Police Office, Preston, and adminis- tered from there according to the requirements of the two divisions of artillery, and already a number of contributions have been received, in addition to Lord Derby's generous donation.

Mr. Lane wishes it to be understood that this fund will be separate from that started •in con- nection with the Liverpool "Pals," with which it has been associated under a misapprehension. Lord Derby's letter is as follows: " Knowsley, Prescot, Oct. Bth, 1915. " Sir,—The County Palatine Artillery which now forms the Divisional Artillery for the 30th and 32nd Divisions of the new Army will, in all probability, be going abroad within a- very -Short time, ind it is very desirable before tley go that some fund should be raised for supplying 'them with comforts while at the Front. Mr. IL P. P. Lane, M.V.0., the Chief Constable of Lancashire, has been good enough to undertake this work, and at his request I write to ask that all who may be willing to contribute to this fund should communicate with him at the ?County Police Offices, Preston. The avticles chiefly required are socks, gloves, mufflers, and cigarettes, and any of these articles can be sent to him at his address, And will be gratefully acknowledged.- '" In addition, funds, to which I, as the raiser,' have been glad to contribute £5OO, will be needed to buy the above-named articles as may be required. Any who may_wish to con- tribute to this fund are asked to send the money to W. B. Rideal, Esq., manager, Lancashire and Yorkshire Bank, Preston, who has kindly con- sented to act as hon. treasurer.

"The money will be used to make good any, deficiencies that may exist in the number ot any of the above specified articles that may be presented, and also to buy any luxuries to send out from time to time to these two divisions. "Funds for the infantry of the 30th Division, composed of Liverpool and Manchester troops, will be formed separately, the above fund being confined entirely to the two divisions of County Palatine Royal Artillery.—Yours faithfully, "DERBY."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
Transferred To Post Of Registrar For

Sale District.

Mr. Harold T. Mercer, .qf Belmont, Station- road, Lymm, has been transferred to the regis- trarship of Sale and district. On Wednesday afternoon, at a meetinc, of the Bucklow Board of Guardians, the resolution was passed; and Mr. Mercer will not leave Lymm until a suc- cessor has been appointed. _

To Lymm Mr. Mercer came twelve and a half years ago from the office of Mr. T. J. Ridgway, Warrington; and during the whole of that time has held the post of registrar and school attendance officer for the district. • •

Apart from official work he has during the past year, acted as secretary to the Belgian Relief Committee, in Charge Of the "Beech- weed" Home.

kiED CROSS EXTENSION.

New Accommodation Provided At The

"Brookfield" Hospital.

"Brookfield," the Lymm Red Cross Hospital, has been unexpectedly extended by eight beds. These include a ward instituted in the newly- equipped conservatory to the left of the build- ing. Under the care of Sister Colburn and her staff there are now in the hospital 29 wounded Tommies," of whom some occupy the new portion. It need hardly be pointed out that subscrip- tions are now needed more urgently than ever. Altogether the institution will now accommo- date 32 patients.

ROYAL ENGINEERS.

Private Henry Cadman, of Booth's Hill— serving with the mining section Royal Engineers—writes from' the Gartshore -Auxiliary Hospital, liirkintilloch (where he is ill) to his sister at Brookfield Cottages, to say he will probably arrive home to-day for a stay of about a week.

After his -visit home to Booth's Hill, Private Percy Clare arrived safely back at the Front on Saturday morning. On Wednesday morning his cousin, Private Joseph Clare, reached home on *similar 'leave. The experiences, he relates are much similar to those of his usin reported by us last week.

BAPTISTS' HARVEST FESTIVAL.

Rev. E. E. Lovell Introduces A Novel Idea For Children.

The HigheNane Baptist Church harvest thanksgiving services were held on Sunday, and the Pastor (the Rev. Edward E. Lovell) was the preacher for the day. ,

- Following his usu.al custom of devoting a small part of the morning service to the young people, the pastor's address on Sunday was particularly appropriate. He called it "A Basket of Summer Fruit," and his remarks were rendered the more effective as he demonstrated them by a basket full of various kinds of fruit. The text in the morning was Ecclesiastes xi. 6, and in the evening Matthew xx. 28.

At each service Miss Mary Brundrett sang. In the morning the solo was The Prayer "; in the evening, "How Amiable are Thy Dwellings" and " The King of Love." Her renderings of these were much appreciated. The officers of the church had invited girts of fruit, vegetables and flowers, and sincerely, thank all for so generous a response. All the fruit and vegetables received were sent to the Lymm Auxiliary Red Cross Hospital. The decorations were carried out by various members of the church and congregation, in- cluding Mrs. Lovell and Miss Daintree. Throughout the day there were good congrega- tions, and the collections were in aid of the church funds.

THE ADVANCE.

PRIN ITE J. FRED PERCIVL.

Wounded Lvmm-Nathe Describes

2nd Cheshires' Charge.

Piivate James Frederick Percival, of the 2nd Cheshires—who, as we reported last week, has been wounded in France in the right forefinger —writes to his wife and two little boys at Canal Bank, Grappenhall :—" I am at present in good health. I am in a convalescent hospital [Stationary Hospital No. 4, France] for a week or two. . . My finger is going on very nicely. I keep coming nearer home, hoping to be at home altogether soon. • It ls a fine place where I am now, there are hundreds of sick and wounded here. lam sorry to keep putting you to trouble to write to different places, but I am settled now for a time. . . ."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
" Tumbling I n All Directions."

On October Bth, Private Percival writes :—" am myself all right again for the trenches. It was awful, the charge I was in. We wtre tumbling over in all directions, the trenches being full of dead. I was lucky to get out as I did; it was Simply murder. I shall never for- get the 3rd of October. How our Sam came out of it unhurt I do not know; but he was all right when I came away, and all was quiet. Jack Russell got wounded in the head in the same battle. I was in the charge at "I have had no letters lately, but I suppose Sam will have got them for me, or else they will be in the company. So I think I will get them when I get back again. ' Smack ' Booth sends his !Jest respects to all at Lymm; it is not true about him being killed."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
LOADING HIS RIFLE.

Private Shm Percival Tells How His Brother Was Wounded.

Mrs. J. F. Percival has also heard from her brother-in-law, Private Sam Percival, of New- fields, Lymm—also in France with the 2nd Cheshires. Explaining how her husband's casualty took place, he says:— " He was loading his rifle when a shot came, and went through the first finger on his right hand. I don't think that it is serious. . . . I got burned twice myself, but thank God I came out quite safe. I think it was as hot a place as 1 have ever been .in; but still we are here to do our duty—that is what we joined the Army for. Fred Maddocks sends his best respects to you all."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
SCOTS GUARDS' PART.

Corpl. Ben Elliott Tells How Reserves

Rushed To Fore-FronL

In a letter dated October 3rd to his parents at Canterbury Cottage, Lymm, Corporal Ben Elliott, of the 2nd Scots Guards, says:— "I am still in the pink- . . Since I last wrote' you we have been in action, and I am pleased to say I have again come out safe ex- cept for the gas. lam all right; I got slightly gassed in the attack, but I overcame that. A Lovely Sight

"This is Sunday, and we are going into action again; I only hope I have the same luck. But we did very well. It was a lovely sight to see us advancing under heavy shell fire as cool as you like. We have got used, to them now, but it is terrible, I wish it was over: .. . Well, mother, I am just going to have bully beef and biscuits instead of roast beef and Yorkshire pudding." On the same day, writing to his sister at Altrincham, the Corporal says: "I expect you have read in the papers about the big advance. Well, we came out of action the night before last, after being in over a week; and we are just going into action again. We were a week with nothing scarcely to eat or drink. But that is nobody's fault; they could not get it up to us where we were. We took the village of and all the mines about it, and Hill 70. It had been taken three times previously and lost, but the Scots Guards division let them know who were there. Everybody said it was a glorious sight to see us advancing, and you should have seen the Germans run when we got up to them. I can tell you it was a sight I shall never forget, but it has to be done. Sir John French Congratulates.

"We had a visit from Sir John French, and he said he was proud of the 2nd Battalion Scots Guards. We were' supposed to be in reserve, but we went on running and passed all the others, so they brought us away for two days,' for something to eat—and we wanted it. As luck happened, I received a parcel the night we got into billets. However, this is Sunday, and I am just going to have my dinner ; then we fall in with about 801 b. on our backs and about 10 miles to walk.

"We were gassed during the advance. I got slightly touched, but I overcame that."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM TERRITORIALS.

LEAVE VILLAGE AND AGAIN ENTER THE TRENCHES.

The group of local members of the sth Cheshires now at the Front are understood to have left the village and wood which, up to a month ago they were guarding, and to have once again entered the trenches. To his mother at 1, West Hyde, Private Henry Brookes writes:—" I am glad to say that I am keeping well—wonderfully well considering the wretched weather we have had lately. lam writing this letter by the light of a candle, with- in a stone's throw of the firing line. I don't suppose they, have the least thought that we are billeted In such battered ruins as these places, or they would soon have us out of it with d ' Johnson ' or two. You would hardly credit that we could make ourselves so much at home, being so. near the firing line. A Homely Scene.

"I must tell you. that Corporal Jack Moston and I have just finished our tea. We have had a little toast and cooked cheese—which is a meal much envied by Many out here, as it is not often the billets Are so convenient, and we thought it would be, nice for a change. We can afford to keep a cat, and while I am writing this letter she is sat on the hearth, and seems quite contented, . even with us British Tommies.' Many valuable pictures still hang on the wal4l, and also pieces of costly furniture are in the place. But, of 'course, this is only one of many places round here which have been evacuated to make room for the warriors.

"I don't know what the people in England would think or do if they had bullets flying in hundreds through their windows and back- yards as they are out here, and have been even when the place was inhabited by civilians. Fiom what remains of this ruined place you can see that ,it had once been the happy home of the village blacksmith, as he has had to leave everything behind him in the way of tools and,other implements. • "The church has been knocked down by shells; but we hold services in an old chapel which still remains intact.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
Six or Seven.

" I was saying in illy last letter' about the number of Lymm lads in the Platoon. . Well, we have only about six, or seven actually in the platoon at the present time, as some have been transferred to other kinds of work, and our late Platoon Sergeant (11, Clare) is now Com- pany-Sergeant-Major. It will soon be eight months since we came out on this business; and many wild travels we have had—and I suppose we shall have to do many more before we are entrenched on the banks of the Rhine. I wouldn't mind spending Christmas Day there, but, of course, much prefei spending it at home. • . . I enclose a ring for my father, which I should have sent in one of my earlier letters, but somehow I mislaid, it. It was one that Jim Gilberts gave me, that Teddy {Gil- berts] was wearing when he got killed.;. and he gave it me as a keepsake. I put .ray initials on.

it; it is made from the nosecap (aluminium) of a German shell."

NEV VICAR INDUCTED.

Rev. And Hon. C. F. Cross On The

People's Guarantee.

To the Vicarage of All Saints', Thelwall, vacant in consequence of the resignation of the Rev. L. H. Tiarks, the Bishop of Chester has instituted the Rev. Charles Mallam Johnston. On Monday evening, in the presence of a large assembly the new vicar was inducted by the Rural Dean (the Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross, vicar of Stretton). A shortened form of evensong was conducted by the Rev. W. J. Stanton, and after the Third Collect, the hymn "Come, Holy Ghost," was sung.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
Wise Words.

Having read the Mandate of Induction, the Rural Dean said the chief feature of a service was the touching of some part of the church— in this case the key. The outward sign of the touch was a pledge of contact :and union be- tween those who worshipped in that church. They were like wheels in a 'great machine; each wheel touched; its neighbour—so in paro- chial matters they lived in touch with each other and each must give of his or her best. They must do their best, to assist their new Vicar; there were many ways if they were in earnest. The outward sign of the contact by hand was a symbol of the touch of faith; there was none like humble. faith in God! He hoped that service would be the beginning of a very happy Chapter in the history of the parish, and took their presence there to be a guarantee that they ,would do their part to help the new minister.

The clergy wardens—Messrs. Jones Parry and Edwin Hewitt—and choir proceeded to the west door, where the Rural Dean placed the Vicar's hand on the key, and tolled the bell. The procession returned to the chancel singing "Guide me, 0 Thou great Jehovah." During the collection the hymn " Now thank we all our God," was sung. As the congrega- tion dispersed the organist played " Now are we ambassadors" (from Mendelssohn's "St. Paul").

===== 0002601_19151016_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
New Vicar's Career.

By the Bishop of Salisbury having been ordained deacon in 1606 and priest in 1907, the Rev. Charles Mallam Johnston was, from 1906 to 1912, curate of St. Martin's, Salisbury; since when he has held the post of assistant curate of Uppingham.

KITCHENER THE MAN.

No Stopping The " Tommies " Now They Are Equipped.

Mr. James Whitley, Post Office, Stockton Heath, who has two sons and two grandsons serving the King, has just received a letter from his son James, who is in Flanders. - "Right glad I am," he writes, "to hear all is well. I am in fine health and, as you say. we are very busy now. Kitchener's order was the best order out. Plenty of everything to work on and work with. • The. right man in the right place, as we chaps know quite well here. If. our "fommies ' get the stuff to work with they will go, .and there will be no stopping them. . . As regards coming home, I might come any minute; I shall get leave some time if all goes well. I leave everything to. God: He knows best,. and He will carry us through. . _ _ _ _ _

.. . . Tell little Annie her dad will be home bye and bye. .. . Cheer up and he bright through it all. Everything will work well."

PENKETH.'

Recruits For The Army.—At last the military spirit has broken out afresh in our township, for no fewer than eight worthy members of the community have given themselves up to King and country.

Their chief ambition at the present moment is to give the Huns such a nasty jar that will make them lose their individuality for some time to come. The names of our new defenders (who have given such a clear lead to the many eligibles left behind) are :—Thomas Edward Carrington, Grenadier Guards; John Alfred Pusill, Albert Goulden, Alfred Hughes, Lewis Sparks, King's Royal Rifles; Ernest Glover, Edgar .Molyneux and John Davies, Royal Garrison Artillery.

Message From The Vicar.

A sale of work in connection with Christ Church was held in the Church Schools, Pad- gate, on Thursday, the proceeds of which are to be given to the Church Repair Fund and the reduction of the Woolston Loan. The opening ceremony was performed by Mrs. Rylands, Thelwall, while the Rev. J. Hirst presided. The latter, in a few short opening remarks, ex- plained the objects of the sale of work, and said what a great pleasure it was to him to see so many earnest workers present.

In declaring the sale open, Mrs. Rylands spoke of the pleasure it gave her to be present. and hoped the sale would be a big success. Mr. Percy Royston proposed and Mr. George Mather seconded a hearty vote of thanks to Mrs. Rylands. Mrs. Rylands suitably responded. A vote of thanks was proposed to the organisers of the sale by Mr. Gorton, seconded by Mr. Blackwell. Miss Phylis Jump then presented to Mr.; Rylands a bouquet of roses. A telegram arrived during the afternoon from the Vicar, tile 11,?v. H. F. S. Collier, expressing his good wishes tor the success of the sale. - The proceeds reaChed the handsome total of £125.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR NATIONAL FOOD.

Pudding Lady And The Building Of An Imperial Race.

The forthcoming visit to Warrington of :Tins§ Petty—the Pudding Lady—is arousing a good deal of anticipation. She works under the auspices of the National Food Reform .Associa- tion—a society which arose shortly after the startling report on physical degeneration. During the ten years of its existence it has grown steadily in public favour, largely because of its singularly useful constructive work. First it called together a conference on diet in colleges, public schools, hospitals and other large insti- tutions. This was so successful that another followed for secondary and, private schools. Schoolmasters and matrons refer to the reports of these proceedings when they are bent upon putting school diets upon a better rooting. It is now recognised that the ill-effects of the school " tuck-shop " can best be countered by diet re- form in the schools themselves. Then it was seen that the feeding of the primary school must be tackled, and a third con- ference proved exceedingly useful. _ _

Besides this, the diet Of nurses in our hospi- tals received practical consideration, for this subject, despite its importance; takes a very sub- sidiary place in ordinary hospital Administra- tion.

Since the war began the Food Reform Associa- tion has enormously increased its work owing to fresh applications from those who feel the need of economy. The sale of booklets has increased. especially a little penny One with recipes entitled "Economical Dishes for Workers." Among other publications are some on dietetic cure for inebriety, feeding of young children, diet in pregnancy, and feeding of nurses: Then 'there is a staff of lecturers who go all over the country, led by ,Miss Petty, who ,has carried out dietetic experiments with excellent physical results that can be measured in scales. She can point with justifiable pride to school children whose weight has perceptibly increased above the average as a result of bringing dietetic science into practical operation. But her lec- tures are not scientific disquisitions. She is practical to the finger tips. The National Food Reform Association counts among its supporters Professor Sims Woodhead, Dr. Chalmers Watson, the Duchess of Portland. Mr. Seebohm Rountree, Professor Howard Marsh, Right Hon. Sir Horace Plunkett, Mrs. Despard. Lady Paget, Sir Lander Brunton, and many others, who together form a very repre- sentative set of people. At a time when certain popular foods have seriously adtanced in price, it is well to have an association which will educate people in the direction of broadenpig the basis of our national food supplies.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE WORLD WAR."

Rev. P. meson On Good That Will Come Out Of Great Crime.

The Rev. Percy meson, the neli minister at the Bold-street Wesleyan Church, preached, on Sunday evening, the first of a short series of sermons on "The World War," the special subject of the first of the series being. "-The God of Battles." Christianity And War Not Irreconcilable. In the course of his remarks, which were listened to by a large congregation, the reverend gentleman referred to the so-called argument of the so-called plain man that, be- cause Christianity and war were irreconcilable, the present state of affairs proved that Chris- tianity had failed. Rather was it not the fact that it was because Christianity had not been given a fair chance that civilisation found itself in chaos to-day? Had the warring nations been truly imbued with the Christian spirit, was it not true that war would be impossible? It might be that the churches had failed in not being true in the past to the spirit of their Master, but, certainly, Christianity had not failed.

The war was not the will of God, 'nor had He willed all the agony and suffering that was following in its train. God was infinitely mersiful, and althougt man, in his blundering way, had brought the present continent-shaking disaster on himself, He would not forsake man. His (the preacher's) own opinion was that because the best heart of this nation had gone to war for the maintaining of righteous prin- ciples, they could unfalteringly claim that God was with them—that God was the God of battle, in that He mustereth His hosts for battle, that the great spiritual hosts were with them, and that the sacrifices they were called upon to make would purify them of all the evil that was too truly in their national life. Remember, said the preacher in closing, that if the Lord of Hosts mustereth His hosts for battle, He will make the very devils to serve him. Somehow, He will make this evil bring forth good.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
SILVER-STREET SCHOOL.

The Staff Retained On Transfer To Council.

In view of the transfer of the Silver-street School to the Local Education Authority from the Ist September, 1915, it was resolved at the last meeting of the School Management Com- mittee :—" That the services of the members of the staff of the Silver-street School be continued as from Ist September, 1915, upon the usual con- ditions for teachers employed in Council Schools, as follows, viz. :—the engagement in each case to be subject to the conditions set forth in the committee's scale of salaries and the regulations governing such appointments attached thereto, together with the conditions of employment contained in the Board of Educa- tion's Code of Regulations for publie elementary schools in force for the time being:"

WAR PROBLEMS.

Mr. Percy Alden, M.P., At

Warrington.

N ENLIGHTENED DEMOCRACY

(ii it BEST SAFEGUARD.

On Saturday the Lancashire and. Cheshire Union of. Adult Schools held their half-yearly meeting at the Friends' Meeting House. There was a good attendance of delegates, who devoted the afternoon to a business session and the evening to a discussion of problems of war.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR COMMON RESPONSIBILITY.

Mr. Percy Alden, M.P. for TottenNam, was the principal speaker at the evening meeting, and devoted his address to a discussion of democracy and our foreign policy. The cause of the war was the want of understanding among the nations, and for this, said Mr. Alden, we must all take our share of responsibility. The. Eng- lish people mast bear Lunch blame; for few spoke the languages of other nations or took any close interest in their affairs. If we under- stood each other we should not fight each other, but would recognise that all are needed to com- plete the universal well-being.

The trouble was that the people had not taken their destinies into their own hands. The German people had allowed themselves to be swayed by a few .men. They did not know the facts of the crisis of last year. They believed that they were attacked, and that they must fight a war of defence. They believed so still, for the Press was in the hands of the rulers of the nation. The multitudes were sacrificed to carry out the dictates of. half a dozen men. The democracies had never had a fair chance. The people as a whole had no real knowledge of what was being done. Even in England things tend to get into the hands of those who believe in war. Yet the average man did not believe in war and did not want it. It would be essential that the democracies as a whole should apply themselves to the solution of the problem. He confessed that he himself had failed until

recently to realise the importance of the mat- ter. He had devoted his thoughts and strength to social reform. But if all their wealth was to be spent on war how could anything ever be spared for social reform. at was vital to them all that some Cure should be found for war. Things would be bad at the close of the preseht strife, hut if the future held out no hope of permanent peace; if all they could save was to be spent on armaments, then the future would be worse than any past had been.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
TRUST IN WORKING MEN.

It was important that there should be wide- spread interest, and that their Statesmen should be given to feel that that interest existed. Sup- pose Sir Edward Grey were asked to enlighten the public to a greater degree he might easily replv "What do the pcople ki) about foreign affairs?" It was perhaps not possible for them to grasp all the intricacies of such a subject as the Balkan problem, but at . least they could have certain fixed principles in their ni!,mls. This added to the inherent love of fairness and the common sense of the working classes would carry Us a long way. .He would rather trust the average working man than any Statesman he ever knew, for deep in his 'heart-Was a feel- ing of brotherhood. The average man believed that we ought to be able to do our business With other nations without resort to arms. It must be remembered that many in high places did not want to think that way. The armament firms did not. They were vending money to make war, and bribing journalists to create war scares. ,

===== 0002601_19151016_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
AFTER THE WAR.

Mr. Alden next discussed Mr. Asquith's state- ments that the object of the war was to destroy militarism, to secure full liberty for small nationalities; and toThring about a more settled state of affairs in Europe. As to what should happen afterwards, two main theories had been formulated. The first, proposed by Professor Leonard Hobhouse, was the formation of a League of Peace from which Germany would be excluded. After hr crimes of the past year, Germany was not fit to mix in civilised society until her people had altered her policy or her government. There were already signs that the best element in Germany was beginning to re- assert itself. Admiral Von Tirpitz was in dis- grace, and part of the German naval policy seemed to have been discarded. In some of the better class German journals there was a more friendly feeling—a discouragement of the policy of hate towards Britain. - - -

The second programme which had been advanced—that of J. H. Hobson—was that after the war a League of all the Nations with a Court of" Arbitration should be formed. It was pro- posed that a first court should be created, whose functions should be Jjusticial, and which would deal with matters affected by international law. A second court was proposed which should represent the people of every nation. To this would come all. the questions that lie behind war. These would be discussed and receive publicity, and the entire world would be able to give a verdict. It was proposed that any State that was inclined to kick over the traces should be brought to a sense of its responsibilities by an economic boycott by the other countries. Economic pressure was the most powerful force that could he used. It Was doubtful if any nation that came under the boycott of all or -the majority of the other European countries could remain solvent for. three months. Not- withstanding the darkness of the present hour he had hope for civilisation because deep men's hearts there was a belief in brotherhood, and they would yq,t give effect to that telief.

Mr. Alden's a-ddre-ss was followed by a use- ful discussion. It was urged that a practical way in which the Adult School movement would further the ideal of an educated democracy was by the formation of study circles, in which in- ternational problems could be systematically studied.

It was stated that study circles had already been at work in Warrington, with beneficial results, and that a winter circle was being formed, having Gooch's "History Of Our Times" as the textbook.

As an illustration of the sense of importance possessed by some people, Mr. Percy Alden told a good story in the course of his address. A man appeared at a London recruiting station one evening intending to enlist. But it was closing time and the staff had gone. The would-be recruit offered himself to the one remaining assistant. He was told that the office could not be reopened and that he must come on the morrow. "So you won't open the shop again?" he said. He was told that they could not bring back the staff and reopen the books for him. "All right," he said savagely, "You can lose your bloomin' war."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN NURSE FUND.

At the annual meeting of the Runcorn Nurse Fund the Aatement of accounts, &c., showed a balance on the wrong side of £5l. The fresh air and beef tea funds each showed balances on the right side. It was decided to withdraw the £3OO they had invested in the Runcorn Urban Council,nd to reinvest in War Loan £2OO, the other 000 to tide them over the year and provide them .with a balance. •

KILLED BY TREE BOUGH.

Ex-Head .Constable Dunlop, of the Irish Con- stabulary, was cutting a bough off a large tree in his garden at Drumada, Armagh, a few days since, when the bough, weighing over a ton, broke and pinned him against the trunk, killing him. .His child told her mother that her father was asleep up in the tree, n nrl Mrs. Dunlop, going out, found her husband dead.

WOUNDED SOLDIERS AND MOTOR DRIVES.

Sir,—We have in our midst a large number of wounded soldiers, many of whom are unable to walk for any-, great distance, and to whom motor rides are very beneficial. These men are worthy of our sympathy, and entitled to the best we can do for them. -

We- have in Warrington a number of motorists who have been taking these wounded soldiers for afternoon rides. Now winter is coming on, and the men are expected back in hospital at six o'clock, so that to take them out from, say, two o'clock to six o'clock, is too long to he motor- in„,c, all the time. I wish to take this opportunity, through the medium of your paper, to ask our country friends within a radius of about 15 miles if they would be prepared to provide a plain tea for one motor ear (four or five persons) or more. Tea would have to be ready for bout four o'clock, and the soldiers would be able to stay about an hour, according to the distance to be covered on the return journey to the 'hospital. I have already obtained the promise of four or five motor cars to assist in taking the soldiers out, and would like to have offers to entertain them, or suggestions for a more suitable method of giving them a few hours' (out of hospital) enjoyment.

THOS. DOMVILLE Summer Hill, Walton-road, Warrington, October 6, 1915.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SIGN OF THE RED CROSS.

New Flag Unfurled At Raddon Court Military Hospital,

There was an interesting ceremony at the Raddon Court Military Hospital on Tuesday afternoon, when Colonel Moggridge unfurled, in the presence of a large comPany, the new flag which henceforth will float from a mast in the grounds of the hospital, and which will denote that the institution is now recognised by the Government as being a military hospital. The institution was opened under the auspices of the British Red Cross Society en October 19th, 1914, as a hospital for the reception of wounded soldiers, twenty-five wounded Belgian warriors being received at the inception. Later on British soldiers were sent, and altogether, up to last week-end, 285 war-broken men have been succoured. At the beginning of the present week the number of inmate. 3 was 48. The hos- pital was organised by the Daresbury Division of the British Red Cross Society--in which division Warrington is included, and of which division Miss Margaret Broadbent is the com- mandant and Miss Sylvia Broadbent the vice- president. Rain fell. heavily during the afternoon, .and the musical programme which was to have been gone through by the band of the 2nd South Lancs, in the grounds was, instead, brought off in the entrance hall to the spacious residence-. turned-hospital. The unfurling ceremony was witnessed by a !large company of ladies and gentlemen, which included the Mayor (Alder- man Peter Peacock) and Mayoress, the Rev. IL !V. and Mrs. Pigot, Mrs. Broadbent, Alderman !Bennett and Mr. John C2xon.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
CHANGE IN STATUS OF HOSPITAL.

During a lull in the rain ,Colonel Moggridge pulled the cord which unfurled the flag and left it floating in the breeze. Prior to this the Rev. H. V. Pigot read a letter which had been received from the Lady President of the British Red Cross Society (Catharine, Duchess of Westminster), in which Her Grace regretted that she was unable to visit the hospital that day, but hoped to have the pleasure of doing so later on. Colonel Moggridge, addressing the company, said that the ceremony that afternoon was really to denote a change in the status of the hospital. It was not a very great change. It simply meant that the hospital, instead of being —if one might be pardoned the phrase—an amateur establishment, was now recognised by the Government as being a military hospital. There were certain terms, or conditions, which the Government imposed on all hospitals be- fore they were entitled to fly the Red Cross flag, and those terms, or conditions, would now bp binding on Raddon Court, the 7ice-President of The Daresbury Division having signAl a paper to the effect that the hospital wa pre- pared to abide by those regulations. The new flag which flies outside the institu- tion is an Army Red Cross flag and does not, like the flag that flew there up to this week, bear the letters B.R.C.S.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
COL. MOGGRIDGE'S GREAT WORK.

The company returned to the hospital, where a vote of thanks was accorded to the Colonel. The Mayor, in proposing the vote, said the officials of the hospital were to be congratulated upon the excellent arrangements they had made for the ceremony, with the exception of the weather. (Laughter.) It was gratifying to find that, despite the weather, there was such a large attendance, which must be very encouraging to all the officials to continue in, the good work in which *ley were at present engaged. (Applause.) Alderman Bennett, seconding, said the Colonel was a great friend to all of them, and had become quite, a familiar figure in the streets of Warrington. They had got to know and to like him very much, and when he left Warrington the loss would be a personal one to all of them. (Applause.) He was doing a great and 4 good work in two ways—in endeavouring to get men to join the ranks and to help in the great fight for freedom, and also in ministering to the sick who had been engaged in the great fight. That day he was discharging the last-hamed task, and it must be very congenial to him to help in a cause so noble as that which was associated with the Haddon Court Hospital. (Applause.) He hoped they would all do their best to induce men to join the ranks, and that those who were unable to join would show how much they appre- ciated the services of the lads who had made such great sacrifices at the Front by seeing that they received that care and attention they so well deserved. (Applause.) Colonel Moggridge, in reply, said all knew what a valuable work the Red Cress Society had done since the outbreak of the war. He knew a great ,deal about what had been accomplished at Raddon Court Hospital, and the difficulties the ladies had had to encounter. Those difficul- ties had been overcome with a high spirit and a cheerfulness- which had won the admiration of all of them. (Applause.) Thanks were due to those in charge of the Red Cross Society work in Warrington and the district for the time and energy they had given to it, good as it was, and particularly to those in charge of Raddon Court Hospital. (Applause.) , Cheers were given for Miss Sylvia Broadbent and the other lady workers, at the call of the Mayor.
