THE EXAMINER,

SNTITRD.A.Y, OCTOBER 16, 1913.

GERMANY DESPERATE AND UNSCRUPULOUS.

Germany, "dominated by the Allies on the Western front and held in check on the Eastern front, is now seeking on a new front, with the aid of Bulgaria, a success which she can no longer possibly achieve in France and Russia." That is how the Balkan situation ' was summed up in the statement of the French Government, read in the Chamber on ;Tuesday by M. Viviani. The situa4ion de- mands, it is true, the utmost strenuousness, of co-ordinated _effort, from the nations of the Entente. But it is no less obvious that this latest adventure of the Central Powers is a desperate throw inspired by the consciousness of a long series of failures—failure in the drive upon Paris, in the violent attempts to break 'through to Calais, in the piratical campaign against our trade on the high seas, and in the great thrust eastwards which was intended to put the Russian armies hors de combat. As Viviani announced, France and England, in accord with their Allies, are fully agreed to give effective aid to Serbia, and to ensure for the benefit of Serbia, Greece, and Rumania, respect for the Treaty of Bucharest, of which they are guarantors. Russia has expressed her anxiety to join her Allies in sending help- . to Serbia, and her troops will be found fight- ing side by side with ours and those of France. It is, perhaps, natural that the first impres- sion made by the news that the Bulgarian Government had thrown in its lot with Ger- many and Austria was to credit Germany with a great dipleanatic triumph, and to suggest an unfavourable comparison for the diplomacy of the Entente. Germany had this initial advan- tage, that she was hampered by none of thp principles which have moulded the policy of the Entente Powers. They had set them- selves to restore the union of the Balkan peoples, and to establish an equilibrium on the basis of mutual sacrifices and advantages which would satisfy within the limits of equity the national aspirations of the respective Bal- kan States. Germany, who had enjoyed, in approaching Turkey, the advantage afforded by perfect readiness to. condone any atrocities and any misgovernment, was restrained by no considerations of justice in formulatingier offers to Bulgaria. It was easy to outbid the other side, provided King Ferdinand was ready to take German offers at their face value, and agree to the virtual suzerainty of Germany for a trifle of nominal aggrandise- ment. The herzainer who is prerared to offer

anything and everything is not a successful

diplomatist, but a " confidence " trickster.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
BUDGET MODIFICATIONS.

Herbert Samuel, the Postmaster- General, announced in the House of Commons on Tuesday that the proposal to abolish the halfpenny postal rates has been abandoned, as not "likely to secure a continuing source of revenue." The weight of newspapers to be carried for a halfpenny is reduced from 51b. to 60z.; every additional 6oz. entailing a fur- ther charge of a halfpenny. The new scale of Press telegraph rates has been modified. In the text of the Finance Bill, now issued, there are some important modifications of the original Budget proposals. The relief in respect of children under it is now fixed at £25 instead of £2O per child. Wage-earners now brought under the income-tax may, in default of payment, have to submit to deduc- tion (up to 25 per cent.) from their wages until the State's claim is satisfied.

Mr.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS WORSHIP

The customary "additional /meeting" of the 'Town Council will be held as usual on Tuesday evening. War, or no war, the routine business of the Corporation must be transacted, the year wound up, and the wai Made clear for a nevi star:. There will be no municipal elections this year, but the com- mittees close their stewartlA'nip and cowe up for re-appointment. The Mayor makes his bow and is thanked for his services. In the present conditions, the thanks have been well earned. War has darkened both years of Alderman Peacock's term; but it may be said, without tetractin.g from the sex vices rendered by a long list of predecessois, that no one could have filled the chair and acted as the head of the town in the circumstances quite so well as Alderman Peacock has done. Added to his natural gifts for managing is the fact that he has been free to devote almost the whole of his time to his public duties, and he has not spared himself in the presence of an unprecedented multiplicity of those duties. Alderman Bolton will, therefore, have a good theme on which to dilate when on Tuesday he moves:

"That the best thanks of this Council be presented to Mr. Alderman Peter Peacock, for the satisfactory manner in which he has dis- charged the responsible duties devolving upon him as Mayor and Chief Magistrate of the Borough during this, his second year of office, and for the unvarying courtesy and impartiality displayed by him when presiding over the de- liberations of the Council, and in his intercourse with its members."

Fortunately, we are to have the services of His Worship for another year; we hope he will, at the close of the year, hand over his chain to his successor in times of peace.

MASONIC INSTALLATIONS.

Three New Local Worshipful Masters. During the past few days three local W.M.'s have been installed.

"OUR DAY."

Next Week's Great Effort For The Wounded.

The programme of the local committee for assisting the Empire-wide effort on Thursday to raise funds for the British Red Cross and St. John Ambulance Association to aid our wounded at the Front is making satisfactory progress. Tickets for the grand concert to be held at Parr Hall, are going "like hot cakes." Those who have not already done so are advised to lose no time in obtaining .their tickets. The employers of labour and head teachers of schools have been approached with a view to collections being made. A whist drive and dance are being held at Latchford, and the ladies' section of the St. John Ambulance Brigade Are organising a whist drive at the Cairo-street School.. •

These -1661 efforts are all making for a gratifying result, and what is now needed is the unstinted support of the inhabitants. We know Warrington will not lag in giving this.

PENNY CONTRIBUTIONS IN WORKS

SUGGESTED.

The following letter has been addressed to the various firms in town:—

Sir,—The British Red Cross Society and the St. John Ambulance Association are organising an appeal, all over the Empire, in aid of our wounded sailors and soldiers at the Front, on Thursday, October 21st (Trafalgar Day), to commemorate the first anniversary of the amalgamation (for war purposes) of these two Societies. The object is to raise as large a sum of money as possible in honour of the wounded sailors and soldiers, from home and overseas, and for the alleviation of their sufferings at the Front.

An 'opportunity is now given to every man, woman and child throughout the Empire, to help to keep the Red Cross flag flying. On be- half of the local committee I have been asked to suggest that each of your employes should be invited to contribute one penny towards the fund.

In view of the worthiness of the object for which this appeal is being made, may I rely upon your kind co-operation and assistance? Any sum received from your employes or your firm will be gratefully accepted by Yours sincerely, EDITH R. MOCATTA, Township Leader for Warrington. Stanley-place, Warrington, 12th October, 1915.

ELMWOOD SALE.

SOUTH LANCASHIRES'

War-Time Effort For A Worthy Share In The Recent" Exciting Object. Work " At The Front.

At a cost of nearly £2,600, the Elmwood- avenue Congregational Church was built in 1913. Up to three days ago there still remained upon it a debt of about £4OO, and in order to clear this a sale of work has been held. Very kindly, Mr. Ziba Armitage promised that what- ever was raised up to half that amount he would double. Amid a crowded attendance on Wednesday afternoon, the sale was opened by -Mrs. Frank A. Haworth, of Manchester. Mr. J. W. Slack presided, and there were also on the platform the Revs. T. T. James, C. P. Pitt and John Yonge, and Mr. Ziba Armitage. The hymn "0 God, Our Help" having been sung, Mr. Pitt offered prayer. Accompanied by Mr. W. H. Payton, Madame B. Eckersley sweetly sang "There's a Land." Mr. Pitt read a letter of apology from Sir Arthur H. Crosfield, Bart., who. should have taken the chair, but was unfortunately detained in London by urgent and important business. He, however, forwarded a five guinea cheque and consignment of toilet soaps. "I am sure," added Mr. Pitt, "we all regret that Sir Arthur Crosfield is unable to come this afternoon."

The Chairman explained the objects of the sale, remarking that many churches thought it a good thing to be in debt. (Laughter.)

CHURCH ECONOMY.

Mrs. Haworth apologised for her husband's absence through illness, and said he was much disappointed at not being able to come amongst them, "as a son-in-law of Cockhedge.-' (Laughter.) She would like every one connected with the church to go in for economy—of ,course,. preserving their efficiency. Economy was no use unless efficiency was also preserved. • She should not have liked to come that afternoon had they been, not wiping off an Old debt, but incurring a new one. She only hoped the committee had been wise -enough to obtain a lot of useful garments, and then they would be able to buy with a perfectly clear conscience, and know they were doing good. (Applause.) Mr. James thanked the opener and. Chair- man, paying a high tribute to Mr. Slack's fine work and generosity for the new buildings. Mrs. Haworth's wise words it was worth their while to think about. Mr. Z. Armitage, who was heartily received, said in seconding, the Chairman had proved himself a very good friend to Elmwood-avenue, and he was sure they had been very interested in hearing Mrs. Haworth. Mr. Yonge, who supported, was also heartily received. This, he said, was a happy scene with a glorious prospect; he hoped they would gain their end, and more than their end. (Applause.) ' Unanimously the motion was carried, the Chairman replying.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THURSDAY'S CEREMONY.

At Thursday afternoon's opening there was again an excellent attendance. The hymn "Praise My Soul" was rendered. Mr. Joseph Mason (of Fearnhead), occupied the chair, and in a general way referred, as one who was in a measure interested therein, to the work.

Mr. Peter Travis (of Bispham), in opening the sale, gave capital recollections of the early Cockhedge Mission days, when he was a worker there. •

On the motion of Mr. F. Morrall, seconded by Mr. W. H. Wallington, cordial thanks were accorded the Opener and Chairman. Miss L. Wilson accompanying, Miss Hazeldine gave a solo in good style. LAST NIGHT: THE CHILDREN.

Last night the sale was concluded—once more in crowded state. Mr. Pitt having offered prayer, the hymn "All Hail The Power" was joined in; after which the children gave in pretty style a grand opening by means of songs, carols and recitations. The leader was Willie Caldwell.

CASUALTIES 248.

Generals Appreciate Territorial Work In Late Operations.

. Captain S. T. Boast, of the South Lancashire Regiment, writing to Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, Town Clerk of, Warrington, says:— _ •

_ "The cigarettes mentioned in your letter of the 17th September, arrived to-day. The Battalion is again. in the trenches, and as men were rather short of 'smokes,' your gift is going up to them to-night, and I am sure it will Lid fully appreciated. On behalf of all .ranks of the Battalion. I.ask you to convey sincere thanks to all those who so kindly , contributed to the fund for supplying . the cigarettes. It is really very difficult to make people .at home realise how .much these gifts are appreciated, especially when our men know they are from their War- rington friends and admirers. "Since my last letter to you we have had some very exciting work, and _took part in the affair at -- on 25t1i September. !Practically very little ground was gained by the British troops in the area mentioned,. and it was not really expected. The objective was to keep the enemy fully occupied there, and so prevent him withdrawing. Men or guns to assist further south, where, as you know, the French and

British troops made good progress about the same date. These affairs canmit be carried on without casualties. ours on .the occasion men-

tioned being. 8 officers and about 210 other ranks; of the officers, two were killed and one reported missing and believed killed.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
WONDERFUL CHEERFULNESS.

"Of other ranks 25 were killed and about 40 reported missing. All the remainder were wounded. Unfortunately., some were badly wounded, but the majority were slight. •

_ "The names of the casualties will•have been published before this reaches you, so that I need not refer to any particular ones. . "The Battalion was -relieved on the night following the attack, and although the men looked very weary and tired, it is surprising how soon they recover, and after a few hours' rest they were as cheerful as ever. Indeed, it is this wonderful cheerfulness amongst them which keeps them going so well.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
ENJOY THE WARRINGTON SMOKES.

"After a few days' rest the Battalion agair moved to the trenches, net quite the same ones. and there they are now, and to-night they will enjoy the 'Warrington smokes.' lam writing this in rather a hurry. just before ve move off with supplies to the Battalion, i,ut I -.vantel to let you know as soon as possible that the cigarettes had arrived safely. It is getting very cold here now—especially at night and early morning—and there seems no prospect of escaping another winter in the trenches. Still, we shall be better prepared for the winter coming, and we hope to get through it safely, although I per- sonally did not expect to spend a second one under ' war ' conditions. However, lam thank- ful to say my health is very good, even after nearly 15 months of 'roughing it' more or less. "I should like to take this opportunity of sending my regards to my many old friends in Warrington. from whom I often receive mes- sages by men arriving here from time to time, and by men returning off leave. "With sincere good wishes to you, believe me, yours very sincerely,

"SYDNEY T. BOSSY."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
TERRITORIALS BUOYANT.

Praised By Two Generals For

Work Done.

Colonel Fairclough, commanding the War- rington Territorials at the •Front, writing to the Town Clerk, says : " Please thank the generous donors of - the

most welcome consignment of cigarettes, which arrived yesterday (October 9th), for the men of the Battalion. It is indeed kind of people to remember us in this way. "The Battalion is going strong as usual, and has been the recipient during the past few days of telegrams from two different Generals, ex- pressing their appreciation of the work done and assistance rendered since the beginning .of the recent operations. Better Off Than The Germans.

"Unfortunately, 'the summer days are over aml winter days are come;' and the attendant .evil of wet weather is very much upon us; in fact inches of it at times, . mud,. mud, awful mud. However, what is mud for the- goose is equally mud for the gander, so that one possible ray of consola- tion is that our friends, the other side of the barbed wire may perhaps ,be in a ranch worse condition. There is a great deal of satisfaction to be derived from the sight of volumes of water being pumped out of the German trenches, and knowing that one's own trenches drain them- selves, as happened at one Period- when we were in a certain line of trenches. 'There is a silver lining to every cloud,' but when one's vest is wet through one is so very prone at times to forget the bright side of things. Little Rays Of Sunshine From Home.

"However, 'Dam all, and carry on,' is our motto, and it is the little rays of sunshine which occasionally come from home, such as certain boxes containing cigarettes, sweets, cakes and other little luxuries which do certainly help to maintain the excellent spirit which is so notice- able in the Battalion. "Kindly thank all, "Yours sincerely, • • !" BRERETON FAIRCLOUGII."

AID RECRUITING

Colonel Mocatta's Thanks And

Hopes,

•To the Editor of `• The Examiner."

Sir,—Will you allow me throng'," the medium of your newspaper to convey to the many ladies and gentlemen, girls and boys, who have so kindly assisted in the preparation of the National Military Register, my very sincere thanks?

The clerical !art of the scheme is now com- plete, but without the work so willingly per- formed by the numerous voluntary helpers this would not have been accomplished. I trust that I may be equally fortunate in obtaining the services of men over military age to act as recruiting agents, and so make the scheme a success by encouraging eligible men to join. Yours faithfully. D. E. MOCATTA, Lieut-Col., Recruiting Officer, 40th Reetuiting Area, Warrington. The Recruiting Office. Central Police Buildings, Warrington, 14th October, 1915.

Owing to the increased cost of ingredients the Warrington Master Bakers' and Confectioners. Association announce that from Monday next they will be compelled to advance the price of all confectionery, tea-breads, &c.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Insurance Committee on Wednesday evening thee, appointment of Councillor Wilkinson as a member in place of Councillor Joseph—who has resigned—was notified.

Cinderella Club.

This year the Mayor does not propose to call a Meeting at the Town Hall of those who have hitherto given and dressed dolls for the poor children of Warrington, but he will, however, be glad to receive at the Town Hall any dolls or toys that may be sent. Care will be taken that they are distributed in the proper quarters.

Improvements At Market Gate. The centre of old Warrington is rapidly dis- appearing, and new wonders in the shape of modern and palatial business premises are now opening out. The ladies will doubtless be keenly interested in the display of „Messrs. Hammond, whose doors are open for the first time to-day. Mr. J. G. Crail has removed this week from his old shop to his new premises in Bulter- market-street.

A Belgian Anniversary. To celebrate the anniversary of their arrival in Warrington, the Belgians staying in Museum- street write to thank the Mayor and Mayoress, under date October 14th, for the kindness which

has been shown them in England, adding "We shall never forget what has been done for us." The youthful messenger who delivered the message also left a beautiful sheaf of chrysanthe- mums for the Mayoress.

Panel Doctors' Offer.

It was reported to the Warrington Insurance Committee on Wednesday evening that the Medi- cal Benefit Sub-committee had received a letter from the Panel Committee, asking that a deduction of one half-penny per insured person per annum be made fro,m the payments to panel doctors, to meet the expenses of the Panel tommittee. The letter 'was referred to the Finance Sub-committee for consideration, and they passed a resolution, which has been duly endorsed, to the effect that the request of the Panel Committee be not acceded to, as the matter does not come within the province of the Insurance Committee.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Postal Staff Whist Drive.

Great success attended the efforts of the general members of the Warrington Postal Staff, who promoted a whist drive and dance, held in the Crosfield Memorial Hall on Thurs- day evening for the benefit of the local Prisoners of War Fund. About two hundred people attended, and some 25 prizes, given by the members of the postal staff and local tradespeople, were competed for at cards. The audience left in the early hours of Friday morning, after having spent a most enjoyable time. The organisers wish to thank all who in any way assisted to make the event such a success.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Finishing A Negative.

On Tuesday evening the members of the Warrington Photographic Society had the pleasure of seeing one of their own members— Mr. A. Barnett—give a demonstration. His subject was " Finishing a Negative." He showed in a clear and interesting manner how locally to reduce negatives and bring out all detail, and how to work out a background. The negative he worked on before those present was certainly a vast improvement upon what it was before he started. Mr. Davis (the chair- man) expressed the thanks of the members to Mr. Barnett for his Instructive evening.

' Church Society's Anniversary. Last evening in the Parish Church, the War- rington Church Society held their anniversary service. The preacher was the Rev. F. W. Cooper, rector of Prestwich, who gave an eloquent sermon from the words of the Lord's Prayer "Thy kingdom come, on earth as it is in heaven." _

There were, said the preacher, quite a number of good, thoughtful men who believed that we were going backward and not forward in our religion. That was not really the case, however, for the Holy Spirit was working silently and unobserved in our midst. If we wished to re- produce the life of _Christ upon earth we should be very patient. When this war came to an end God would surely do great things ,for us. We felt that all this sacrifice would surely end in much fruit-bearing.--The choir sang the anthem " The Lord is loving unto every man." The Rector (Canon Willis) and the Revs. S. T. Stanton and G. Vale Owen (secretary) were present.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
Crimean Veteran Passes Away.

The death took place at a late hour on Tuesday night of Mr. George Haddock, of Ellison-street; Howley, Warrington, who for thirty-five years had been in the employment of Messrs. R. A. Naylor, timber merchants. The deceased had been in failing health for some time, but it was not until recently that he was compelled to relinquish his employment. Mr. Haddock had had • experience of warfare, for as a Member of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers he went through the whole of the hardships of the Crimean campaign, but it was difficult to get him to talk of those times. He was of a retiring disposition,. and was held in the high- est esteem by all. He was well known in the Runcorn and Halton districts. The funeral will take place this (Saturday) afternoon at the Warrington Cemetery.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
Insurance Commissioners and Deposit

Contributors.

The Finance Sub-committee reported to the Warrington Insurance ComMittee on Wednesday 'evening that there had been submitted to them revised model rules received from the Insur- ance Commissioners, the revision being -required to meet the amended arrangements now in force for medical certification. It was resolved that the amended model rules be adopted with the exception of paragraph 3 (2) kb) which shall read, "shall not, without the permission of the committee or some person duly author- ised by them in that behalf, be absent from home between the hours of 7 p.m. and 9 a.m. from the Ist October to 31st March,, nor be- tween the hours of 9 p.m. and 7 a.m. from the Ist April to 30th September, and shall nbt be absent at any time without leaving word where he may be found."

WHERE SPANISH IS TAUGHT?

One Month's Instruction For 10s.

There is no lack of facilities in Warrington for learning Spanish—the language which is the key to trade success in the Spanish Markets and the great countries of Spanish America. An evening class for beginners in this language is now in the course of formation at the Market Gate Branch of WOOD'S COLLEGE, the instruction will be given by a diplomated commercial specialist. Early application from those desiring to eiirol should be made to the Principal at Wood's College, Bold-street, or to the Tutor-in-charge, Market Gate Branch of Wood's College, Warrington.— (Advt.]

Tribute To Botha.

Mr: A. J. Thorp's tribute to Botha has called forth a reply from the great General, whose Secretary writes to the author of the lines "General Botha has asked me to thank you for your letter of the 22nd August last, and to say that he has much pleasure in accepting the poem composed by you."

Mr. Tom Tyrrell, Clairvoyant. To-morrow, Mr. Tom Tyrrell, of Blackburn, the famous clairvoyant and clairaudiant, pays a return visit to the local Spiritualist Society, whose meetings are held at the Druids' Hall, Sankey-street, in the afternoon at 3.15 and evening at 6.30. On Monday evening Mrs. Fox, a well-known local medium, will give clair- voyance, &c. Social In A Good Cause.

A very enjoyable parochial social was held in St. Peter's Schoolroom, John-street, on Monday evening. There was a good attendance of par- ishioners and friends, and vocal items rendered by Miss Sherwood and Miss Bamforth were very much enjoyed. Dancing was indulged in, and a most enjoyable evenifg came to a close about 11 o'clock. The accompanists were Mesdames Martin and Greaves. The proceeds, which were very satisfactory, are to be given to the funds of the Church Pastoral Aid Society.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
A Good Record.

Despite his multifarious engagements during the past year of war, with all the special duties and responsibilities it has placed upon him, the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) found time to attend 39 of the 45 meetings of the Insurance Committee and its various sub- committees held during the twelve months ended July 15th. Only one member attended more often than did his Worship, viz., Mr. W. Bibby, who was present at 43 out of the 45 meetings to which he was summoned.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
A Gentle Cow.

If there could be as awkward a customer as a bull in a china shop it would possibly be a cow in a fashionable dressmaking and millinery establishment. Such a customer made its ap- pearance in ,the rooms of Messrs. J. and W. Dutton, Sankey-street, last Monday morning. It did not come unceremoniously, but deliber- ately, and even at the moment the assistants were enjoying the sight of a visit paid by the animal to a shop immediately across the street. Whether it was the good humour or the excite- ment apparent in the faces and the movements of Messrs. Dutton's assistants that attracted Madame Ai-ache we know not, but she had no sooner left Messrs. Pattison and Perkins' pre- mises than she passed into Messrs. Dutton's, where the space in which to turn rather pleased her. An attempt to reach the showrooms up- stairs was exciting, but fruitless, and the intruder was induced to leave without making further investigations and without having done any damage of any kind. • 0. A. P-eers !

The lot of the old age pensioner in war time was one of the matters under consideration at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday evening. It was pointed out that the receipt of a pension disqualified a person from getting Poor Law relief, and the Council passed, without dis- cussion, the following resolution :—" We, the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, appeal to the Local Government Board to suspend, for, the period of the war, the clause which prohibits old age pensioners from obtaining Poor Law relief, and to empower grants of such relief to old age pensioners."—The Secretary (Mr, Chas. Dukes) remarked that it was a great shame, to say the very least of it, that a person in such sorry circumstances as to need an old age pen- sion should not, particularly at the present time, be qualified for Poor Law relief, if it were found necessary.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
Picturedrome Assessment.

Mr. Dickenson, on behalf of the Warrington Picturedrome, Ltd., sought, at the last meeting- of the Warrington Assessment Committee, a reduction on the assessment of their house in Sankey-street, and stated that owing to the war the takings were lower than in previous years. He supported a letter which the Clerk (Mr .4- Bottomley) read, in which it was stated that the increase to 050 gross was unreasonable for five years when buildings and adjuncts had remained normal. He said he could hardly con- ceive the committee putting up the assessment at a time like this. The entertainment world was suffering somewhat, and there was !ikplv to be in the future one of the worst periods that had ever been experienced. The committee, having heard particulars as to takings and profits, decided that the valuer I (Mr. E. Gunson) should interview the manager, and adjourned the matter until three montha I hence.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
Women's Bible Class Anniversary.

The Women's Bible Class at the WoAcing Men's Mission, celebrated their tenth anniver- sary by a tea and concert on Thursday evening; 82 members were present. Mr. Thos. Dale pre- sided at the evening meeting, And said he followed with great interest the progress of the class and its ever-increasing members and vitality. Mrs. Moore, the leader of the class, read the annual report, which spoke well for every branch of the work; 12 new members had been welcomed during the year, bringing the membership up to 146. The concert was a great success, all items being rendered by the glee party, members of the class. Great credit is due to Mrs. Morris, Mrs. Barratt and Mrs. Gray, who were respon- sible for that part of the work. Mrs. Rigby spoke (as a member) very highly of the work of the class and of the benefit she herself had received by being a regular attendant. Thanks were given to Mr. Dale for presiding;and tec all for making both tea and concert the great success it was. ,

THE ART OF HENRY WOODS., Annual Devoted To The Life And

Work Of The Great Painter.

The Art Annual, 1915, is devoted to the life and work of our former Warrington townsman, Mr. Henry Woods, R.A., the great painter of Venetian scenes. For many years Mr. Woods has lived in Venice and has painted the beau- tiful city in every aspect—the water carnivals, the gondola life, the daily routine of the in- habitants. His backgrounds of magnificent buildings, his foregrounds filled with animated people, and all his pictorial compositions are attractive in a special way. Many of the illus- trations in the book have never been repro- duced before. There are six plates in colours : "The Ferry," "A Net-Maker," "A Venetian Vintage," "The Honeymoon," "La Tombola, Venice," and "First Communion Day." Som'e, of the 'other illustrations include: "Rivals "La Promesa Sposa," "The Towers of Sap; Rocco," "In the Belfry of the Campanile,' "The First Communion Veil," "La Friulan- ella," "A Venetian Christening Party,'" San Rocco, Venice," "Cupid's Spell," " gaining for an Old Master," "The Water' Wheels of Savassa," "Saluting the Cardina,', :an "The Gondolier's Courtship," "A enet , t Autolycus," "The Admonition," and At he Fountain." There will be about fifty reproductions "f, pictures, including the six plates in colour, an Mr. James Greig gives a descriptive essa, Y. Mr. Percival Pearse, Sankey-street, la ing a large number of the Annual in view its special local interest.

PRIVATE G. LEWIS. Wounded.

whose husband has been wounded in the left arm in two places below the elbow, informs us that the patient is progressing as satisfactorily as can be expected. He is at present confined in the Vernon Park Military Hospital, Stockport. The bullet wound which he sustained caused a com- pound fracture of the -bone, which has turned out more serious than was at first expected. The doctor informed Mrs. Lewis, who visited her husband a day or two ago, that possibly the arm would have to be amputated, but that he (the doctor) would do his best for Private Lewis. He is to be treated under X-rays this week. Private Lewis was wounded during a fierce bombardm6nt whilst going into the trenches. He was hit first by a piece of shrapnel, which fractured his arm, later by a bullet which lodged a few inches higher up. Some time ago Private Lewis was "gassed " When on duty with a battalion in the trenches. Shortly afterwards he was transferred to the transport section of the battalion, and it was whilst serving with it that he was wounded. An old Territorial, he has served 17 years in the battalion, and was a little over 12 months in South Africa. He re- joined when the war broke out. " Got Such a Clout."

In a letter to his wife, Priiiate Lewis said: "We were going up to the trenches when a fierce bombardment began. It did not half rattle on. We went through in the old style, and all of a sudden I got such a 'clout.' I got one of the chaps to put my field dressing on, and I went in a motor for a few miles to a dressing station, from there to a hospital for a day, and then to another hospital, where the doctor looked at my arm. A piece of shrapnel hit it and fractured it, leaving a few pieces in; also a bullet lodged a few inches higher up."

_ - Private Sutcliffe was employed in the box- room at Messrs. Crosfield's works, where he was widely _known and respected. He joined the Territorials on September Ist, 1914, and was drafted out to France last February. He went through the great battle of June 16th and came out without a scratch, and was one of the first seven to obtain a furlough, arriving

home in the early morn of June 25th. His mother states that he was very reticent in imparting information concerning the ordeal through which he had passed, and of the hard- ships endured. Private Sutcliffe, when younger, joined the 'Boy Scouts, and later the St. Paul's Church Lads' Brigade, where his kindly disposition and good-nature earned him many true com- panions and friends. He was extremely fond of football, cricket and gymnastics. We trust that news of his safety will soon reach his parents, who at present are conflicted with anxiety and doubt.

CORPORAL J.

Writes That He Has Been Hit, But Is Quite Well.

Corporal James Whittaker, who before the outbreak of war was employed by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., and who resides in 'peace times with his parents at 40, Hale- street, was wounded on Saturday. Bullet Still In The Hip.

From the 25th General Hospital, France, he writes :—" I have been wounded, but am now quite well. I was hit with a bullet whilst sitting in a Maxim-gun emplacement oil Satur- day morning. It is not serious, being in the right hip. The bullet is still in and the doctor says it does not need to be extracted. lam expecting to be in England before very long." Corporal Whittaker enlisted soon after the outbreak of war. When he left for the Front he was a Private, and h 6 has been promoted to the rank of Corporal since crossing to France.

PRIVATE FRED GUY.

Old Wycliffe Volunteer In "New Home."

Private Fred Guy, who in peace times lives with his. wife and family at 38, Tanner's-lane, has been, wounded and is pow in the Warren Red Cross Hospital, Hayes, Kent. Writing from the latter place to his wife, he says : " I have got to my new home, as you will see by my address at the top of the post-card. It is a gentleman's big residence, and it is like being in heaven. The doctors and nurses give us every attention, only I have got put to bed for the present." Before going to the Warren Red Cross Hos- pital, Private Guy was an inmate of the Royal Herbert Hospital, Woolwich. Private Guy, who is forty years of age, was educated at Wycliffe School, and attends the P.S.A. in connection with that church. Before the outbreak of war he was employed by Messrs. Randall Bras., Ltd., tanners, Tanner's-lane. He

WHITTAKER.

PRIVATE J. NIANNION.

Suffering From INTounds--BUt No Particulars.

In an official statement issued from the Record Office at Preston, Private Joseph Mannion, of "A" Company of the Warrington Territorials, and son of Mr. and Mrs. Mannion, of 6, Derby-street, off Hopwood-street, is said to be wounded. The statement reads: "I regret to have to inform you that a report has this day been received from the War Office to the effect that No. 1.412 Private J. Mannion, of the 1/4th. South Lancashire Regiment,. is ill at (place not stated), suffering from wounds. 18th September, 1915. Any information received at this office as to his condition or progress will be at once notified to you." Private Mannion himself has not written home for almost a month. Nineteen years of age, Private Mannion was educated at St. Mary's School, Buttermarket- street, and also attends St. Mary's Church. Before the outbreak of war he was employed by Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, in the Tin- ning Department. Ile has belonged to the Territorials for about four years, being a member of the band when fifteen years of age. Three Other brothers are also serving with the colours. They are Private Edward Mannion, also of "A". Company of the Territorials; Rifleman John Mannion, of the Rifle Brigade, who was wounded in April; and Corporal Michael Mannion, of the Lancashire Fusiliers.

PRIVATE JAMES lIELSBY.

Secondary School Old Boy Wounded While Bomb-Throwing.

Yesterday an official casualty list contained the name of Private James Helsby, of 20, Algernon-street, among those wounded in the Great Advance. Not quite three weeks ago the misfortune was experienced; and he is now ia hospital at Coventry, where his arm is making , good progress, hut his fingers are still useless.' An unmarried man, aged 24, Private Helsby is the son of Mr. and Mrs. James Helsby, and in boyhood attended the Wash-lane School and later the Secondary School. As a galvaniser he was employed at the Whitecross Works until the war outbreak, wheji he joined the local Territorials. In February he left for the Con- tinent, and so finds hiniself back in England within eight months. He is a former member of' St. Clement's Mission.

PRIVATE WILLIAM HOUGHTON.

Bomb-Thrower Home, Shrapnelled In Right Wrist.

In an official list issued yesterday was con- tained the name of Private William Houghton —a local Territorial bomb-thrower, who lives with his parents, Mr. and Mrs. Josiah Hough-

ton, at' 84, School-brow. He is now hoe, slightly wounded by shrapnel in the right wrist; he arrived on 'Friday night of last week for ten days' furlough. Aged twenty-five and single Private Houghton was educated at the Hamilton-street Schools and had since been employed as a wet-drawer by Messrs. Rylands Bros. On the war out- break he joined . the 4th South Lancashires, going out to France eight months ago. Since then this it his first "trip" over to England. As a lad he attended the Brick-street Sunday School. His brother David is now in the Dardanelles, serving with the R.F.A.

DIED OF WOUNDS.

PRIVATE A. HANKEY.

Fatal End To Wounds In The June

Charge.

While taking part in the fighting on June 16th, in which, the Warrington Territorials played a glorious part, Private Albert Mulkey, son of Mrs. Howard, of 62, Sharp-street, and a member of the Warrington Territorials, was wounded in the right hip, and below the left knee. After being treated in France he was removed to England, and news this week reached his mother to the effect that he had died from his wounds in hospital at Leicester. Private HinkeY, who was twenty years of age, was educated at Arpley-street School, and was formerly a member of St. Peter's Church Choir. He enlisted shortly after the outbreak

SERGEANT W. HARRIS. Killed.

Corps at Chester some time ago. This gentle- man died only a little while ago, so the double blow has been particularly severe for Mrs. Harris, ; Wife's Dream Swiftly Fulfilled.

This lady, granting an interview to our repre- sentative, imparted some strange news to him as follows :

"I retired to rest on October 11th, 1915, at the usual time, feeling tired out after the day's toil. My thoughts, as I lay at rest, were with my hus- band far away in the firing line, and then I dozed in a fitful sleep. I had a strange dream, which now I look back upon with awful memory. "When I arose on Wednesday morning early I related my dream to the good people I am residing with. I dreamt that my ring papers had been returned to me blotted out with 'black with the exception of my husband's name, against which was written the tragic words: Missing—cannot be traced.' When I awoke] breathed a prayer of thankfulness, for it was only a dream. I dressed and came down, and had just finished dining when a knbck came to the door. It was the postman bearing the ill- message; tidings of my beloved husband's death. The dream had been fulfilled."

The above photograph of the deceased was taken when home on furlough at the beginning of August.

Woman Fighter in Indian Mutiny.

Mrs. W. Harris comes from an old soldier's family. Her grandmother (Mrs. Semmett) was the only Warrington lady who carried a gun in the Indian Mutiny. She acted as sentry to Sepoy prisoners. She died just before the out- break of the war.

Her father served 30 years in the same regi- ment as her husband. Her brother-in-law is in the same regiment, and is at present in England convalescent from the Front. Mrs. Semmett's daughter (Mrs. J. Phillips) is a retired Army Achooltaistress and her daughter also. Sergt.- Major J. Phillips was the bandmaster of the Royal Irish Regiment. Her daughter's husband was sergeant-major in the same regiment.

WOUNDED.

CORPORAL G. RENNET!.

Worrying Germans Lead To "A Bit

Of A Row."

Corporal George Bennett, of the Grenadier Guards, whose father and mother live at 79, Birchall-street, and who was formerly a shunter on the Cheshire Lines Railway at Warrington, writing to his sister, Mrs. J. Bradshaw, 85, Birchall-street, from the North Stafford Infirm- ary, Stoke, says : " You see I am at last settled in England, after my terrible experience of a week ago. We were clearing a trench where they had been worrying us a-great deal. It caused a bit of a row. We went into them.

"Well, to shorten the story, we were going along nicely when a big shell came along right past my ear and into the trench. The explo- sion buried me and blew my comrades to -, well, God bless them! I lay buried three hours calling for help.

Rendered Dumb. "Then I remember nothing properly until Saturday, when I found I was dumb, and now I am a wreek. They hope to get my nerves and speech back by careful -nursing. I only arrived here last night, but it is the finest hospital I ever saw for looking after men. I have had four lady doctors and six men doctors up to now, so I must be patient. I should be pleased of a a few coppers to buy stamps and odd articles, as Ilost all'my Corporal' Bennett, who.is-28'years of age, and

A

married to Miss Martin, of Winwick-road, now resides and works in Bolton. He attended Ham- ilton-street day school and Brick-street Sunday school. He has one child.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL R. FRASER.

"60t A Little Bit Here And There

AA'ith A Piece 01 Shell.

Lance-Corporal Robert Fraser, of the Sea- forth Highlanders, was wounded while taking part in the big advance, and is now progressing favourably in an. English hospital. In a letter to his wife, who resides at 4, Forster-street, he says : "You ask if I am badly wounded. I only got a little bit here and there, with a piece •of shell. It is nothing, but when I got it, I lost a good deal of blood. I am all right, and I get fed on the very best of food. When I came off the field I had no cap.- I lost everything, in- cluding some little things I was going to, bring home. I came to England without a cap, but I had my smoke helmet on. A good many people wanted it, but I think I will bring it home to remind me of . I never shall forget it !" Previous to "joining up," Lance-Corporal Fraser was employed by Messrs. Whyman's Foundry Co., Ltd. He enlisted in September of I last year, and went to the Front in July.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL ‘f. FOY.

Describes Irresistible British Advance. Writing to a friend, from a convalescent camp in France, Lance-Corporal T. Foy, of the King's Own Scottish Borderers, and son of Mr. and Mrs. John Foy, 4, Winwick-road, Warrington, gives a vivid account of the fighting during the recent advance made•by the Allied Troops. He

says : "All the time I have been out here I wanted to be in a charge. My wish has been grati- fied. My word, it was great to see our lads taking the trenches and advancing beyond them. When the story of that advance is told you will know what part the 'Jocks' took in it. They were ,pimply irresistible. The Ger- mans tried all they knew and used all their murderous weapons of modern warfare; but it was no use. Our line advanced just as if it was on parade; it was wonderful—smoking cigarettes and laughing and joking while the Hun's were pouring a murderous fire with their machine guns and shrapnel. "The officers took my eye. They were always ' first, leading the way and cheering us up. No wonder there is such a heavy casualty list of them! We would go anywhere with them; it is a pleasure to have such leaders. There is no turning back with them.. If the Huns had the same fighting spirit, what a different tale would be told of that advance! To give you •an instance, we were advancing in front of a deadly machine gun fire of the IlunS. "They Got Mercy."

"They were mowing us down, but as soon as we got in touch with them, after doing what they had done, they put up their hands, shout- ing Mercy, Camarade,' and offering watches And money, in fact, anything to take them pris- oners. They got mercy! One of our lads is worth six of them. They are all right behind machine guns and gas and everything like that, but when it comes to close quarters their num- ber is up! They have a practice of leaving men in their dug-outs and snipers Up trees to get. At us when we pass.-- There were four of us a few yards behind our front line waiting for a chance to rush forward, when the other three got shot in the back, and I got one in the heel of.„,my shoe. It was a sniper up a tree on our left, just behind us. He never sniped any mote. Hit in the Thigh, • "It was three hours after that they got me in the thigh with a bullet. I made my way back as• best I could to get the wound dressed, but when I got to the dressing-station they were very busy with more serious cases than mine, so I toddled with a lot more to the next place. When we got there it was only 8 miles from where we started. I shall never forget what a sight that battlefield was when we were on our way to hospital. I am not going to describe it; it was so horrible. Well, I eventually landed in hos- pital. I must say a word in praise of the doctors and nurses. They are absolutely it.' What a pleasure it was to wake up in the morn- ing on a snow-white bed and hear the nurses

===== 0002601_19151016_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
Buried In Coal Bank And Dug Out By

Officer.

News has reached Mr. John Taylor, 18, Bostock-street, to the effect that his son, Private Ernest Taylor, of the Royal Fusiliers, was crushed during the big advance, and is now in Seymour Park Hospital. Writing home, Private Taylor says:—" I got buried in a coal bank by a shell, but • our officer dug me out. . . . . When the order came for us to go over the ''sticks,' we went like mad. We took the first trench -with about six wounded, and then we went for the next. . . . Still we went on, till we put out all the Germans who were in it. Germans Cried For Mercy.

"I am only little, but the Germans were six feet, and they had to cry for mercy at our knees. . . . They tried to use gas on us in this big attack, but they made a great mistake. The wind was strong, and it was over us in about two minutes. It put a few of our men out. . . . We also made an attack on a coal bank, and we took it all right." Nineteen years of age, private Taylor received his education at the Heathside School. He was formerly employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Ltd. He enlisted list November. He is greatly interested in boxing.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE FRANK STANLEY.

•aid To have Been Recommended For The D.C.M.

Private Frank Stanley, whose parents reside at 33, King-street, Warrington, has been wounded in the shoulder whilst taking part in the recent great advance. He is known as the " mascot " of the South Lancashire Regiment. Writing home to his parents he states in his letter: "We were ordered to take the German trenches, and this we did, in spite of a galling fire. When the bayonet got to work the South Lancashires never stopped. On and on they

went, in spite of German cunning. We mowed them down as fast as ninepins." A box of tea tablets was the means of saving Private Stanley's life, for when he received them he put them for safety in the top pocket of his tunic, and the bullet glanced off. It is stated that he has been mentioned in connection with the coveted award, "D.C.M.," but he is reticent on this point just at present.

RIFLEMAN C. BUTLER.

Local Footballer Receives His Second

Wound.

Mrs. Butler, 100, Lythgoes-lane, has received news that her son—Rifleman Charles Butler, of the 9th Battalion Rifle. Brigade—has been wounded for the second time since he went to the Front. Ile received his first wound in May last, and he had only been back in the firing line about five weeks when he was again hurt, his injury this time being a bayonet wound in the left arm. Describing to his mother how he got wounded for the second time, he wrote "It was about 4.30 on Sunday morning . near the German second line as I was throwing bombs. It was awful—what with our shells land theirs! We had to shout in each other's ears to make ourself heard. Waiting For Order The Worst Part! "The worst part was waiting for the order to charge. When that was passed along we forget everything but to get to their trenches." Twenty-two years of age and single, Rifleman Butler was, before the outbreak of war, employed in the galvanising department of Messrs. Rylands's works, and he played football in the Warrington Works' Competition for his works' team, and won a gold watch. He also belonged to Rylands's Harriers team, and the Latchford Rangers Football Club. A brothei--Corporal \John Butler—was wounded during the fighting around Hooge on June 19th, whilst serving with the Warrington Terri- torials.

KILLED.

'LIEUT. N. B. ANTROBUS.

Knew No Fear And Died Like A Soldier.

The death of Second-Lieutenant Norman Briggs Antrobus, of the Warrington. Terri- torials, is reported, after service at the Front since the early part of the present year. Coloner Fairclough, writing to the Lieuten- ant's father—Mr. Ernest Antrobus, Park-road, Hale—says that the deceased officer had endeared himself to his comrades. One of his chief charms was his unfailing cheerfulness in performing whatever he was asked to do. "Our company," writes the Sergeant-Major (A Lieutenant Antrifbus' Company, "was under the command of Captain Armstrong when he was compelled to go • into hospital, and the duties of cempany commander fell upon Mr. Antrobus. You will realise that for a second- lieutenant to take over the command of a company working under very difficult and most dangerous conditions was a very severe task. Never have I seen a man improve so wonder- fully under. such a weight of responsibility. Major, Crosfield, who had charge of our work, was deeply moved, and said things about your son which would gladden your heart and make you feel proud of your lad. The regiment has suffered an irreparable loss. He died like a soldier ;he knew no fear and took 110 heed of his own safety. He was always most anxious about the safety of his men, and was calm, cool and buoyant under the most dangerous and trying' conditions." Lieutenant -Antrobus was 22 years of age. He was educated at St. Anselm'S, ,Bakewell; Sed- bergh, and Victoria University, where he was secretary of the Rifle Club. For some years he bad been a member of the Officers' Training Corps. He received his commis.sion in Septem- ber, 1914.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE ARTHUR STAMP.

"A Magnificent Fellow," IVriles Sympathetic Officer. Private Arthur Stamp, a member of the Machine Gun Section of the Warrington Terri- torials, has been reported killed in the recent fighting in France. The news was conveyed to his father, Mr. Arthur Henry Stamp—who resides at 39, Orford-street—through the medium of the following • letter from Lieutenant H. R. Clarke, machine gun officer : "I am awfully sorry to have to give you some very sad news. Your son, Private Stamp, was unfortunately killed in the big battle, which started the British advance. " Had Been Doing Splendid Work."

"Re had been doing splendid work with all his comrades in the machine-gun section, and whilst he was in a dug-out sheltering from shellfire, a big shell pitched on his dug-out and buried him and two comrades. One of them miraculously was alive, but Stamp And the other were killed instantaneously. I cannot say in words how deeply grieved his fellow- gunners and myself are at is death—for he was a magnificent fellow, and would always be one of the first to volunteer if anything had to be done. I can quite believe how deeply grieved you will be, and can only say that your grief will be shared by all of us. Many brave and gallant soldiers gave their lives-that night for England. I lost half of my section and even now cannot ascertain how -many are killed, Our one consolation is that we did our duty - and helped the great cause which we are all fighting for. Your son died like a brave and gallant soldier, and ;,s such his loss must be borne by all of us."

Private Stamp, who was nineteen years of age, attended, when a boy, the St. Mary's R.C. School, Buttermarket-street, and he belonged to the choir Of St. Mary's church. Before the out- break of war he worked with his father, who is a foreman bricksetter at Messrs. ,R. Collin and Sons. Previous to'this, he had been employed as a range fitter for Messrs. Richmond and Co.. Ltd. Ile enlisted soon after the outbreak of war, and had been in France sime February last.

Says Fight Was Knocked Out Of Enemy.

_ Whilst acting as ti bomb, thrower during the big advance, Private James R,owles, of the War- rington Territorials, whose home in peace times is with his wife at 129, Cartwright-street, was struck by a fragment of shell, and is now making good progress in a Birkenhead Hospital. Writing home he says :—" At about ten minutes to four our, guns started rapping away at the Germans' first line of trenches. The din was deafening, and the air trembled. We got no opposition in the first line, for the Germans had had all the fight knocked out of them. Then the two other lines were taken, and we gotplenty of prisoner. PARCELS FOR • GERMAN 1 .

Additional Funds Needed For Christmas Consignments.

This week's dispatch numbers 111 parcels to men of the South Lancashire Regiment in Germany. It is gratifying to note the receipt of ..,t2l from the Works' Bowling Association. Consid- eration is now being given to the supply of Christmas parcels to the prisoners, and as these will have to be dispatched during November it is hoped the funds will be forthcoming to allow the men to know that those at home are not overlooking- their plight whilst in cap- tivity, particularly at Christmas time. Many acknowledgments are received daily at the Pam Hall from prisoners, and amongst the latest are:- "Please accept my thanks for parcels which Sergeant Thomason and myself are receiving so regularly, as nothing could be more accept- able. Hoping it will not be long before I can tank you personally." "I received to-day a parcel of food sent by you, and thank you very much for same. Pleased to hear the Lancashire lads are play- ing the game. We are all fairly fit and keep smiling, knowing right is might. Please let committee•know we appreciate their efforts."

KILLED.

SERGT. WALTER HARRIS.

Wife Whose Tragic Dream " Imme- diately Came True."

The wife of Sergeant Walter Harris, Ist King's Liverpool Regiment, has received notifi- cation from the War Office that her husband has been killed in action.

Sergeant Harris, 31 years of age and a man of cheerfulness and kindly disposition, admired and widely respected by a host of friends, was killed in the fighting on Sunday, September 26th, when the great British move was in pro- gress. He was a Regular Army man, and was stationed at Warrington Barracks previous to his being transferred to Seaforth, 12 months last Christmas. Sergeant Harris, immediately ()A the declaration of war, was drafted out to Prance, and his wife made her abode at 218. Lovely-lane, where she has resided ever since. Going out to the firing line as a private he, by his ability was promoted in a short time to Sergeant, and had been through the engage- ment at Mons and other achievement associated with the glory of British pluck and valour. Sergeant Harris was a fine and clever army boxer, entering many scompetitions in connec- tion with this particular branch of sport. He was a brilliant marksman, and earned the coveted cross guns. Of music he was passion- ately fqnd, taking more than ordinary interest in all local concerts and musical festivals.

Writing to his 'wife four days before his:- death be Slated, in a letter particularly touch- ing and pathetic, that he was in the best of spirits and health, and uninjured, and was con- gratulating himself on his good fortune at coming out of the many stirring engagements successfully and unscathed. Not only has Sergeant Harris served in the present campaign, but he has also served in India and throughout the late South African War.

The deceased soldier's wife is the daughter of Quartermaster-Sergt. Chamberlain, a soldier who for many years was stationed at Warrington Barracks prior to his transfer to the Army Pay

October 10, ran as follows: "I have to write to you some sad news about your son, Rifleman P. T. Taylor, 10'cli Rifle Brigade. He was brought into here severely wounded; being shot through the chest, and in spite of all that could be done for him, the poor boy died yesterday. His funeral took place to-day, and I took the ser- vice. I know what grief this •letter must in- evitably bring to you, and one dreads having to write this, but I feel that you would rather hear like this from me, than the news come in ,a simple official notification, which will follow. I see .so much of it here that I know that it alone would convince one of its reality. God grant that in 'the great hour you may feel that you are not alone, but being led by God's pretence along the dark road into the light which really is ahead." ===== 0002601_19151016_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE E TUSON.

Old Warringtonian Meets His Fate In

Dardanelles.

Mr. and Mrs. Roger Tuson, of Stanley Villa, Chester-road, Grappenhall, on Tuesday re- ceived official intimation that their son, Private Ernest Tuson, of the machine gun section of the South Wales Borderers, had been killed in action in the neighbourhood of the Dardan- elles. Later came the following letter from Sergeant C. G. Prout :—" I deeply regret to have to write to you on behalf of the machine gun section and inform you that yonr son, Ernest, was killed in the evening of September 22nd. His death was merciful, as he was shot right through the head. He will be sadly missed by all his comrades, and by his death the machine gun sectn to which he was attached suffers a great loss." The deceased was 31 years of age and was single. He was brought up at Grappenhall and attended the Grappenhall School, and afterwards the People's College in Arpley- street, Warrington. For a short time he was in the office of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company. leaving there to go into the service of the London and North-Western Railway Company. He worked himself up until, at the age of 24, he had become a fore- man electrician, and in that capacity was transferred to South Wales. He had the dis-

Corporal Tombs, the Warrington V.C. hero. The Corporal arrived at the Nottingham Mili- tary Hospital the previous week-end, and had expressed the desire to journey over to Grantham to see his old school. Still suffering from the effects of wounds in the shoulder and right arm, and partially deaf, Corporal Tombs was motored over from Nottingham by some lady friends, and •it was extremely .gratifying to see him looking so well. The school was determined to extend to him a right royal recep- tion, and on, the arrival of the car at the entrance gates it was met by a guard of ' honour composed of the School Officers' Training Corps, with their band. The gallant hero was wearing neither his Victoria Cross nor his Russian decoration of the fourth class of the Order of St. Anne, but ribbons indicated that he was the possessor of both: After a short conversation with the masters, the O.T.C. had a march past the Corporal, and later, with the other scholars, they were formed up in a square, to be addressed by the Head- master (the Rev. CAW: Nicholl). He reminded , them that Corporal Tombs had performed a deed which, for bravery and perseverance and self-sacrifice had not been surpassed by any- thing- that had taken place in the whole of the war. Those of the school would never forget that noble and glorious deed which, had made Corporal. Tombs's name famous throughout the length and breadth of the land. He was glad to say that with respect to both the wound he had received in the shoulder and arm, and his partial deafness, their gallant hero was pro- gressing favourably. They all wished him a speedy recovery, and expressed the hope that later he would be granted a rest, for if anyone deserved it; he did. He had done his bit, and done it well. (Cheers.)

Corpopl Tombs also made a short speech, recallinj the pleasant days he had spent in the old school.

Afterwards the scholars joined in singing "For he's a jolly good fellow," and the proceed- ings—informal as they were on account of there being no undue fatigue for the dis- tinguished visitor—closed with the National Anthem. Before departing Corporal Tombs's wish to see the O.T.C. put through their rifle drill was gratified, and with the result he said he was highly delighted.

PRIVATE THOMAS DEAN.

,•otitil African Herm' Meets Ills Fate.

Official news has reached Mrs. Deon at 18, Amelia-street, that her husband, Private Thos. Dean, of the Lancashire Fusiliers, has ,been killed in action.

Private'pean was a St. Helens man, and was educated in that town, subsequently. working there, at Messrs. Pilkington's Colliery. He was an old ig 9.1 dier, and served throughout the Soeth African War. When in the South Lancashire Regiinent as a regular he was stationed .at Orford 'Barracks. He joined the Lancashire ,Fusiliers immediately on the outbreak of war, and was very soon drafted to the Front. He was thirty-six years of age, and leaves, in addi- tion to his wife, several children.

Territorials, and worked at the spoke machine at Dallam Forge, went to Australia and lived at the home of his sister, Mrs. J. Fay, Arden- street, Waverley, Sydney. THE V.C.'s INJURIES.

On the return of Corporal Tombs. V.C., to the fighting line he received a decoration from the Czar of Russia similar to the one personally given him by his Majesty the King. Unfortun- ately he was wounded. He was visited in hos- pital by his father (Mr. F. Tombs, of Chester), who found that he had been badly shot in the right arm, a shell having burst quite close to him. It had also injured his head, and some of his teeth have had to be taken out. We also regret to state that it has affected his hearing and somewhat shattered his nerves.

VUE lIITHUR. ABBOTT.

Mr. T. Dumbell, the licensee or the White Hart Hotel, Sankey-street, has received a letter from Private Arthur Abbott, of the Connaught Rangers, who was wounded whilst taking part in the recent fighting in the vicinity of the Dar- danelles. Private Abbott, prior to joining up, worked in Warrington, in which town he has many friends and acquaintances, who will be pleased to learn news of him. He is at present recovering from his hurts in a hospital at Exeter.

"As you can see," he writes, "the Turks 'did

it on me,' on August 25th. I got badly hit in both legs and in the left thigh, and, whilst pulling myself out of the trench, I got a bullet through the left hand—betwe& my thumb and. first finger.. I dragged myself a distance of two‘ miles, when I was picked up by our ambulance men and carried to the landing stage, ready to, go aboard the hospital ship. "While I was lying on the stretcher awaiting my turn, an officer near to me thought he would have a smoke. As soon as he lit his match it Turkish sniper fired. The bullet missed the officer and got me clean through the big toe on the right foot, so that I am 'made up' nowt I can tell you!

Assessments.

At the last meeting of the Warrington Assess- ment Committee, Mr. W. Furness in the chair, Mr. Davies, valuer, Manchester, appeared on behalf of between thirty and forty publicans of Warrington, who - appealed against the assessments of their houses.

Mr. Davies suggested that the settlement of the • assessment in these cases should be ad- journed for three months, when they would know better how they stood. There had been a discussion and they considered their output was. down 20 per cent. They did not know exactly how they stood, but there had been some talk of' Government compensation. Mr. W. J. Shaw said his output had gone down 50 per cent. since the last valuation, the expenses were practically the same, and there were five and a half hours' restriction in output. A few years. ago his place was assessed by Mr. Gunson at £75. Be knew at the time it was over-assessed, but he let the committee see he was willing to pay. Now the business was neither more nor less than half it was then, but he was quite prepared to accept £5O, the sum he was on before it was increased to £75. The Chairman explained that if the com- mittee were to come to any decision :they must have some figures, giving comparative details. He suggested an adjournment for a month, which would give Mr. Gunson an opportunity of going into the figures with Mr. Davies. Mr. W. C. Brooks asked if it were possible for Mr. Gunson to go over the whole lot of cases. That would help the committee, and if he would confer with Mr. Davies it would save a lot of tinie. _ _ .

The Chairman said if Mr. Davies was pre- pared to take exception to these valuations they could recommend Mr. Gunson to visit the premises. Mr. Davies: That suggestion could be con- sidered in three months' time. The committee adjourned the case of Mr. Shaw until the next meeting, .Mr. Gummi to report. Consideration of all the other eases was adjourned for three months.

members who can undertake ambulance duties, canteen and orderly work. Women of means and leisure, able to work during the day, are ,a pressing need, because so many of the girls who . give their services in the evening are engaged in offices or business houses. The organisation has more than three thousand members, and twenty-one country branches in England. Scotland, Ireland and Wales have branches also, and centres have been formed in Vancouver and Winnipeg, Montreal and New York. Regular drill forms- an important part of the training,-.but as this has proved im- practicable in- many eases, it has been decided to admit members who would form an auxiliary body, -under the name of the Women's Legion. Thus women able and willing to undertake various forms of work, but not to follow the military drill, &c., are now eligible as members, and would be warmly welcomed. The uniform of the corps has called forth considerable dis- cussion, but it is a serviceable dress, and its -colour does not show dirt.

Medicine As A Career For Women. The ranks of male, doctors have been so thinned that women must fill their places in the future as well as during the war. In recognition of the signal service rendered by women dur- ing the past year, the Charing Cross Hospital has opened its doors to them on equal terms with men. Two women doctors hold rank to- day in the British Army as Majors, thus the handicap of sex is fast .disappearing. Any well educated girl, seeking a profession in which she can serve humanity, win distinction, and earn a fair income, should not hesitate to take up medicine. Before the war women could not enjoy equal privileges with men its students, for London had but one school in which they could qualify, viz., the School of Medicine for Women. The training provided there is most efficient, and the Army authorities have ex- pressed unstinted appreciation of the value of the work done by women doctors among the soldiers in France and Flanders. Taffetas Still Leading.

Despite, the fact that manufacturers and dyers are short handed, and that they are labouring. under many other difficulties, there is ample choice this winter, both in woollen and other fabrics, and. in colours. The selec- tions of serges,‘ garbardines and cloths • are ex- cellent in quality, and as varied in tints and shades as last year. Silk and velvet are most effectively combined with . transparent fabrics, such as ninon and marquisette and voile, for smart afternoon and evening wear. Taffetas

still remain in the forefront, but as the weather .gets colder, velvet and velveteen, both plain and corded, will replace it. Dresses, coats and, skirts, and evening coats will all be made of velveteen, which i purely a British manufacture, and on account of its, soft, rich texture, particularly suited to this winter's modes. Styles are practical, whilst materials are sometimes curiously Mingled. The wide skirt is unquestionably popular, but there is a laudable feeling for moderation in width and length. Woven Furs.

Very few women will be in a position this winter to spend money on even moderately priced peltry, consequently this,year's wonderful woven furs will be warmly welcomtd. Manufacturers continually improve on these productions, but this season's goods excel any that have preceded them, and are so like the real skin that they are aptly called woven furs. These cloths are British in make, wear remarkably well, and the coats made from them and shown in the big shops, are equal it cut, style and finish, to the most sumptuous fur wraps. Long broadtail coats trimmed with fur, are exceedingly hand- some garments, and by no means prohibitive in price. Only an expert could detect that the material used .in their cdnfection was -an imitation. Novel effects are in moire and crocodile, and these are cheaper still. Very at- tractive sets of velvet and fur are shown which are inexpensive, yet look well and are cosy and ; warm.

High Collars And Hats. The collar, or perhaps its height, continues to be the most notable feature of autumn and early winter frocks and coats and blouses. In fact, the smartness of these garments Seems to lie in the shape and height of their collars. Coats for cold weather have huge collars ,in all manner of shapes, that take the place of last year's stoles. Some. are military in character, others are square in shape, resembling a sailor collar of immense size; yet others are as big as the •capes worn Early Victorians, and the latter are among the newest and latest designs. Height is also a characteristic of the season's millinery. Every other hat has a tall crown, whilst the trimming, or brim of the rest give the impression of an upward sweep. Old world riding hats and the topper, or high crown man's hat, have apparently been used as models. Quite appropriately plush. smooth and silky, is chiefly used in the confection of these pic- turesque shapes, that suit the fuller skirts and coats, and are becoming to youthful and piquant faces.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Most Brilliant Match In Europe. Germany has long thrived upon the rule that Royalty must marry Royalty. • Many of the Teutonic rulers of small principalities and duchies are far from being richly endowed with this ,world's goods, and they have mostly looked abroad for wealthy brides or bridegrooms for their sons and daughters. The war .has changed all -that, and for a long time to come German prineelets will find it ,impossible td choose hus- bands cr Wives outside their own .country. The Kaiser would, if he could, wed his only un- married son—Joachim—to the Grand Duchess Marie Adelaide of Luxemburg, who is to-day the Most brilliant match in Europe. Although the Germans have trampled over her .little State, she is reigning sovereign, and when she succeeded her father, she also inherited his immense fortune. The Grand Duchess is not only • wealthy but good looking, and is twenty- one. So far She has kept her numerous suitors at bay, and will have nothing to do with the Germans, who have made a doormat of her little kingdom.. Myrtle Trees For Brides. .

The war has brought about. changes in every social institution, but in nothing so much per- haps as in weddings. These are more often than not simple affairs without bridesmaids, and lacking the traditional ".breakfast " or the more modern reception after the ceremony. The Wedeiing-present habit that before the war had become almost a nuisance, has also declined, the time being. But custom dies hard, and a new and pretty fashion has arisen from the desire to send a' bride a gift, if only.. a trifle, on the occasion of her marriage. The myrtle has for centuries been considered the harbinger of good luck, eousequentlY a miniature tree in bloom has become a fashionable wedding pre- sent. When flowering trees are not available, sprigs are set in a pot and placed in a gilded basket. If watered at frequent intervals these little sprigs keep fresh for some time. The trees, of course, last much longer, and make a charming dinner table decoration.

CROFT CASES.

In respect of the appeals of Henry .Wright, the General/ Elliot Hotel, Croft, and Thomas Kerfoot, the Joiners' Arm, Croft, Mr. John Stephens, valuer, represented the , appellants. With regard to the General Elliot he said it was assessed at a gross amount of ..tl5O a year. The trade of the house was such that the sum was larger than it ought to be. He would not have the least obje6tion to telling Mr. Gunson what the trade was. It would not justify the assessment. _

Mr. Gunson : But you expect it to go back to the old figures? Mr. Stephens: Then you can increase. Mr. Gunson : It is a case of abnormal takings at the present. Mr. Stephens said that the reduction in the case of the Joiners' Arms was asked for prac- tically on account of the war. In the other it was asked for because the original assessment was too high. They should be satisfied if it came down to -,t40. The cases were referred to the Valuer for report.

Everyone who lives in the country or has a garden can produce something to eat—the more the better: vegetables, fruit, poultry, eggs, rabbits, milk, cheese. Plant at once what you can, and prepare in all Possible ways for next year's cropping. , Every Plant In Your Garden May Save You

Money. Produce all you can; buy as little as possible! Cultivate thoroughly! Destroy insect pests and Iveeds ! Prepare* manure! Preserve And Store Your Crops With the Greatest Care!

FAVOURITE HEROES AND HEROINES. Ml' DEAR CHILDREN,— . There were twenty-four entries for the "hero and heroine" Competition. The prize goes to 351 Nephew HORACE HIMSWORTH, 15, Fitzherbert-street, who wrote the following essay : My favourite hero is General Gordon. General Gordon's life is a lesson. We may learn from 111 him, as from all good men and women, lights to guide us in the formation of our characters and • the direction of our. actions. • He could not be idle. He had few opportunities, but when they came he was eager. He felt that the day is: given for work, wherever the day may find,us. What- ever was worth doing at all he considered was worth doing well. The second best letter came from 381 Niece Mona Archer, 11, heathside:— My favourite hero is Saint Stephen; he was a deacon- of Jesus Christ, and a man full of faith. He was accused of blasphemy by the Jews. Saint Stephen gave his own life for the faith of Jesus Christ. We should all look upon him as a great martyr. 496 Niecg Margaret Massey, 80,.Bostock-street, came .next : My favourite hero is Lord Nelson,- who was a very brave man. He fought three- great battles; which were the -Battle of Trafalgar, the Battle of Copenhagen, and the Battle of the Nile. He made England "mistress of the seas." As long as this world lasts Lord Nelson will always be remembered for the great deeds he has done. There is a statue of him in London, which many people go to see. The black tie which a sailor wears is in memory of the death of -Nelson. 479 Niece Maud Hickey, 35, King-street, .also chose Lord Nelson, and said he was a good and clever man, and he saved us from • the rule Of another country—which is quite true. I am going through the heroes first, and the heroines will come after. Here is what 493 Niece Jeannie Wright, ,10, Church-street, wrote:— You asked me to write about my favourite hero, or heroine, for my subject. I have chosen that great hero, Lord .Roberts. Lord Roberts was a

man who obtained the Victoria Cross. Last year everyfiody was sorry to hear about this great man's death.- He had been in earlier years one of England's greatest soldiers. He died in France, after he had inspected Our " Tommies " there. Lord Roberts was not only a brave man, but a good man. 516 Nephew Jack Barton, 42, Wakefield-street, wrote :

My favourite hero is Lieutenant Warneford. He belonged to the Flying Corps. Lieutenant Warneford destroyed_ a German Zeppelin, so he

was awarded the Vitoria Cross. Another modern soldier .was chosen by 504 Niece Annie Parker, 7, Railway Institute. Bank Quay : My favourite hero is Lance-Corporal Beale, R.E. He repaired the telephone from his battery to the headquarters, under heavy fire. He got wounded in nine different places, but he went on with his work. He was presented the D.C.M. 478 Niece Sarah Stephens, 15, Hoyle-street, sent this :

My favourite hero is Corporal Evans, L.F., who received the D.C.M. for a brave deed. While Tie was saving his officer he was wounded, but he managed to carry the officer about 10 yards

from the field hospital, but was instantly killed. Of course, a certain man was bound to come in, I am glad to ,say. This is what 499 Niece Nellie Griffiths, 22, Wakefield-street, said : My favourite hero is Lord Kitchener, who was born in Ireland. He has been in many great battles. He is the head of our War Department.
