HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes And Useful

Baked Beets.

Wash young and tender beets, and pitce in an earthenware baking dish with a very little water; as it -evaporates add more, which must be of a boiling temperature. - Set in a moderate oven and bake for two or three hours. When tender remove the skins, dud dress with lemon- juice or cream sauce. Cold Meat.

A (rood way of getting rid" of the remains of cold boiled meat is to cut up the pieces in small cubes or dice. Taking a quart as the proportion by which to .proceed, fill a baking dish with four potatoes .cut into dice and par- boiled for ten minutes. Add the Meat and also two slices of raw bacon cut into dice. Having in the meantime made a brown sauce by melt- ing one tablespoonful of butter and frying in it till yellow one tablespoonful each of chopped onion and carrot, turn this over the meat and potatoes, add one tablespoonful of 'flour,. pour on slowly a pint of brown stock, season, and then cover the whole with a paste. This can be easily made, and withal be very good, simply by using one pint of flour, one cupful of butter, .One half-teaspoonful of salt, and chopping together and mixing them into a stiff dough with ice-water. If desired, the butter may be supplemented with half lard, or part drippings. One thing to remember; do not put the crust over this pie while any Of the contents inside are hot; in fact, never put a pastry crust over anything while the steam is •rising, but wait for the under mixture to cool. A Substantial Stew.

Meat is at a prohibitive price, 'but there are still inexpensive cuts available, and for those who like chestnuts the addition of ten or twelve to the following recipe makes an exceedingly substantial Irish stew. of pleasant flavour. Have about lib. of scrag of mutton cut into • neat pieces, peel thinly 11b. of pOtatoes, and cut into thick slices. Slice two medium-sized onions, and have the chestnuts boiled in water and skinned. Now arrange all these ingredients- in a sauce- pan, sprinkling them with salt and pepper, add about half a pint of Water, and simmer very gently for about two hours.—" Church Guardian." Crecy Soup. -

Those in normally good health should use beans, lentils, pease, cheese and oatmeal in place of meat and fish. For good vegetable stock silb. of either rice, macaroni, pearl barley, or white haricots to • 4 quarts of water, and stewed gently for four hours, will make excel-

lent white stock for soups and gravies. For the Vown variety use 11b. of brown or red haricot beans, or red lentils, to 4 quarts of water, and stew gently. The outer leaves of celery, Cabbage, and other leavy vegetables, hnd the parings of well-washed and sound fresh root vegetables may all be utilised for flavouring this stock, if they are well cooked and then strained and put aside to settle. Ingredients: 1 quart Of white bean stock made according to three- tions given above, 4 large carrots, loz. of Quaker Oats, 20z. of dripping, 2 onions,' salt and pepper. Method: Scrape and It ut-- up the carrots, and slice the onions. Put the dripping into a saucepan, and when hot cook the vege- tables in it for a few minutes, being careful that they do not burn. Pour the stock over, and when at boiling point sprinkle in the oats; cook very gently until the vegetables are tender. Now rub !through a sieve or colander, return the soup to the saucepan, season to taste; re- heat and serve.—" Church Guardian."

Diamonds Burn Like Coal.

The jeweller at closing time was putting his diamonds in a huge safe. " But why do you bother to do that, when two watchmen walk the shop all night long?" " On account of fire," the jeweller replied. "Diamonds are nothing but coal—carbon—they burn beautifully. Their hardness makes us think them indestructible, but as a mattey of fact a fire of diamonds would be the briskest; prettiest thing in the world.' "Put a handful of diamonds on a plate and set a, light to them. They will burn with a hard, gemlike flame till nothing is left. There will be no smoke, no soot, and at the end the plate will be as clean as though just washed— not the slightest particle even of ash will remain."

THE HEROINES.

Now we come to the heroine-letters. Among these the best was from 491, Niece Cicely Northall, 116, Cartwright-street : My most famous heroine is Joan of Arc. She was only a poor girl, but in spite of the sneering from the people round her, she did as her con- science told her, and went to fight for her country. Her success was great, but she came to a sad end, as she was burned to death by her enemies. It is a name to always honour, the nante of the poor French girl, who gave her life for her country. - ' -

- - This was' very neatly written, and if only 'the rules had allowed nie to give two prizes the second would. hate gone to Cicely. Another member chose the , same heroine. HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information ln Popular Language. Akohol.

There is nothing to be said in favour of alcohol. It is a drug; it is not a food; it is not even a stimulant. It impairs the action of the heart, the liver, the stomach, the kidneys. It congests the blood-vessels. If lowers the tem- perature. It aggravates the effect of shock. It spoils brain control. It retards the rapidity Of mental action. it plays havoc with the muscles and with the nerve centres. In the emotions it destroys ideals, it produces callousness to moral obligations, and it multiplies lunacy and crime. Lastly, it gives an entirely delusive sensation of increased efficiency. These statements are not arguable. • They cannot be challenged. Its results on the conduct of the war are notorious. —Arnold Bennett.

The Blood and its Purifying. The .idea that the blood may be purified by medicine of ally kind is a very great error, and one which has been productive of a vast deal of mischief. There are no herbs or drugs the taking of which will purify the blood. The blood is not to be purified by putting something into it, but requires that something to be removed fronr it. Water is the universal cleansing agent, and its free use is essential to blood purification. Neglect to -maintaik the proper activity of the skin, kidneys, bowels, and lungs leaves poisonous matters to accumulate in the blood. If the lungs are not rendered active by proper exercise, the amount of oxygen - taken into the blood will be deficient, and the accumulating wastes 'will not be burned, or oxidised, and consequently will be deposited in the skin and other tissues, producing a dingy, sclerotic, or muddy com- plexion, and other evidences of blood Contamina- tion. By exercise, the amount of air taken into the lungs may be increased sevenfold, and blood purification may be proportionately increased. Neglect to keep the skin active by sweating baths, or, better still, perspiration induced by exercise, and daily cold bathing, prevents the elimination of the poisons which naturally pass off -through this channel. When the bowels are allowed to become constipated, the 'poisonous matters which are retained are absorbed into the blood, and become a source of universal disturb- ance and injury throughout the body. If a suffi- cient amount of water is not taken to dilute the blood, to wash the tissues, and 'encourage the kidneys in removing the acid poisons which it is its particular duty to separate from the blood, these highly injurious substances are retained, giving rise to rheumatism, gout, headache, and many other maladies. . Vigorous exercise out of doors is one Of the most important means of maintaining blood- purity.—" Good Health."

HARVEST FESTIVALS.

AVYCLIFFE 11.11tX EST.

him% Nature Proves Existence Of LiVillff God.

The Wycliffe Congregational Church was beautifully decorated on Sunday for the services of harvest thanksgiving, and the pastor (Rev. T. T. James) occupied the puliikt. The morning text was Psalm civ., 3: "Who maketh the clouds His chltriot, who walketh upon the wings of the wind." This psalm, said the preacher, was not a petition but a medita- tion; a Oman turning over in his mind the whole universe as he knew it, the 'whole world in its richness and beauty, and recoinising in it all the master hand of 'the living God. Nature was the voice of God become articulate.

lie supposed this was the first meaning of a harvest festival. It declared the world to them as the world of God and the sphere of His revelation. It reminded .tliem that the Divine revelation was not a limited thing. Behind the material and outward they reached out a hand to the Master Who had created them.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
Unbrco.kabic; Laws.

Speaking of the significance of Nature as it related them to God, the preacher thought it was impossible. in these days for them to talk about the natural world without realising that it was in ~some sense new. He did not mean that the mystic world and its appeal to the soul was different, but that they came into contact with Nature with a new feeling about it, possessing a knowledge of its history and development and processes. And sometimes he thought this new knowledge made their faith less easy to hold so simply and straightfor- wardly in the living God. But one of the things they had been learning through these days was the, way in which the natural- world was bound to hold together by universal law; the unbreak- able law of Nature was one of the great factors of the Universe. There were those to whom that idea seemed to make it difficult to believe that -God reigned and willed as men believed it once.; yet that might come as a great reinforce- ment in our faith; for, after all, surely it was the very fact of order and law that made it possible to believe that the Will and Intelli- gence lay behind, working always out of one changeless purpose and desire. Look at the strange story of evolution! Alt development had been the result of certain adjustments between that which was within and that which was without. The simplest and most beautiful forms that the natural world had to offer had taken their shape because of a certain capacity they possessed, played upon by certain Threes that they were without. This was true also of the human frame, and of higher things also—a mother's love, fidelity, honour, patriotism and so on:. Addressing that along the line of religious faith Man stretched out his hands to the Unseen because there was an objective reality calling to him—a God to be worshipped and adored. And they could not deny that without denying their scientific basis altogether. So that morning they found their way to 'God through Nature, and there was a sign of His will and purpose; and it was good for them. There was a proof that He lived, and loved and longed for them; and strengthened in their faith they might go home to serve Him and to do His will. .

THE VOICE OF GOD.

First Day Of The New Order In Revelation Of Truth And Goodness And Inspiration To Seek Them. London. On Sunday ,evening, in the. Friends' Meeting House, in connection with the half-yearly meet- ing of the Lancashire and Cheshire Adult School Union, Harry A. Lacon, of Birmingham, spoke on "The Voice of God." Quoting from the prophets of Israel, especially those of the Mosaic period, he referred to their 'close, personal in- timacy with God. Their account of these ex- periences seem strange, and sometimes grotesque to modern minds. There is a familiarity which almost seems irreverent. God appears as a great man speaking as a man to men, walking with them, leading them in battle, urging them on to the fury of the fight. This.anthropomorphic- idea of God only appears in the chirdhood of a great religion. And many devout souls have despaired because they have never been con- scious of God's voice, nor heard His word, in this intimate, personal way. For example, the story of Samuel and Eli has brought anxiety to many children, because it has not been made clear to them that God speaks..to men and to children in a thousand ways. Throughout the prophets the phrases are often used, "Thus saith the Lord" or "The word of the Lord came," and it is well to remember the various modes of Divine revelation to men. While we may still hope that some day we shall, in Tennyson's phrase, see " Our Pilot face to face," would it not be well if we could throw off finally the idea that here and now God's voice is con- fined to one definite, personal expression, and that He has no other method of revelation? Let us remember that Gct.d 'is seeking to reveal Him-- self and make known His will in a thousand ways. He stated.that if he were asked to define what the voice of God was. he should say the voice of God is the revelation of truth etnd good- ness, and the inspiration to seek truth and goodness, that come to us, through every avenue of our consciousness. We may not bear in the darkness of the night the clear call that came to Samuel; we may not have face to face talk with God as Moses had; we may never see the pillar of cloud by day and the pillar of fire by night; but we are not therefore without the power to hear God's voice

The Voice in Diverse Forms. Let us remember that God speaks to us in bird and song, In winds that drift the clouds along, Above the din of toil and wrong, A melody of love.

God speaks to us in every land, On wave-lapped shore and silent strand, By kiss of child and touch of hand, A melody of love.

Whatever stimulates me, said the speaker, to high endeavour for personal purity—is the Voice of God. Therefore all true poets have uttered the word of God to my soul. According to Tennyson, " Self-lieverence, Self-Knowledge, Self-Control; these three alone lead life to sovereign power. Whatever urges .me to -work for my fellow-men, in howsoever humble a capacity, in order that they may reach a higher state of life—is to me the Voice of God. What- ever revelation comes to me of the need of men or women or chihiren—is God's call to me."

Mr. bacon believes that God is calling every man in some way. If we have not seen His word written like Moses on tablets of stone, surely we have 'read God's message to men in " The Word Made Flesh." Revelations can only come to those who in the spirit of love seek to know the meaning of things. Even foul smells have sometimes called men to high Service to the community by means of the science of sanitation. In the foulsome dens of city life there May appear "sport" to a mere " nut," but to the Christian who knows that men were made in the image of the highest and capable of living a God-like life, a tragic call to work for its improvement. To other men the Voice may speak through art and. music, home-life and friendship. Christ, when speak- ing to Pilate, said: "For this cause came I into the world, that I should bear witness unto the truth. Everyone that is of the truth hear-- eth My voice." In conclusion, Mr. Lawn pleaded for the acceptation of this wide recognition of God's voice.

J usti liable Inference.

" I note," said the editor, "that you speak of Nero as running his hand through his long, flowing locks." "Yes," answered the author, "there is nothing remarkable in that." "How do you know that Nero had long hair?"

"My . dear sir, aren't you aware that Nero was one of the most celebrated fiddlers of his day?"

NO "TREATING."

A CHEERFUL ACCEPTANCE.

The Order for the prohibition of " treating " in the London area came into force on Monday. Its immediate effect was to reduce considerably the consumption of alcoholic liquor. Every- where it was accepted cheerfully, both by licence-holders ,and their staff and by their customers.

- The new ban, regarded from one point of view,, was an infringement of the liberty of the subject; from another point of view it was an emancipation of the subject from the bondage of habit: As between friends, the " standing " of drinks has always been recognised in,certain classes as the legitimate method of demonstrat- ing good fellowship. To Some men it had be- come, by long usage, as spontaneous and auto- matic as the shaking of hands; and—herein lay the evil—among most men it was quite as reciprocal. As between business men a joint drink at the cost of. one party was the common sequel to a deal. Treating, in short, was one of the established courtesies of life. Yesterday it became a grave offence, punishable by im- prisonment for six months with hard labour and a fine of £lOO.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
NO EVASION.

Afest Londoners, with ready altruism, ac- cepted as inevitable-- the principle that the majority must suffer, and they suffered, if not gladly,--nt least gracefully. The nlvelty of ihe thing appealed to them: it Wag too new to be irkyome. Light heaited yo ing men racked their brains and exhausted their ingenuity- in the attempt to find a loophole in the Order; but it was too cleverly drawn to be evaded, except by the connivane2 of the person behind the bar. That connivanoe was not to be obtained. Even the most fle::ible and obliging barmaid was deaf to entreatie.s. The pro- prietor had given rigid order that the pro- hibition must be loyally enforced, and the bar- maid, knowing the pains and penalties for in- fringing it, refused to tolerate any device, how- ever ingenious, for evading the law.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE WORK FOR BARMAIDS.

From the point of view of the barmaid or waiter the new restriction is not altogether welcome. Not' only does the Order throw upon them the responsibility for preventing "treat- ing 'f —a difficult task in busy hours—but itt necessitate g the separate handling of each man's drink and each man's payment, and multiplies journeys from the bar to the taps and the cash register. This, however, is counterbalanced by the decrease in drinking which has already been very marked, particularly in those City taverns where brisiness men have been accustomed to take drinks together immediately before and after lunch.—" The Times."

JOY FOLLOWS SORROW.

This for the heart that is aching to-day; „ This fcir the soul that has ceased to be gay; Tears like the raindrops must come now and then, Clouded the skies, but the sun shines again.; This for the eye that is shut to the rose: Joy follows sorrow wherever it goes.

Have you still faith in the Maker so kind, . Do you forget Him when trouble you find? Turn to Him then with your burden of care, Show by your faith you have courage to bear Heartache and worry and fate's heavy blows, Joy follows sorrow wherever it goes,

After the storm come the peace and the rest, After the heartache comes love to the breast; After the battle the weary ones sleep, After the tempest how calm is the deep! After the night time the rising sun glows, Joy follows sorrow wherever it goes.

Though you mud weep, let it not be for long, Though you must grieve, let your faith remain

strong; Brave in your sorrow with eyes straight ahead, Kind to the living though mourning the dead. As spring gently follows the ice and the snows Joy follows sorrow wherever it goes.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

A Scrious Blunder.

The little paragraph in last week's local news announcing the Cemetery Committee's con- cession respecting fees for lettering somehow brought to my mind an instance, occurring in a burial ground in a Yorkshire town in which I once liveil, in which a bungling monumental mason had transposed the letters in the name " Louiso " so that it read " Lousie " ! Singu- larly enough, the surviving relatives of the respectable girl whose memory had been thus unconsciously defamed seem never to have 'discovered the awkward error for themselves, for when I left the district years afterwards the groundless scandal continued to assert itself to all who had eyes to see. Gallant and Distinguished.

By a remarkable coincidence, just as I was reading a note conveying the gratifying intel- ligence that a young friend of mine whose home is in East Lancashire had had con- ferred upon him by the Emperor of Russia the Cross at the Order of St. George, 4th class, for "gallantry and distinguished service in the field," I learnt from this medium that a Warrington constable now a corporal in the Life Guards had received precisely the same decoration. Unfortunately, my acquaintance, disabled by wounds, was captured by the enemy, and is now a prisoner in Germany. His mother confesses to me that if he had died fighting she would have considered it a hap- pier fate than that to which ill-luck has con- signed him. He will not yet be aware of the honour done him by the Czar.

Curiosities of Dialect. As I read the account of Mr. Mansell's lecture at the opening of the winter session of the Photographic Society (" The Camera in Western Norway ") I remembered how forcibly I was struck years ago with the similarity of many common Norwegian words to corresponding expressions in the North of England. I simply mention the fact without multiplying examples, for I do not expect many readers to be interested in a subject which happens to have stirred my enthusiasm; but I will just add a striking English example from my own observation. Take tho word "going." At an catrome point of . Lanca- shire bordering upon West Yorkshire it is " beawn " ; only a few miles away in an easterly direction it is " baon " ; about Hali- fax it is " bean " ; and at Bradford, Leeds, Wakefield, and generally throughout the heavy woollen district, it is " barn "—pro- nounced exactly like the Norwegian word for child, this in the localities last indicated being, of course " bairn." The Workhouse.

• in my own time the union-workhouse was by older folk invariably termed " th' Bastile." Whether those excellent people, with their wholesome dread of the 'institution for indoor paupers, would have the like horror of it now that a more. enlightened and humane genera- tion has so thoroughly reformed it, may per- haps be doubtea ; all the same, some of my comparatively recent recollections of it are not exactly pleasant. I have known -indi- viduals who have been content to allow a near relative to spend his declining years in the workhouse, and, when death has mercifully released the poor old sufferer, they have been ready almost to move heaven and earth to prrent 'disclosure of the whereabouts of the event in the obituary column of the local news- paper. I could give" several instances from personal experience, but such things leave a bad taste in the mouth. Why Towels Particularly?

At railway stations and many public insti- tutions the towels appear, like the great Apostle, to be "in jeopardy every hour." I was reminded of it by a report of a local police case, which was adjourned last week and disposed of on Wednesday. Some half- dozen years ago, while visiting one of the men's wards at the Manchester Eye Hospital, I had a bit of talk with an inmate or the men's home connected with the; local unions. An old tradesman who had failed in business, ho was a person of more than average intelli- gence, and I enjoyed the chat. I admit that I was surprised when, in answer to my ques- tion as to what had struck him especially since his admission to that hospital, he confided to me that he was astounded when he noticed patients leaving towels in the lavatory. "Here," he had said to one who had just washed for breakfast, "you're 'leaving your towel." " Well," was the reply, " I always do. What about it?" Then he went on to tell how, at the place he came from, if one forgot his towel for a moment, it would be "pinched to a certainty." Something .over 50 years ago, when I was a youngster in London, the towels at Somerset House (as in the case of every Government office, no doubt) were marked with a broad arrow; all the same, whenever I visited the homes of any of the clerks, the towels in use there so far as I ever saw bore that same sign of State ownership. Unauthorised Hare Killing.

That " trivial case" of flio Croft farmer and the hare reminded me of a period when there was no Ground Game Act, and when the tenant farmer killed a hare at his peril. An uncle of mine named Harrison in those days hold Limdrick, near Studley, under the late Marquess of Ripon. I was once walking in his fields with a dog when the latter started a hare and killed it. I was obliged to hide the hare among some brackens and there leave it to rot. I dared not even report the circum- stance at the house when I got back. 'I think I have told before of a little adventure I had with a gang of poachers when a youth, and I must have had rather a weakness that way. On one of my visits to that same farm three miles from Ripon, I was walking alone in the park at Studley Royal, and succeeded in taking a rabbit from a warren. I put it in my inner coat pocket and was strolling home- ward when I met the head gamekeeper, to whom I had been introduced on the previous day. He stopped to speak, and actually hold me in conversation for a while—and there was,that uncomfortable little beast giving an occasional kick in my pocket. However, like Cmsar's wife, I was above suspicion, and so got clear. It was a foolish thing to do, for of course I was afraid to take the rabbit home. But there you are Some find towels irre- sistible; others will run almost any risk for a hare or a game bird or even a rabbit. I knew one old poacher against whom there was a formidable list of convictions, who as long as he lived continued to roam the moors by night, with a gun which he could take to pieces and put out of sight, and a pocket which ran all round the back of his big coat. He never did anything worse than that, and he dis- played qualities which I for one could hardly help but admire, reserving my contempt for the towel thieves. Musical.

From "The Examiner." report of the Infirmary concert at the Parr Hall, I see that the ballet music in Delibe's " Coppelia " was included in the programme. I have not myself heard-it for some fifteen months when it was given by the Royal Marines Band at a place which I happened to be visiting at the time. The selection comprised the prelude, mazurka, Slavonic folksong, Hungarian dance, a charming movement in 2-4, in which the flugel horns and basses were conspicuous, and a slow movement for solo cornet, concluding with grand valse. That was what my anno- tated programme promised, and right worthily it was fulfilled, leaving a deep impression and an earnest longing for early repetition. That was on the 28th July, 1914—the very eve of the outbreak of war, although we knew it not—and lots of German music was on that occasion performed which I warrant me the conductor has since decided must have a long rest.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
BERLIN.

Ufa in The German Capital 'A Great Decisive Event In Our "As A Dutchman Saw it." History.

JOVIAL AND CONVIVIAL.

BY HAROLD MAYBURY. Author of " A Russian's Impressions."

I met my Dutch friend the other day, he who hails from Haarlem, that picturesque and quaint old-world city of historic associations, famed as the birthplace of Frans Hals, the celebratbd painter, and the centre of the tulip industry. He had just returned from Berlin, after a sojourn of two months, during which time he had the opportunity of noting the con- ditions of life in the German capital, and also of obtaining impressions of the optimism which prevails amongst all classes of the popu- lace, that success is assured for German arms. "I arrived in Berlin," he said to me, "in the early part oT August, when the re- treat of the Russian armies 'was in full swing. 'The day Was a memorable one. The spacious capital had not altered much since my last visit there two years ago, except that -perhaps there was not the order maintained as was the case previously. The last rays of a fast set- ting sun cast a pink glow, over the city, and one by one the great arc illuminations in the Unter den Linden ' shone their light into approaching night. The streets were thronged with people bustling,and jostling one another, as they made their way along the broad thoroughfares of this spacious city. Many were journeying to the theatres and cinema halls, for it was nigh the hour of evening entertainment. They were lighthearted and gay, buoyant with enthusiasm, for the news had just been announced that their country- men were at the gates of the Polish capital. The kiosks, with their display of posters and coloured electric lights, were besieged by noisy Germans clamouring for the late edition of the Berliner Tagblatt,' which had just been published, and as they read the news their countenances beamed with satisfaction and excitement. Every now and then a cheer would be raised from the occupants of the tables seated in the many open-air cafes and gardens in this broad thoroughfare, the Friedrich Stress°, deep bass voices boisterous and loud,' shouting the praises of their Em- peror and country. As I journeyed along the Strasse ' I saw that Berlin was in one of its jovial and convivial moods. On the bal- conies of its buildings elderly Germans and their wives were making merry. Frauleins ' were there, of tender years, throwing flowers to the passers-by in the street below, and the clink of glass and sound of laughter were to be heard on every side. The sellers of the pic- ture postcards whereon were depicted carica- tures of the Grand Duke and the fleeing Rus- sians were doing a brisk trade at the street corners, .as were the proprietors of the many cafes to be found in this broad and capacious boulevard. As I sat in ill() Cafe Bauer, with its famous frescoes, the babel of voices sounded unpleasantly to one's -ears, noisy toasts to the Emperor mingling to the strains of the National Anthem played repeatedly by an orchestra composed of old men, grey and be-spectacled; blue pewter pots banged heavily on the marble slabs of the tables, coarse Germans of mature years shouting im- proper jests to their countrywomen ; this is what greeted one On arrival that August even- ing.

"It is not good to be anything but a German nowadays in Berlin, so I quitted the restaur- ant just as the proprietor was announcing the news that Warsaw had fallen. Mon Dieu', what cheers; it was deafening. By now Ber- lin was 'a blaze of light, for it was night. I made my way past the English and French Embassys in the Wilhelm Strasse and:Parisier Platz respectively, and as I rooked up at the buildings now silent and unoccupied, I thought of how the news would be received in this England of yours of Warsaw's fall. One soon forgets the horrors of war in a busy throng, or when one is flushed with the excite- ment of victory. Berlin, on this cool sum- mer's night, was intoxicated with success, for not once did I hear the remark What of the price?' Only were they aware that at last their Emperor had fulfilled his promise. It was only in the poorer quarters of the city that one found a little respite from the glamour and glare of the principal streets. It was here that I, a neutral in a strange capital, saw the toll of war. Young women were to be seen there, a great many draped in black, standing at the entrance of their humble dwellings, each pre-occupied with their own thoughtsplooking up into the now starlit sky, maybe thinking of their own kith and kin, whose blood had drenched the fair plains of Flanders, Franco and Poland. I heard the prattle of children playing at soldiers, others tugging at their mother's skirts and asking when father was coming home. It was here, my friend, that I thought of the awful price; just here in this part of the city. Yet no matter what station of life one may come in contact with, this much is to be found. Rich and poor alike are confident that victory is assured for German arms: what else can they think when it is only the successes that are proclaimed? Yet I often wonder whether there comes to the German mind the thought of why it was their fighting machine never got to Paris. I wended my way back to my hotel, for the hour was getting late. Still to my ears came the rumble and noise of this busy city, sounding in the distance like the break- ing of surf upon the seashore. It was only at dawn that the noises ceased, and the city grew silent, and with it came a chill wind blowing from the East, from across the vast Steppes of the mighty Russian Empire, and as I looked out through the double windows of my room it seemed like an omen, for as I thought of the inexhaustible number of Rus- sian soldiers fighting for liberty, justice and honour, it was as though they were likened unto the breeze of this grey dawn, over- whelming and uncheekered."—So I left my in- teresting 'Dutch friend, he who had lived amongst the German people for a little while, seeing things and life as they really are. His concluding words were, "I am only a neutral, but my sympathies are with ." I under- stood.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. ASQUITH ON OUR RESOLVE. Victory At %Nit:ll(l'er Sacrifice And Cost.

My constituents, to whom I owe a deep debt of gratitude for their sympa- thy and support, will, I know, recognise the paramount claims of public business upon my time. Had I been able to join you I know that I should have found in Fife the same unity of aim and spirit and steadfastness which animates the country from one end to the other. The spirit of fixed determination, at no matter what sacrifice, to carry the war to a victorious con- clusion has never wavered from the first moment that we took up arms at the call of duty, and no momentary difficulties can for an instant weaken it."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
THISWEEK'S ANNIVERSARY.

BY W. J. LEAPER.

"Every schoolboy knows the date of the Battle of Hastings," wrote Macaulay, and though in a sense this is true, there are not many who know the exact date. It was on Saturday, October 14th, 1066, that this, one of the most famous and decisive battles in the history of England, was fought. In passing, it might be said that October is, above all other months, the month of battle, for there is not a day that does not commemorate some victory. Besides Hastings, it is the month of Sebastopol, Trafalgar, Edgehill, Agincourt and Balaclava. It also commemorates victories won by present Allies against the present enemy. On October, 7th, 1571, the Italians (with the Spanish) obliterated the Turkish Fleet at Lepanto; October 10th, 1806, France defeated Prussia at Saalfield ; on October 14th, Napoleon defeated the Prussians, and, later, entered Berlin; on October 15th, 1877, the Turks were overwhelmed by the Russians at Aladja Daga ; on October 22nd, 1876, Turkish forces were captured by an inferior Montenegrin force at Medun; and on October 20th, 1805, the flower of the Austrian Army surrendered to Ney. So October can claim the doubtful privilege of being the month ' of battles. -

The chief interest of the Battle of Hastings is materially enhanced by the high personal character of the competitors for the Crown. of England. At this time they were three in number; one was a foreign prince from the North, another was, a foreign prince from the South, and the other was a native hero of our land. They were Harold Hardrada, the strongest and the Most chivalric of. the kings of Norway; Duke William of Normandy; and the Saxon Harold, the son of the Earl Godwin. Never. was ,a nobler prize sought by noble champions!

===== 0002601_19151016_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
HAROLD SWEARS ON THE RELICS.

A little time before the death of Edward the Confessor Harold was in Normandy, and though outwardly treated with every courtesy, was practically a prisoner in the hands of William. Before a full assembly Of Norman barons, Harold was forced to do homage to William. Kneeling down, Harold placed his hands between those of the Duke and repeated the solemn form by which he acknowledged .the Duke as his lord and promised to him fealty and true service. But William was not satisfied with this. He caused all the bones and relics of ,the saints that were presesved in the Norman monasteries to be reflected and placed in a chest, which rested in the Council room, and was covered over with a cloth of gold. On the chest of relics, which were thus concealed, was laid a misspl. Harold was then summoned, and, addressing him, William said, "Harold, I require thee, before this noble assembly, to con- firm by oath the promises that thou bast made to me, to assist me in obtaining the Crown of England after King Edward's death, to marry my daughter Adela, had to send me thy sister that I may give her in marriage to one of my barons." Taken by surprise, Harold was not able to deny his former words, and took the oath required by William. When Harold rose from his knees, William showed him upon what holy relics he had sworn, and we are told that Harold "was sorely alarmed at the sight." Soon after this Harold was allowed to return to Eng- land, and on January .sth, 1066. Edward. the Confessor died. Harold resolved to disregard the oath . he had made in Normandy, and on January 7th wav anointed King of England, re- ceiving from the Archbishop's hands the golden crown and sceptre of England and also a weighty battle axe, an ancient national symbol. It was not long before William sent a messenger to Harold reminding him of his oath. "It is true," replied the Saxon King, "that r took all oath to William, but I took it under constraint; I promised what did not belong to me; my royalty is not my own." 'William asked that the Pope should decide who was the rightful king, but Harold refused to . acknowledge an Italian priest as his judge.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
WILLIAM INVADES ENGLAND.

William at once began to make preparations for the invasion of England, collecting a vast army. Harold likewise made Preparations to repel the invader, his hopes resting in both an army and navy. But the unexpected attack in the North by King Harold Hardrada of Norway upset the skilful plans which the Saxon had prepared. The Norwegian King landed in Yorkshire, and it was not long before the gates of the city of York were opened to him and all the country between the Tweed and the Humber was under his control. This news compelled Harold to leave his position on the Southern coast in order to defend his country. By a remarkably rapid march lie reached Yorkshire in four days, taking the Norse King by surprise. A battle was fought at Stamford Bridge, which, after a long struggle, resulted in a victory for the Saxon. The victory, however, cost Harold many of his best officers and men, and the time he was away gave William his opportunity of landing in Sussex. Harold was at York rejoic- ing over his recent victory when it became necessary for him instantly to hurry southwards to meet his long-expected enemy. He stayed at London for six days increasing and equipping his army. Then he pressed forward as rapidly as his men could march through Surrey and Sussex, hoping to take the Normans unawares, giving them a surprise similar to that which he had given the,Norwegian, but now he had a far more wary foe. Near Hastings, the Duke had fortified a camp and had built two wooden castles. William also sent strong bodies of cavalry into the Country as scouting parties. Harold found that from the point of view of numbers his army was inferior to William's, and lie was advised to retreat to London. This policy was unquestionably the wisest, for Wil- liam was absolutely cut off from France, and Harold could have dealt with him at his leisure; but Harold's blood was up, and, being eager for the fight, would not listen to this wise counsel.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BATTLE.

Harold Placed his army in a strong position in a little place then called Senlac, but where now are the ruins of an abbey built by William to commemorate the victory. It is easy to trace the scenes of the chief incidents of the action; and it is impossible to deny the sound generalship shown by Harold. After vain negotiations, the battle was fought on October the 14th. From the chronicles of an old Norman writer it is easy to imagine the scene that morn- ing as the Normans swept over the green fields to attack the Saxons at three points. The Saxons wore entirely on the defensive, and had built a fence in front of them with their shields and ash, "Their aim," we are told by the chronicler, "was to defend themselves; and if they had remained steady for that purpose, they would not have been conquered that day; for every Norman who made his way in lost his life, either by hatchet or bill, by club or other weapon. The Normans moved on to the assault, and the English defended themselves well. Some were striking, others surging onwards; all were bold and cast fear aside, Loud and far resounded the bray of the horns; and the shocks of the lances, the mighty strokes of paces and the quick clashing of the swords. One time the Englishmen rushed on, another time they fell hack; one time the men from over the seas charged onwards and again at other times retreated. The Normans shouted 'Dex aie ' and the English people 'Out.' Then came the cunning manoeuvres, the rude shocks and strokes of the lance and blows of the' swords. a mohg the sergeants and soldiers, both English and Norman. When the English fall' the Normans shout. Each side taunts and defies the other, yet neither knoweth what .the, other saith ; and the Normans say the English bark, because they understand not their speech. From nine o'clock in the morning, when the combat began, till three o'clock in the afternoon the battle was up and down, this way and that, and no one knew who would conquer or who would win the land." Then the Norman archers began to shoot upwards, and Harold's eye was struck. The Normans pretended to flee, and when the English had left their barricades turned upon them. The English fell back upon their standard and after a glorious fight this was beaten down and taken. Harold was killed and, victory rested with the invaders.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
A BLESSING IN DISGUISE.

Many historians, including Sir Edward Creasy, consider Hastings a national blessing in disguise.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 16, 1915.

BATTLE OF HASTINGS.

COTTON AND WAR.

Mr. H. Whitham's Riflections

On A Previous Crisis.

OUR ATTITUDE TOWARDS UNITED STATES.

A little over twelve months ago few people saw, or knew little of, the close relationship that exists between some of the deadliest ex- plosives and the material grown in the Southern States of America, that is so welcome in the cotton factories of Lancashire.

Cotton is being used by the Germans to make the explosives which are killing British soldiers in the West, French and Belgian soldiers. on the same line, and compelling the retirement of the Russian Armies in the East. Cotton cannot be proditced in AustroLGermany. So it comes about, if the Allied Navies can keep cotton out of Germany, she must be crippled for want of it. The above, so tar as it goes, being quite a correct statement of the situation, the reader will conclude at once that the question of cotton must, at this time, be a very much debated one, where different races meet. I am new thinking that then must be a few young fellows like myself who well remember the Civil War that raged in the attempt to rend asunder the United States. This war meant a cotton famine in Lancashire. It meant the factory workers forming themselves into groups and travelling all over the country seeking aid for themselves and for those at theme that could not travel. Every one of the Fraternal Orders freely gave of their funds. During a part of that fight. for the freedom of the slave and the integrity of the United States I was sitting N.G. of the "Prince of Wales" Lodge, L0.0.F. I think every official of every lodge of every Order in Warrington (lid, at that time, his duty, not only to his fellow country men and women, but to North America.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
British Workers and.the States.

Now, I call two men to witness to the attitude of mind of the British working men during those terrible three years—Henry Ward Becher and the then President, Abraham Lincoln. The following thoughts, put into words, and with the caption, "The Last Manuscript," were found on H. W. Beecher's table after the attack which resulted in his death :

"We steamed up the Mersey in a dull, rainy morning. When the tug came off to the ship it brought a committee from Manchester request- ing me to speak in that city. . . . I gave them a sharp refusal. In my then state of mind I felt that England had played false to my country. I was thoroughly angry. I deter- mined to pass through to the Continent and shake off the dust of my feet against • our un- natural mother." The above describes Mr. Beecher's state of mind on landing in England. A. change came over his mind. He saw the attitude of the Tory mind, and then he saw that "The labouring classes, especially cotton workers in Lancashire. were friendly to the cause of the North. But for the non-voting hand workers . of Great .Britain, Parliament would without doubt have decreed belligerent rights to the South. It was in the hearts of the legislators,, but they were restrained by the knowledge of the strong sympathy of the com- mon people for the cause of liberty. It would naturally be expected that the men whose live- lihood depended upon the South 'and its cotton would be prejudiced against a war which in- terrupted commercial intercourse and stopped the supply of cotton.• A more pathetic example of the heroism of the poor was never exhibited than in the ease of the Lancashire weavers. They saw their industries wasting, the bread grew scarce, even their poverty became peorer, nor was there any 'sign upon the horizon that this cloud would soon pass away, and yet they held fast their integrity, and, believing that the cause of the North was-the cause of the day labourer the world over, they patiently bore famine and distress with fortitude till the day dawned. .No other men among all English- sneaking people gave a testimony of the love of liberty so heroic and so pathetic as the weavers of Lancashire."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
Object Lesson for Slackers.

I quote now from a paper read by Robert Applegarth before the Hotspur Club, London, in 1898:

"In December, 1862, I visited the centre of the famine district in Lancashire. In consequence of the American War stopping the supply of cotton, many women and children died for lack of food, and thousands would have starved to death but for the public subscriptions. Still these poor people, with little food and less fuel. met in the midst of. their dire distress and passed a resolution congratulating Abraham Lincoln on his having abolished slavery." On January 19, 1863, Lincoln replied: "I cannot but regard your decisive utterance upon the question as an instance of sublime Christian heroism, which has not been surpassed in ,any age or in any country." If the above plain and unvarnished tale can sink deep into the hearts of the Lancashire boys of to-day. " slackers " will hide their head in shame. To-day is the 27th of September, 1915. my readers, like myself, have had the best of news from the Western front; rush the men, rush the war munitions, and strike one more heavy blow for Liberty.

WIIITIIAII.

San Francisco.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
EUROPE'S REVOLT AGAINST

ALCOHOL.

All the nations engaged in war have had to take measures in this time of stress to guard themselves against the " internal enemy," strong drink. The Rev. H. Carter, who has issued a pamphlet with the above heading, is well known as a writer on temperance and social sub- jects, and he knows how to marshal the facts A succinct account is given of what has been attempted both by our Allies and our epemies. The wonders of reform in Russia are particu- larly noteworthy. But it is the condition of affairs in our own land which 'demands and receives most serious consideration.

Mr. Lloyd George and others have made the public aware'of a very grave state of affairs, and Mr. Carter applies the instruction. He focusses. attention on the important matters or drink in relation to the soldier, to the maker of muni- tions, and to the transport worker. No wise man can ignore the perils through which the nation has been passing, and every removable evil Ought to be removed. Especially significant is the statement that the evil which all the nation deplores •to-day is not new; it is the old evil, tolerated for so long, now revealing its true nature in the lurid light of war, a menace to the national welfare, a hindrance to victory, a peril even to national existence, in some re- spects the most disturbing factor in a critical situation.

"It is the case against the normal use of strong drink which has been established. When peace return, a determined effort to uproot the mischief must be the first charge on the time and skill of Parliament." This is the conclu- sion of a careful and well-balanced argument based upon the consideration of indisputable evidence.

The publisher of the pamphlet is Chas. 11. Kelly, and the price is one penny.

BATTLE OF HASTlNGS.—Continued.

There is no doubt that England owes her liberties to her having been conquered by the Normans. The political liberties of England arose from the situation in which the Anglo- Saxon and the Anglo-Norman populations and laws found themselves placed relatively to each other in this island. The gradual blending ,of the Normans and the Saxons softened the harsh and evil points in their characters, and in return the Saxons were fired with a new epirit or animation and power. The authority of Gibbons may be taken as decisive when he pro- nounces that "Assuredly England was the gainer by the conquest,"-

===== 0002601_19151016_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. W. BRACECAMP.

Vicar-Chaplain Talks Of " Tommy " And His Task.

JUSTICE AND RELIGION.

This year's harvest festival services at St. Paul's Church, Warrington, were made memor- able by reason of the fact that the popular Vicar (the Rev. W. Bracecamp), who fOr many months past has been serving at the Front as an Army chaplain, was able to pay a flying visit to his parish and to preach at both the morning and evening services. It is almost unnecessary to add, there were crowded congregations. The reverend gentleman appeared to be in the best of health, and his sermons, which contained many references to the war, were delivered with his accustomed vigour.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
ON THE SIDE OF HIGHT.

Why The Allies Cannot Be Beaten.

Taking as his text in the evening the familiar words, "Lift up your eyes and look upon the fields, for they are white unto harvest," the Vicar began by saying that he felt very much like one of old, who said on a very particular occasion, "It is good to be here !" He could not express to them his feelings of thankfulness to Almighty God that He had given him the privilege and opportunity of once more coming back to them aml of meeting them as. he was meeting them that night and of telling them once more "the old, old story of Jesus and His love!" The text bade them lift up their eyes. There were people who were always wanting to look down; always wanting to look on the dark side of things—people who were pessimists. Probably there had been something of the pes- simist about them all just recently; they had said to themselves over and over again, "When- ever will the war end? When are we going to make progress?" When they had heard what they had thought to be very luld, sad news, they had been depressed; had thought that time country was losing her all and that the enemy was gaining ground and was going to, win in the end. They seemed to forget all the time that this country and her Allies were on the side of right; that their cause was right and that G'od was ever on the side of right. An Important Lesson for the Nation.

God was going to teach this nation an imiiort- ant lesson; He was going to get the people on their knees before victory came—He was trying them as He tried Abraham. He could tell them, only he dared not—his mouth was closed—of noble deeds that had been done by the leaders of the Armies—of the men'who were leading and not following the brave lads on to victory, for who could doubt that, with such gallant leaders and such brave fellows to lead, there could be any question as •to what the result of the war would be? lie wanted them if they could to conjure up a picture of the terrible warfare that was going on; this warfare of right agpinst wrong; this war which was one of the most bloody—one of the worst—that had ever dis- figured history ! Taking part in this dreadful war were lads from every part of this country.; lads from every part of Britain's vast dominions, and they .were full of courage, and were pre- pared to give their all without a murmur—their lives themselves, if necessary, and in how many instances had even that supreme sacrifice had to be made?—knowing that they were making the sacrifice for'what was right—for liberty, justice, and religion ! " Tommy's " Pertinent Question !

Frequently as he went amongst them in the battle area they said to him, "What are our friends doing at home? How are they helping Us?" He answered them as well as he could that they were using—at least, be thought and hoped that they were using one of the strongest weapons possible, and that was the weapon of prayer. He could tell the people at home of the wonderful machinery that had been introduced into the battlefield—machinery of which th4y would hear later on—the could tell them of the work performed by that machinery, but instead be would just tell them one striking truth, and that was that if all the machinery in the world were put together it would still fall short of the power of prayer—that great and wonderful power that had no equal. ''He was afraid that the great majority of the people had never be- lieved in the power of prayer as they ought to have done; their prayers had been a farce. When the war broke out how keen they were to go to church and pray! But the war had been raging a twelvemonth now, and they were get- ting tired of praying! .Was that not so with many of them? Were they not leaving the result to chance, hoping that the Allies would pull through somehow? Do Those at Home Yet Realise?

He did not believe that six out of every ten persons in England realised what was happen- ing, or had the slightest idea of the suffering, or cruelty, of that abominable war. He could not think that they had the slightest conception. Otherwise it would be unnecessary to keep on asking for men; otherwise the appeals would not meet with refusal! "Is it," the reverend gentleman continued, "that you don't believe us? Have we got to keep on showing you specimens of the wounded before you believe that there is any reality about the war? Have we got to keep on sending round the drum? It is a terrible state of affairs when everything is at stake—the people's homes and their religious and civic liberty. The mothers of England of to-day, who .are holding back their sons, have taken upon themselves a tremendous responsi- bility. Out there I have seen homes broken up —I have seen the little children weeping because they have no homes—and yet there were living in luxury and ease in England some who said, 'lt's not our business. It is only the business of those who are soft enough to volunteer.' We want .Men and we shall have them! Do not forget that!

===== 0002601_19151016_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
If Not As Volunteers ?

"If we do not get them as volunteers, we shall compel them to go, because we have got to flog the enemy; we cannot do that without men." Continuing, the reverend gentleman said that he wished those at home Could have heard what the lads at the Front had to say about the men at home who were striking, or talking of striking, for a penny an hour extra. Why,- the French- men were working for twopence a day, and proud to do it for the sake of their country! Let the workers of this country think of what the " Tommies " were doing! Fighting their battles for them, risking their lives almost hourly—and all for a shilling a (lay. Yet there were those at home who must have their enter- tainment; must go on as usual, leading their selfish lives! In impassioned tones the preacher went on to refer to the fact that the churches of France and Belgium were daily visited by the prayerful faithful, whilst, he heard, the churches of this country were empty, compara- tively speaking. In this land the people were going on just the same as if there were no war, and that, despite the fact that all the horrors of which they read, and which their fellows had experienced, were so near to their own doors that if he started out for the war zone at 5.30 in the evening he would be in the midst of the battle area at 5.30 the next morning. It was a privilege to have been permitted to do the work he had done out among -the poldier lads. There in the land where Death stalked by day and by night he had no trouble to get men to come to church parade; he had no need to ask "Should I pray with you?" when he saw them lying .wounded in hospital. No; on the con- trary, as soon as the -men saw him, or any og the Padres come in, they said "Offer up a little prayer for me, please !" In conclusion the reverend gentleman thanked his congregation for having given him the opportunity Of going, and also thanked them for the support whictb had been accorded his colleague (the Rev. E. Downham) and the church during his absence. During the day the anthems "The ITeaVens are telling" and "0 worship the King " were excellently rendered by the choir, whilst the vesper hymn was sung to music written by the organist (Mr. E. Wilkes). A' feature of the musical side of the service was a hymn written by the Rev. E. Downham—who, with the Rev. E. thinner, assisted in the services—one verse of which deals with the war and runs as follows:— Lord Almighty, yea, Thou knowest, That our cause is just and right; Make us steadfast, make us hopeful, That we vanquish in the fight. May our spirits never languish, As our hearts we lift to Thee, Lord Almighty, Great Jehovah, Now our guide and guardian be.

Collections were made in aid of the Additional Clergy Fund.

A QUIET YEAR

At The Warrington Museum And Library.

FEWER BOOKS BORROWED.

Mr. Charles Madeley, director and librarian, in his annual report on the work of the Museum for the year ended June 30—being the sixty- seventh year since its establishment—says the year has been a quiet one; no great change has to be recorded in any department.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUSEUM.

Accessions.—The number of specimens regis- tered during the year was 1,437, which were, acquired as follows :—By purchase, 943; gift, 494.

To the Trustees of the British Museum and the Committee of the Manchester Museum we are indebted for the. gift of . a number of specimens of Mollusca needed to complete the exhibited arrangement of that group. Amongst the local specimens of natural his- tory received were an albino song thrush (pre- sente(l by Mr. G. M. Nelson), and a stoat in complete winter dress (presented by Mr. C. England). Important additions to the prehistoric section were made by the gift, through the Trustees pf the British Museum, of a collection of flint im- plements (46) from Northfleet and Swanscombe, in North Kent; and another collection (66) from French cave deposits, presented by Sir C. Hercules Read, F.S.A. Bridge Relics.—From the Bridges Committee of the Council was received a second contribu- tion of relics from the excavations for the new Bridge, including another cannon ball, a small pewter tankard, some fragments of early pot- tery, and a number of large baulks of oak, which had been framed together, and it is suggested may have formed part of the foundation of a bridge in mediwval time. Modern Antiquities.—Among the more modern antiquities added was a set of horse-bells, 21 (originally 23) in number, attached to a leather belt, with the mark of Robert Wells of Ald- bourne; a time-candle, dating from 1880 (pre- sented by Mr. E. Lovett); a 30-hour 6lock by "Robert Hampson, Warrington"; and a flint- lock pistol, with spring bayonet attached, by "Bigharn, Warrington." - The twelve halberds, formerly carried on cere- monial occasions by the burgesses of Newton-in- Makerfield, were presented by Lord Newton; and a silver cup, a prize won at Stockton Heath races in 1819, was lent by Mr. C. R. Tayleur. An interesting pewter inkstand, bearing the maker's mark and date 1676, and a box of Bat- tersea enamel, decorated with transfer prints in purple, were purchased. A fine punch bowl of Liverpool delft, painted in blue under glaze; an early English watering- pot; and some fragments of English pottery of the Norman period (the last presented by Mr. T. Leslie) were amongst the additions to the collection of English pottery. A number of pieces dating. from the XVIII. century were added to the collection of English glass, including a wine glass with decoration en- graved and gilt, a tumbler engraved with grapes and barley, some jugs and bottles of dark green and blue glass, and a small jug, dark green with opaque white enclosures.

A -Wigan token of 1657, previously unpub- lished. was presented, in continuation of many such gifts, by Mr. Nathan: Heywood; and a series of copper farthings of the Stuart Kings were purchased.

Decorative Art.—ll the department of decora- tive art, two new lines of development were opened. For the first a series of specimens (25) of lace was acquired, representing typical pat- terns of various Italian, French, Flemish, D.utch and English makes of needle and bobbin. lace., of the XVII., XVIII., and XIX. centuries.

For the second, a few, necessarily fragmen- tary, examples of illuminated MS. were acquired, being leaves of prayer-books or choir- books, exhibiting Continental work of the XV. century, together with a similar piece of English illumination of late XIV. century date. Three water-colour drawings of local scenes, the work of Mr. Oswald Garside, • were pur- chased.

Contributions to the local collections (besides those mentioned above) were made by Alder- man Bennett, Messrs. J. A. Booth, J. Bailey, R. I3urthem, Mrs. Crane, Messrs. G. A. Dunlop, J. Farrington, L. Greening, E. Hannan, W. Hewitt, T. May, G. IL Nelson, B. Pemberton, H. Potts, W. Webster, J. L. Whittle, and T. Woods.

Wildflower Table.—During the year 1,911 specimens were received from the voluntary helpers, of which 1,534 were contributed by 16 adults and 377 by 11 children. The largest con- tributors were Mr. T. Burrows (540), Mr. C. Hill (348), Mr. G. A. Dunlop (275), Mr. G. P. Miln (197), and Jennie M. Dunlop (146). Installation.—The principal piece of new work carried out by Mr. Dunlop was the preparation and display Of a series of speaimens to illustrate the characters and forms of the sub-kingdom Mollusca, which occupies three half-cases in the bird room, and completes the synopsis of the invertebrate animals. He has also devoted a considerable amount of time to the registration and cataloguing of the Kendrick and May col- lections of objects from the Boman station at Wilderspool, which work is not yet finished. The local specimens in the Hodge collection, of plants were remounted and incorporated, mak- ing an addition to the herbarium of about 450 sheets. The original labels and notes were in every case preserved. The lead spoutheads from local buildings 'were mounted on oak boards and re-arranged.

Visitors.—The number of visitors to the exhi- bition-rooms (hiring the year was 50,747, being an average of 163 per day for the 310 days on which the Museum was open. This indicates but little immediate advanci, but is well above the average of recent years. In addition to the previously-arranged visits when lectures were delivered, parties were noted visiting the Museum in charge of teachers from the following schools: School of Art (24 parties), Secondary School (3 parties), Warrington Ele- mentary Schools (9 parties from 4 schools), schools at Earlestown, Penketh and Winwick.

THE LIBRARY.

Accessions.—The number or additions to the library recorded during the year was 1,974, Which were acquired as follows: By purchase, 1,154; by gift, including 260 pamphlets and 419 broadsides, 805; by transfer from reserve, 15. After allowing for additions and withdrawals, the total stock on the Shelves on 30th June, 1915, numbered 58,586, including 17,980 pamphlets and broadsides.

The most interesting single acquisition was the "101 l of the Mock Corporation of Farn- worth in Widnes, 1714-61." This is writteii on parchment, measures 60 feet in length, and-con- tains the names of about 1,800 mayors and free- men. The tin case which formerly held the roll had been in the Museum for forty-two years. during which time the roll had been lost and supposed to ha.ve been destroyed. _

Doctor Said Hair Must Be Cut OIL

ZAM-BCK BRINGS JOY TO .ANOTIIER MOTREg.

"When playing with another child," said Mrs. E. Dryland, of 22, Station-road, Hastings, to a reporter, "my daughter Ethel, aged 31, caught ringworm, which spread so rapidly that her head was soon one mass of bald, itchy places. "A doctor told me that we must have all her hair shaved off. This greatly distressed me, for Ethel has a wealth of flaxen hair. I decided that, if it could be helped, I would not have her hair cut off.

"Seeing that the doctor's ointment was not bringing about any improvement, I remembered that I had .found Zam-Buk very soothing and healing for chilblains. So I thought I Would see if it would do the ringworms good. After only a few dressings of Zam-Buk the result Was so satisfactory that I said to myself, 'l'll stick to Zam-Buk.' The moment the balm was applied, the shocking irritation ceased, and Ethel becamb less fretful. She 'never so much as scratched the places after I applied Zam-Buk, which effectually rooted .out the ringworms, and restored her scalp to perfect health. The hair has grown splendidly all over her head. I can- not be too grateful to Zam-Buk, which alone cured her."

Zam-Buk always retains its high standard of healing efficiency as distinguished from coarse home-made salves and common ointments. Zum-Buk is the one absolutely reliable healer for family use. Zam-Buk is sold only in sealed packages by chemists and drug stores. Do not be put off with anything alleged to be "just as good."

MAGNIFICENCE OF JUPITER.

The King Among Planets— And Larger Than Them AIL

By W. F. DENNING, F.R.A.S,, ac. There are planets bright and faint, large and small, but none can compare with Jupiter in point of size. Vlien shining in the nocturnal sky he is not so resplendent as Venus, but he is incomparably larger in actual dimensions. I-10 is a king among planets. Venus may reign as the queen of beauty and be matchless when shining lustrously as the morning or the even- ing star. But the Jovian orb is larger than all, the planets put together and as a telescopic object is magnificent, for his gigantic disc ex: hibits ever changing details of very diversifieti • and attractive kind.

Jupiter rises at about 7.30 p.m. at the end of August and comes above the horizon about four minutes earlier every night. He will be splen- didly visible during the whole night in October, rising in the east part of the horizon with grandeur which will enable him to be readily' identified from the sidereal brilliants scintill- ating in the same region. Jupiter has special claims to notice. His ex- pansive surface displays a number of dusky bands or belts running parallel with his equator. A plentiful array of white and dark spots of irregUlar outlines are involved in these belts and are affected by changes of form and velocity. The planet has nine satellites, so that he is • abundantly Aupplied with moons to illuminate his nocturnal landscape and impart a picturesque variety to his skies.

The equatorial diameter of Jupiter is about ninety thousand miles, whereas the diameter of our terrestrial globe is less, titan eight thousand miles, so that the great disparity in size between the two orbs may be readily con- ceived. Notwithstanding the enormous dimen- sions of Jupiter he turns very rapidly on his axis, the length of his day being only nine hours fifty-five minutes.

While astronomers survey Jupiter with their powerful telescopes and seek for the explana- tion of his mobile lineamerlts, ordinary observers will be content to view his surface with small glasses and to trace .the motions of his four. brighter satellites discovered by Galileo in 1610.. But even with the naked eye, Jupiter and many other celestial objects present appearances of absorbing interest. His slight changes of position from night to night amongst the fixed stars may be easily traced. His brightness, his pale yellow light, the extreme steadiness of his lustre compared with the ceaseless twinkling of the stars form entertaining details in his visible! aspect. It is said that one or two of hiF.; satellites are perceptible without telescopic aid and there is strong evidence in support of that conclusion. The third satellite is very largo and, when •at its greatest apparent distance from the planet, has occasionally been detected. by persons of very acute vision. The contemplation of the heavens without in- strumental aid often affords a/charming enter- tainment. The morning or evening star, the comet, the moon successively passes through her phases, the Aurora Borealis and other ob- jects and phenomena, form pictures of' entranc- ing interest in the great art gallery of Nature. Telescopes are capable of displaying detail, but the eye alone takes in a large expanse and some- times a wide picture with its various objects contrasted may be more beautiful than tho. limited scene depicted in a glass.—" Great Thoughts."

IN TIIE COURTS.

"VERY DRUNK."

Antrobus Licensee Charued With

- Selling And Dismissed.

At Tuesday's -Petty Sessions at Stockton Heath, Rachael Bradbury, licensee of the Wheatsheaf Inn,. Antrobus, near Northwich, was summoned for having, on Sunday, Septem- ber 19th, sold intoxicating liquor to a drunken person—one Thomas Cliffe; also with having permitted drunkenness on her licensed pre- mises. Mr. W. E. Hough, solicitor, of Run- corn, prosecuted, and Mr. T. S. Steel, of Warrington, appeared on behalf of defendant. Previous to giving their evidence all witnesses were excluded from Court. Prosecution's Story.

Mr.- Hough said that on the day in question three men—Wilkinson, Brittland and Thomas Cliffe, who was mentioned specially, being the man who it was alleged had been allowed on the premises in &drunken condition—left home and went to the Wheatsheaf at 12.30, which was opening time on that day. They were each served with about four pints of. beer, and left the premises at 2.30. In the evening they again visited the house about 6.3 o—opening time—and were ;again each served with four pints of beer. Later in the evening Wilkinson and Britthind left, leaving Cliffe behind. A witness named Hindley spoke to having met Wilkinson coming away from the house after eight o'clock; and at .8.50 Cliffe was found in an extremely drunken condition about 150 yards from the Wheatsheaf. He had in his possession a bottle of whisky, and was in such a condition that he could not walk, and the police had to requisition a conveyance to take him to the lock-up at Stockton Heath. He was in the lock-up for some time. The, following day the sergeant saw the licensee and informed her that he was making inquiries about Cliffe being found drunk; she then admitted that the man had been served with one glass of beer. After a conversation with her daughter she said he had had no whisky supplied. "Might Be Suggested."

It might be suggested that the man had ob- tained the liquor elsewhere, but the nearest licensed house was about half a mile off; and judging from the condition of the man the question of his having been supplied in another place would fall, through. The licensee must have taken absolutely no precaution to prevent drunkenness taking place: William Brittland gate evidence in support of this statement, saying that Wilkinson and he left at about a quarter past nine. In the afternoon they had had an hour or two's sleep, land were quite Sober when they went at night. They did not see anything wrong with Chile when they left him; he was "strong enough to stand three or four pints." Many Witnesses.

George Wilkinson, a Whitley faim labourer, said he left Chile outside the premises. They were sober.

Herbert Bland, of Whitley Superior, said he 'went to the Wheatsheaf at 8.25 and on the way saw the man Cliffe about 90 yards away. He took him to be intoxicated.; he was fast asleep and snoring. He had no idea how long Cliffe had been therej, but he' had not been there when he carae"Past at about 7.15. He took the man to be Cliffe—he did not go and examine him, and it was dark, but he thought it was Cliffe.

Thomas Cook, an Appleton farm labourer, said he saw Cliffe at the inn and he only hact one drink. When he left. Cliffe's condition was "rather mopesed;" but "not overdone:" Cliffe went out before half-past eight. John Smith, an Antrobus farm labourer, said Cliffe had three, or, maybe, four drinks. Cliffe went out shortly after 7.30. He thought him sober. _ .

===== 0002601_19151016_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
"Saw Cliffe Very Drunk."

Joseph Hindley, of Antrobus., said that at about 8.5 at night he saw Wilkinson alone, and at nine o'clock saw Cliffe very drunk, two or three hundred yards from the Wheatsheaf. He was on the road and the police were then _moving him.

Alfred Heaton, licensee of the public-house nearest the Wheatsheaf, said his house was ten minutes' walk from that inn. Evidence Of The Police.

P.C. Fred Worth said that at about 8.50

• -

Constable Weßings and himself found Cliffs in Northwich-road, aboUt 150 yards from the Wheatsheaf, helplessly drunk. They got him up, but he could not stand. Next morning he (witness) visited defendant's house and told her he was making inquiries respecting a man named Tom Cliffe, who. had been found on the road drunk the previous night. "Yes," she replied, "he was here last night and my daughter served him with one glass of beer." He produced the bottle containing whisky alfd said: "I found this bottle on Cliffe! Do you know anything about it?" She said: "I don't recollect him being served with whisky here, but tlOs is one of my mineral water bottles." After going upstairs and conversing with her daughter she denied that whisky had been served, because they had not any. Probably the same bottles were supplied to the other public-houses and shops in the district. Constable Wellings corroborated, and men- tioned that they had carried Cliffe fifty yards before getting the conveyance. Inspector John Breese said Cliffe was brought in the station at eleven o'clock, very drunk.

" Not Proven."

Mr. Steel then submitted that there was not a solitary witness to prove that the man had been drunk on the premises, or served while drunk. The bottle of whisky he could not have got .from defendant because she had none. It was not for him to say. where the man got it or how; but the evidence showed him to have been perfectly sober when he left the house. Mr. Hough contended that there was plenty of evidence.

The Chairman (Mr. F. A. Frost) announced that the case would be dismissed. Unanswered Question.

Mr. Hough: Do I understand that you hold there is no evidence?

The Clerk (Mr. H. Greenall) : The case is dis- missed. You are not entitled to ask the reason.

DRUNKEN MAN CHARGED.

Thomas Cliffe was then charged with having been drunk on the highway. He pleaded guilty, and after the evidence. was fined ss. or seven days' imprisonment.
