LEFT BEHIND!

Uncomfortable Lot Of A Cat In A

Shop.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday morning Margaret Milner, at present residing at Whitchurch-road, Prenton, Birkenhead, was summoned for having between September 20th and 25th been cruel to a cat by causing it un- necessary suffering. Inspector Dee, of the R.S.P.C.A., said that defendant had been the tenant of a shop in Sankey-street. On the 20th of last month she fastened the shop up, leaving behind her, with- out food or water, a black and white cat. It was noticed by three people during the week, and two of them gave it milk by putting the same under the door, whilst a young butcher, employed in an adjacent shop, gave it meat. When the matter was brought to witness's notice he, along with Inspector Hesp, went into the shop and found the cat in a poor and emaciated condition, and they gave it food— which it devoured ravenously—and milk. On the following Tuesday one of the Society's Inspectors saw the defendant at Birkenhead. Leo Hesham, of 13, Pierpoint-street, deposed to seeing a card in the window of defendant's shop to the effect that the premises would be re-opened on the "Thursday morning." He also saw a cat in the shop, and be gave it meat. Put Milk Under the Door.

Iliss Flora \Macdermott, of Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath, lestified to putting the milk under the door for the cat to drink.

Inspector Wiseman, of. the R.S.P.C.A., said he interviewed the defendant at Birkenhead, and when asked about the animal she replied, "Yes; it is _my cat! I left the shop in Warrington and forgot to take it .with me. I would have gone back for it the next day, but I was taken ill." To the Bench the defendant said that the cat was a stray one that used to frequently come

into her shop, and the girls used to feed it. It was not a cat that "had been regularly with her; it was just a cat from the street." She left the shop on the Tuesday morning with the intention of coming buck, but she was not able to do so. She did not think of the cat; in fact, sometimes she never •saw it for days together. She had not the faintest intention of being un- kind to it. Defendant was fined 10s.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
"SCORES OF BLACK-EYES."

IXife's Sad Story 01 Unhappy Married

Life.

Percy Hunt, of 41, Lythgoes-lane, appeared at Tuesday's Police Court to further answer a sum- mons, issued at the instance of his wife, for aggravated assault, and to Show cause why a separation order, with alimony, should not be granted.

Mr. Arthur Browne, who appeared for the wife—Florence Hunt—said he was quite in agreement with the complainant in her refusal to go back to her husband. She had had dozens of black eyes during her married life; in fact, she had two black eyes last Christmas alone, and another one at Easter, whilst her husband assaulted her every Saturday night. Asked whether he had anything to say why his wife should not be granted a separation, the defendant said that his wife had a good home. Going into the witness-box, the wife said that she had had scores of black eyes, not dozens. She had helped to keep the home going by tak- ing in washing, by starching and ironing and by fustian cutting. Her husband had told her many a time that he would murder her, and she was afraid to live with him. Corroborative evidence having been given, the husband said that he was very sorry for what had happened. His wife and he had lived com- fortably together, barring the occasion on which the present proceedings were based. For years before that they had "never had a word." She had "got in with some people," and she was "siding with them" instead of with him. The wife was granted a separation order— with alimony at the rate . of Bs. a week—and 28s. 6d. costs.

"COST RATEPAYERS POUNDS."

Chief Constable's Strong Criticism Of ‘l4 a rri ng ton Family.

At Wednesday's Police Court, there were on the Bench .Messrs. J. W. Hayward, C. Jackson Holmes and W. T. Williams. Mary Harris, of 19, Beswick-street, was summoned for having assaulted Minnie Webster, 17, Beswick-street, on Monday evening. The evidence was that one of complainant's chickens flew over from her yard into defend- ant's yard. Webster sent her little girl for it, but as defendant would not return it she her- self went to bring it. Defendant seized. her. by the hair and thumped her in the eye. The Chief Constable said Harris was a terror to he neighbourhood, and the people who lived round about had absolutely no rest at all. She had a shocking record; having been up on 23 occasions---frequently for neglect of children. Being sent to a home seemed to have done her no good at all. The language she used was the *filthiest imaginable. It was really a shocking case. The whole-of the family was thoroughly bad; they had cost the ratepayers pounds and pounds, and it was to be hoped their Worships would make an example of her. She was sent to prison for three months,

MUNITIONS COURT.

Why Workers Left Their Jobs—

And Must Go Back !

At a sitting of the local Munitions Tribunal, held at the Labour Exchange on Monday afternoon—before Mr. T. J. Ridgway (Presi- dent), assisted by two assessors (Mr. J. Dolan, for the employers, and Mr. A. Bennett for the men)—a quartet of workers applied for certifi- cates sanctioning changes they have made in their places of employment.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
PREFERRED LABOURING TO SKILLED WORK.

The first man had been employed in the pro- duction of electrical copper for war purposes, and had been receiving 61d. per hour with 2s. 6d. war bonus. He desired to have his certificate granted him in order that he might go labouring at 7d. per hour. Questioned as to .why he wished to leave ex- pert work to go to ordinary labouring work, he replied "I think I can get more wages." Ile had not been satisfied with the work he had been doing, or with the pay, for six months past; he contended that the job tvas worth more, and it was in order to get more that he left. "You knew you were engaged on work which was helping to supply the fellows in the trenches with material with which to fight, and yet you were so wanting in that patriotic feeling which is so necessary nowadays that you go to a near- by works to get three farthings an hour extra?" This question was put to the worker by a repre- sentative of the firm at whose instance he was brought before the Tribunal, and, in reply, the man said: "My chief reason for wanting to 4 leave was that I did not consider I was getting a living wage where I was. My average wage was 335." HUNDREDS OF MEN WANTED.

The firm's representative said they were "very bothered for men "; in fact, they wanted hundreds. They were scheduled as a contract company, and were making copper as hard as they could for the Government. The employes not only got a war bonus, but each man who stuck to his work got a £5 War Loan Certificate. He had no complaint as to the man's work, and the one fact was that he could not be spared at the present juncture. Could he bring another man as capable in his place they would not complain of his going away to better himself, as he thought. The man's certificate was refused.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
MACHINES FOR TURNING OUT TOMMY'S" BISCUITS.

The second case was also that of a man who applied for his certificate, and he said that he left the job he had at Earlestown for the pur- pose of obtaining employment on munitions work elsewhere. Ile had been previously employed at 31s. a week, with a 3s. war bonus, on the construction of biscuit-making machines —machines that were actually used for the manufacturing of biscuits for the men at the Front.

Again the Certificate sought was refused, as were also the certificates of two other young men employed on similar work, viz., the con- struction of biscuit:making machines: It was stated in evidence that the biscuits turned out by these particular machines were wanted for the _soldiers of one of our Allies.

The men's excuse—w-a-s-thii-they were discon- tented as to the rates of pay ruling in the shop; some men got time and a quarter and others were getting just the same as before the war.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FRIENDLY SOCIETIES.

Shepherds Decide To Open A Past Masters' Lodge.

The Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds (A.U.), Warrington district, held their quarterly meet- ing on Saturday at the Corporation Inn, War- rington. The Prov. Chief Shepherd, Bro. Henry Johnson, presided, and was supported by the Deputy Chief, Bro. James Hough; P.P.C.S. Jas. Parr, District Tgeasurer ; P.P.C.S. Peter L. Edgar, District Secretary; District Trustee Bro. W. E. Clegg, District Trustee Bro. H. Rough- sedge, P.P.C.S. Francis Taylor, P.P.C.S. Jos. Ashton, P.P.C.S. Jos. W. Pomfrey, P.P.C.S. Jos: Adams, P.P.C.S. J. Haley, and P.P.C.S. F. S. Massey.

The Secretary presented the accounts, which, after the auditors' report, were accepted. Bro. Jas. Lowe was re-elected auditor for the follow- ing six months.

It was reported that the juvenile section had made steady progress.

It was deciciedTto open a Past Masters' Lodge in the district, and the District Officers were authorised to call a meeting of Past Masters from all the lodges in the district for this purpose.

Presentations.

Two presentations were made to Brothers for services rendered to the district. P.P.C.S. Bro. W. E. Clegg, on behalf of the district, presented a handsome clock to P.P.C.S. Bro. Francis Taylor for his services as Prov. Chief Shepherd during 1914-15. The iiresentation was warmly supported by many of the brethren, and Bro. Francis Taylor very suitably replied. Bro. J. W. Pomfrey, on behalf of the district, presented Bro. W. E. Clegg with a Past Chief's badge for services rendered as immediate Past Chief, font which Bro. Clegg returned thanks.

CHESS.

First Meeting Of The Warrington

Chess Club Season.

The first Meeting of the season of the War- rington Chess Club was held in the Club Rooms, Prince's Cafe, Sankey-street, on Thurs- day night, When the following • officers. were appointed for the ensuing season :—President, Mr. F. Dunne; vice-presidents, Messrs. J. New- combe and E. J. Hunt; hon. secretary and treasurer, Mr. H. C. Westmoreland. The following resolution was passed 'We learn with regret of the death of one of the oldest members of the club, tXe late Mr. J. R. Wood, which occurred shortly after the adjournment of the club last season, and it •is desired to express deepest sympathy with Mrs. Wood, and to place on record the high esteem in which be was held by his fellow-members." The Secretary was instructed- to forward a copy of this resolution to Mrs. Wood. • It was decided to hold the usual competition for the handsome "Silver King" trophy be- longing to the duly, and also other competi- tions on handicap lines, for which prizes are given. • A warm welcome is extended to all chess players who would care to visit the club, which meets from seven o'clock to 11 o'clock every Thursday evening. Young players and novices are especially invited.

ASSOCIATION.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

WARRINGTON CENTRAL v. PADDINGTON

The above teams met on Saturday last in Victoria Park; in the above competition, Several alterations were made in the selection of the teams from the previous week, and the following players turned out under the control of Referee J. Wright. Central.—Ashcroft; Millward and Glover; O'Neil, Patter and Woods; Warburton, Mason, Hughes, Hands and Mason. Paddington.—Horrocks; Holmes and Hr- rocks; Marsh, Ditchfield and Pritchard; Taylor, Derbyshire, Rothwell, Meachin and Warren. The Central won the toss and decided to play with the slope in their favour. Rothwell set the ball rolling, and the opening exchanges were of an even character. At the end of five minutes' play Rothwell received a good pass from Derbyshire and secured the lead for the Paddingtonians. Some even play followed until by long swinging passes the Central got into the visitors' goal area. Hands receiving when well placed, equalised the score at the end of fifteen minutes' play. From now a more strenuous game was witnessed, and the ball travelled quickly from end to end. The Central gained a slight advantage, Hughes scored from an offside position and the point was dis- allowed. The visitors now changed the scene of play and Ditchfield tested Ashcroft with a long shot, which was safely cleared. The interval arrived with the score one goal each. The visitors were soon attacking at the Central goal in the second half. Ashcroft and his backs were found plenty of work. Rothwell was always a source of danger to thecdefence, and on one occasion he gave the home custodian a warm shot to deal with, and then Derbyshire was just on the wrong side of the post with a good effort. The Central broke away and made an attempt on the visitors' goal, but the final efforts of Mason were very weak and Horrocks cleared easily. , From the goal-kick some good passing movements were indulged in by the visitors, and Derbyshire succeeded in gaining the lead when in a doubtful position. A strong appeal for offside was disallowed by the referee. The game still continued to be in the visitors' favour and Ditchfield was prominent in break- ing up any attempt by the homesters. He was always striving to lower the home citadel, and a splendid effort from thirty yards' range just skimmed over the bar. The home team re- arranged their players and improved their dis- play, but failed to make any impression on the visitors' defence. What shots reached Horrocks were easily cleared, and at the call of time the game was in favour of Paddington. who ran out deserving winners as follows :—Paddington, two goals; Warrington Central, one goal.

ST. ELPHIN'S v. MERSEY JUNIORS.

Directly after Boyle put the " Saints " further ahead by heading a splendid goal from a corner-kick. The ,J uniors did not relish these reverses, and improved in their game. HOWLEY JUNIORS v. STOCKTON HEATH

SCOUTS.

These teams met in the Victoria Park on Saturday in the above competition. The Juniors set the ball rolling and during the first fifteen minutes the game was evenly con- tested, after which the Scouts began to have the best of the exchanges. The Howleyites could not hold the Scouts in check, Farnell opening the score with a good shot. A second goal was obtained by W. Smith, and at the interval the score was 2-0 in favour of the Scouts. The second half proved to be more in favour of the Scouts, and the Howley forwards only got to their opponents' end on rare occa- sions and were easily driven back. Although the Scouts were often attacking the Juniors' goal they failed to pierce their defence again, and at the call of time the score was :—Stock- ton Heath Scouts, 2 goals; Howley Juniors, nil.

FAIRFIELD JUNIORS v. PADGATE C.L.B.

FRIENDLY GAME.

ELMWOOD CONGREC;ATIONAL V. PENKETH ALBIONS.

The above teams met on the King and Queen ground, before a moderate attendance of sup:

porters. The home club were not able to put the best eleven in the field, and had to play five reserves. The visitors won the toss and soon got into their game and had the best of exchanges. The home defence were quickly called upon to clear their lines, but the visitors were not to be denied, and in their next assault on the home goal their inside-left registered the first point. In a few minutes this was added to, the visitors pressing hard. After these reverses the home team began to assert themselves more and were , successful in making the scores equal, Oxley scoring both goals. The interval arrived with the score 2 goals each. On resuming the home forwards went off with a dash and made tracks for the visitors' goal, and their defenders were again defeated by Oxley. After this reverse the visitors retaliated in spirited fashion, and after a few minutes' play the scores were again equal. Following a spell of midfield play the visitors gradually gained the upper hand and kept the home team on the defensive. They were successful in scoring two more goals, and at the call of time they were deserving winners. Result: Penketh Albion, 5 goals; Elmwood Congregational, 3 goals.

PAROCHIAL BEAT RYLANDS'S REC.

Rylands's Rec. met the Parochial Old Boys' team and an exciting game ended in a win for the Old Boys. Rylands's won the toss and Mathews set the ball moving. The pace was quick and the spectators enjoyed a fast game After a series of scrummages Parochial were awarded a free- kick, and, Wilcocks taking the kick at goal from half-way, the ball bounced twice on the cross-bar and rebounded into play. Latimer made a good run for Rylands's, but danger was averted. Brown made a good run and gave to Broadhurst, who ran well, and a try seemed certain, but he was well tackled by Archer, the Rec.'s full-back. Play was then brought to mid-field, and after a rough five minutes Broadhurst, the visitors' wing man, brae away and dived for the line and was no more than three inches short. Play became faster than ever, and the Parochial lads flung the ball from left to right in fine style, but the whistle went for the interval, and the score sheet was blank.

Dumbell started the second half for Rylands's and both sides set well into the game. After ten minutes' play in mid-field the game went in favour of the Parochial, who played smartly, passing from left to right and fairly baffling the home team backs. A good piece of work was done by Curtis, Robinson and Brown. The first player handled the ball three times and then gave to Brown, who beat man after man andocored a good try round the post. The same player added the goal points. With a five points' lead the Old Boys still kept up the attack, but after a short time Archer was forced to take the ball over his own line and a scrummage took place, in which the home side sent danger to mid-field. Latimer again broke away for Rylands's, but was cleverly tackled by Robinson. T. W. Cook broke away time after time, and when going for the line was beaten and brought down by Archer and Hill, who proved to be faster than the Old Boys' centre. Archer again saved his line from dan- ger, but not for long, as the Old Boys took up the attack and the forwards proved to be too good for the home side. A bit of good work by ended in that player scoring an- other try, but Mathews failed to add the goal points. Rylands's were -awarded a free-kick, and Archer kicked a splendid goal for his side.

When the final whistle went the Old Boys were in high spirits, having made their fourth successive win, taking the honours by eight points to two. Rylands's were short of their two regular centres—Tatcliff and Brady—while the Old Boys were short of Brease. In the first half the Rec. continued to control. the scrummages, but the centres were poor, time after time the passing breaking down when ,it left the half- backs. In the second half, when the Old Boys began to make it warm for the Rec., the home team continued to defend well. Archer, Dumbell, Latimer, Sutton and Hill were the pick of Rylands's; while Brown, Mal- kin, Broadhurst, Mathews and Curtis stood out in the limelight of the Old Boys' side. Teams :

Parochial.—H. Challinor; W. Broadhurst. T. Cook, B. Brown (captain). A. Walker; T. Rob- inson, R. Wileocks; W. Mathews, A. Roberts, S. Stretford, J. Litherland, P. Gibbs, H. Curtis.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
SACRED HEART v. ST. JAMES'.

• These teams met at Wilderspool. St. James' kicked off. The Hearts' line was hard pressed, but Clarke cleared with a long kick down the field. There were fine passing movements by both teams., and Baker had hard lines for a try, he having received the ball a moment too late. Sacred Heart were penalised, and Higham kicked a goal. Play was carried to the St. James' line, and Leah, the Hearts' captain, in- tercepted and scored. Half-time: Sacred Heart, 3 points; St. James', 2 points. On resuming St. James' kept the Hearts penned up for a period, but shortly after the Hearts' left wing ought to have scored, but instead of hugging the touch-line he doubled in and met the St. James' back coming across, and Green made no mistake with the tackle. The second half was well advanced before Heesom put St. James' in front with a fine dropped goal. Shortly after both Higham and Owen were almost over, each effort ending inches short. The last few minutes were spent about midfield, neither side being able to make fitly progress. Result: St. James', 4 points; Sacred Heart, 3 points. Farrington, Leah and Clarke played good games for Sacred Heart, while Higham and Green were the pick of their opponents. The members and supporters of St. James' send their deepest sympathy to Mr. and Mrs. Heesom. whose son William has died of wounds obtained whilst playing in the big game.

EMMANUEL INSTITUTE v. WIDNES ATHLETIC.

Played at Widnes. Emmanuel won the, toss and played with the wind. • Both sides opened well. The visitors soon pressed, and Booth tested the Athletic goalkeeper, who cleared .finely. Parr, for the visitors, was playing a good game, checking the home left wing time after time. The home backs were kicking well and feeding the forwards with splendid judgment. Knight, the visiting goalkeeper, had several warm handfuls to deal with, but he was equal to each occasion. At half-time neither side had scored.

On resuming the home team was put on the defensive, until a dead ball brotight relief. Emmanuel backs were playing well, keeping the home forwards well in hand. The game now became somewhat rough, and after 20 minutes' play, owing to a misunderstanding between the visiting backs, the home team opened the scor- ing. The game became more spirited, and Emmanuel tried hard to equalise. Booth once broke clean through the defence, and with a fast shot almost equalised, the goalkeeper saving in the nick of time. From a breakaway on the home left, the home team again scored, the centre-forward doirig the needful. A few min- ute§ from the close the home inside right scored a third. Emmanuel's first defeat of the season was thus inflicted by three goals to nil. For the winners the centre-forward and centre-half played a sound game, and for the losers Parr, Hardy, S. Hughes and Crozier played well.

WITH THE WOODS.

BOWLING FOR WAR PRISONERS.

Works' Association's Second Handicap Raises £2l.

The second War Fund handicap in connection with the Warrington Works Amateur Bowling Association came to a close on Saturday after- noon, the final games being played on the well- kept green of the Horse and Jockey Hotel, Win- wick-road, in the presence of a goodly company- of devotees. As a result of the handicap the- sum of twenty guineas has been handed over to the local Prisoners of War Fund. The first of the War Fund handicaps, it will be remembered, realised £3O. As on the previous occasion, the proceeds of the second handicap were practically entirely derived from the entrance fees of the competitors.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0275.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTING THE CHEQUE.

At the close of the games Mr. J. Willett handed to the Mayor (Alderman Peter Pea- cock) the cheque for .41, after which His Worship proposed a vote of thanks to the committee and officials for the efforts_ they had put forth to raise so creditable an amount. In these days of war it was gratifying to know that those who could not join the Army or Navy, but who, were working on munitions in Warrington, united in their leisure time in such an event as that, devoting their entrance fees to the benefit of the South Lancashires' prisoners of war in Germasy—gallant fellows who were most deserving of the help which it was sought to render them.

The Afayor's proposition was seconded by Mr. W. Webster and was carried with applause, after which the Mayoress (Mrs. Peter Peacock) presented the prizes, being accorded a vote of thanks for her services, on the proposition of Mr. J. Willett, seconded by Mr. F. Dean, the latter referring to the fact that the donor of the first prize (Lady Greenall) was unable to be present as she was out in France "doing her bit." Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart., and Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., had hoped to be present, but they wrote from London regretting that they were unavoidably compelled to absent them. selves.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0276.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL GROUND ASSESSMENT,

A Temporary Reduction.

Mr. T. Pemberton (hon. treasurer) and Mr. T. P. Bradshaw (hon. sec.) appeared before the last meeting of the Warrington Assessment Committee, over which Mr. W. Furness pre- sided, to appeal against the assessment of the ground now owned by the Warrington Football Club at Wilderspool. It was represented that as professional foot- ball had been suspended during the war, the income would be greatly diminished, and the assessment was excessive.

Mr. Pemberton stated that thirty players had enlisted, and on account of the war and of poor public support they had been compelled to close down. No professional football was now played, but the ground was used for schoolboy and intermediate competitions. This year the total receipts amounted to only £42 15s. The conditions were practically those of an empty house.

Mr. Bradshaw added that the ground was frequently placed at the disposal of the military free of charge. No man of military age was allowed to play football. Only three clubs in the Northern Union had stopped playing, and Warrington was one of that number. There were certain expenses, and the gate receipts were now nominal.

Mr. Pemberton thought the committee would have wiped the assessment out altogether until the war was over. . - - -

The Chairman said that would be impossible.

The present assessment was as follows :—Gross estimated rental, £198; rateable value, £l5B 10s. It was unanimously deuided to reduce to the following :—£so gross and £42 10s. rateable.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0277.txt | included=true | included =====
Who Was Jack Robinson?

Jack Robinson has long been a favourite synonym for rapidity of speech or action, but possibly few people who use the phrase are aware that Jack Robinson was a real, live per- son. "As a politician," says Lady Dorothy Nevill in her Reminiscences, "John Robinson was a great favourite with George 111. His political career was long, for he was a member for Harwich durifig 26 years, being on one occasion bitterly attacked by Sheridan, who, denouncing bribery and its instigators, replied to the cries of ' Name ! Name!' by pointing to Robinson on the Treasury Bench, exclaiming at the same time, 'Yes. I could name him as soon as I could say Jack Robinson,' and thus origin- ated the saying still current at the present day."

L.-CORP. MAYERS, D.C.M.

Confirmation Of Last Week's

" Examiner " Article.

Last week we announced that Lance-Corporal Samuel Mayers, whose photograph we repro- duced, had written home to his parents, who reside at 80, Orford-avenue, stating that he had been awarded the D.C.M. for conspicuous bravery in carrying out his duty as a member of the 173rd Company of the Royal Engineers. The official list, from the "Gazette," appeared in the daily newspapers of Monday and con- tained the names of over 50 warrant officers, non-commissioned officers and men with respect to whom the King had been pleased to approve of the award of the Distinguished. Conduct Medal for acts of gallantry and devotion to duty whilst serving with the Expeditionary Force in France and Flanders.

In this honours list appeared the following : Mayers, Lance-Corporal S., 86428, 173rd Tunnel- ling Company, R.E. At Cambrin, when under a heavy fire, he succeeded in placing in an underground gallery occupied by the Germans a bag of blastine in such a position that a charge could be fired, thereby rendering possible the

success of the operations in progress. This bears out the version we gave last week of the act which won for Lance-Corpl. Mayers this distinction. A large number of the awards, it is interesting to note, have been made for mining and tunnelling exploits.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0288.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL MAYERS MARRIED.

Unfortunately we stated that Lance-Corporal Mayers was a single-man. He is married and has one little daughter. His wife before mar- riage was Miss Frances Crookshanks, her father being a well-known local pianist. Perhaps. the mistake occurred owing to the news being sent to his mother and sister in Warrington, while his wife is at present livifig with her parents in Manchester. Lance-Corporal Mayers, who is 29 years of age, and has been married eight years, is an old scholar of Silver-street Day School. For some years he attended the Golborne-stireet Baptist Sunday School, and eventually became a member of the church. Originally he was in the employ of tit° Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., for whom his father has worked for many years.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0289.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAMMAR SCHOOL

AND SECONDARY SCHOOL.

What Says Councillor Henry

Roberts ?

THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

As this is about the time of the year when this school reopens, we take upon ourselves the duty of emphasising the responsibility that rests upon Christian parents to be careful to see that they select schools for their children where they will have security that they will be instructed in the elements of the Christian religion. Church parents especially are under a clear obligation in the matter, as the Church makes it part of their duty in bringing their children to be baptised, to see that they are taught the Creed, the Lord's Prayer and the Ten Commandments, and are able also to answer the other questions that in the short Catechism are contained."

. The ordinary Municipal Secondary School cannot Offer them any such security; indeed, the Act of 1902 (Section 4) makes it almost im- possible that they should do. They can make, a profession of giving religious instruction, and that is all. They cannot even have any exam- ination in the teaching they profess ta give. The result 'is that they become purely secular schools; and as we have seen what has. hap- pened to a nation (Germany) brought up under such influences, parents must see to it that, as earnest and consistent Christians, they make the choice of a school for their children n matter.more of 'conscience than they have been wont to do in the past,-1191y Trinity Parish Magazine for October.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
"LIT. AND PHIL." SOCIETY.

Opening Meeting Of The Winter Session.

The opening meeting of the winter session in connection with the Warrington Literary and Philosophical Society was held at the Town Hall on Thursday evening, when there was a moderate assembly of members. The President (the Rev. H. V. Pigot) was in the chair, and in the course of a few opening remarks he called attention to the attractive programme that had been arranged for the session just entered upon. Next Mr. Pigot re- ferred to the Council's decision to affiliate the society to the British Association for the Advancement of Science and said that they had sent their Secretary (Dr. J. S. Manson) as a delegate to the Manchester Conference of /the British Association.

THE GYMKHANA.

Official Thanks For Those Who Made Rally A Success.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—On behalf of Colonel Moggridge and Lieutenant-Colonel Mocatta will you allow us, through your columns, to thank all those who helped us to make the military rally last Satur- day a tuccess ? To the Football Club we havo already tendered -our thanks, but we desire to express in addition our indebtedness to the Mayor and to the Mayoress, to the Tramway Committee and the Tramway Manager, who rendered valuable assistance, and also to the Volunteer Defence Corps, Mr. 'Newton and the Boy Scouts, Officers and men of the Church Lads' Brigade, and to the officers and men of the Cadet Corps, whose assistance contributed materially to the success of our efforts. We cannot conclude this letter without making special mention of the ladies both of the Liberal and Conservative Associations, and of Mrs. B. Fairclough's committee, whose assistance in the matter of selling tickets saved us from the loss in which the bad weather would almost inevitably have involved us. To the kindness of the Conservative Association and Messrs. Jos. grosfield and Sons we are indebted for the use of torches. - _ -

May we also take this opportunity of thank- ing those who assisted at the Church Parade on Sunday, and express our special appreciation of the services of Mr. Darby and the Parish Church Choir?

BOBT. M. MATHAMS, ERIC FAIRCLOUGH, Commanding Administrative Ceintre, 4th South Lancs. Regt.

We should be very grateful to you if you will help us to make public our indebtedness to the Warrington Football Club, to whose Secretary we have written as follows :

"On behalf of Colonel Moggridge and Lieut.- Colonel Mocatta and of the troops who are now stationed at the Barracks, the Drill Hall and at Weeton Camp, we desire to thank your com- mittee most sincerely for all they did to make the Military Gymkhana so successful. It was good of you to lend the ground, and for that we are grateful, but without the help of your committee in making the arrangements and seeing them properly carried out on the day we could not 'possibly have succeeded in carrying the sports through so smoothly and ex- peditiously. The arrangements of the committee were perfect, and I am sure that if they had had the power to ordain the weather that also would have been included in their arrangement for the day. Quite apart from the patriotic purpose of the gymkhana, I am sure your com- mittee must be gratified to know that their efforts were instrumental in affording great pleasure and enjoyment not merely to those soldiers who took part, but to those who looked on."—We are, Sir, yours faithfully,. ERIC FAIRCLOUGH, ROBT. M. MATHAMS,

===== 0002601_19151016_art0293.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTARY WAR WORK.

Centralisation To Prevent Waste And

Overlapping.

'The Army Council has 'decided, in order to secure proper direction and application of the great volume of work so freely rendered by voluntary associations throughout the country, and to avoid overlapping with its Consequent waste, that a system of central direction shall be established. With this object Colonel Sir Edward Ward has been appointed Director- General of Voluntary Organisations. Under the scheme an association for the borough would be established, with depots working under representative committees in conjunction with the Central Office. The needs of the War Office would be communicated• to the head association, who in turn would com- municate them to the depot. Each association 'must arrange a system of inspection to insure that all articles strictly comply with the War Office requirements. The association and the local branches will be officially recognised and entitled to use. the words "approved by the War Office," and this recognition will also entitle them to free con: veyance of the completed articles to the destin- ation directed by the DirectorlGeneral. Fin- ished articles will be delivered by groups to the central depot and delivered free of charge to their destination.

Badges will be issued to all voluntary workers who are registered as such in a joint issocia- tion or a local branch. A badge signifies that the wearer is a regular voluntary worker under the Director-General of Voluntary Organisa- tions, and has rendered service to the country for a period of three months. It is returnable in the event of the holder ceasing to perform the duties.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0294.txt | included=true | included =====
Conference Convened By The Mayor.

His Worship the Mayor has convened a Con- ference of those concerned to discuss the mat- ter, particulars of which will be found in another column. The Mayor is informed that mufflers and mittens of the War Office patterns are the articles that are most urgently needed at the present time.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0295.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans' Colossal Cheek.

"The Germans take the biscuit for colossal cheek. They keep on firing till the last minute and then scream for mercy. Imagine a chap telling you he's married after you've come through hell to get at him!' That's what one chap did—" Me married." Several times during the bombardment Germans crawled to our lines and gave themselves up. It would be money for nothing if we had enough men. . . . My wound is in the neck, just at the bottom. I thought that big iron ball in Howard's yard had hit me. It's hard luck being kept so far South; but I think I'll stop and see next year's Derby run."

===== 0002601_19151016_art0296.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED.

RIFLEMAN H. J. WELCH.

Tells How Germans "Put The Gas Into Us."

Rifleman H. J. Welch, a member of the King's Royal `Rifles, was wounded on 'September 25th, and is now progressing favourably, in hospital at Eastleigh, Hants. Mr. R. W. Welch, of 333, Lovely-lane, uncle of Rifleman Welch, and with whom he lived before the war, has received the following letter from his nephew :—" I was wounded and blown up by a shell and also gassed on Satur- day, September 25th. . . • When we attacked we managed to get right up to the Germans. We were in front of the bench, and they put gas into us. I lay at the back of our trench when a shell burst at the side of me, wounding me in the back. I am bringing no souvenirs back except myself." ' Rifleman Welch, who is eighteen years of. age, attended, as a boy, the People's College. He enlisted in September, 1914, having been pre- viously employed by Mr. T. A. Proud, accountant, Sasnkey-street. He has been at the Front since- Jartuary.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0297.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE AV. WILD.

Old National School Boy Wounded Through .Irm-Muscle.

To his parents at 7, Fairclough's-avenue, Howley, Private W. Wild, of the Seaforth High- landers, writes that a piece of shell has passed through the muscle of hi left arm.' "I ex- pect," he says, "you will have read about the British attack. Well, I was in the first line, in a very prominent place, and got through with flying colours. We had got about a mile and half, but after I had got hit I thought we had gone three, as my arm. got too heavy to carry." Aged • 22, the wounded Private was born at Warrington, and as a lad attended the National Schools, under the headmastership of the late Mr. John.Dewhurst. Later he was a tanner for Messrs. Wm. Reynolds and Co., and attended St. Clement's Mission. A month after the war outbreak he enlisted.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0298.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE J. WOODS.

Son Of Father Also Serving Gets Hit.•

Private J. Woods, who in peace times lives with his uncle and aunt at 142, Gorsey-lane, and who is a member of the King's Own Scottish Borderers, was wounded during the Great Advance, and is now progressing favourably at the Brook War Hospital, near Woolwich.

Private Woods •was educated at the National School, Church-street. Before the outbreak of war he was employed by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd. He joined the Army in September of last year. His father belongs to the National Reserve.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0299.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE CHARLES RUSHTON.

Long-Record Soldier Wounded For

Second Time.

That Private Charles Rushton, of the 2nd South.Lancashires, had been again wounded, the news was received in a field card by his wife at 6, Howley-lane. This stated that he was wountled in action on September 25th. Two later letters from him show that. the wound is in his left arm, and be is doing nicely at the Preston Hall Military Hospital, Alby, Kent. Aged 42, Private Rushton is a native of War- rington, and an ex-scholar of St. Mary's Roman Catholic School. At the age of 18 he enlisted in the 2nd South Lancashires, in which he re- mained 14 years, attaining the rank of sergeant. Then followed four years in the Special Reserve, during which he was a wire-worker at Messrs. Rylands's, and attended St. Mary's Church. On the war outbreak lie was called up, and just over a week later went out to France. Previously. he had been wounded in the right shoulder and left leg. He has three children. It is interesting to note that he served ten years in India.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0300.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

WAR-FLAVOURED 'DRAMA AT THE ROYAL COURT.

"Somewhere a Voice is Calling !" is a drama that makes a decided appeal to the emotions of the average theatre audience, and for two reasons. Firstly it has a war flavour, and secondly it tells a straightforward love story -- a story of that " heroinic " devotion which never fails to win plaudits when it is told from the other side of the footlights. The piece is well-written—two facile pens collaborated in its production—and it is also well acted, every character in the cast receiving adequate in- terpretation at the hands of Air. Joseph Millane's company. Mr. Norman Partreige enacts most convincingly the role of the naval lieutenant who is prevented by war duty from carrying out his obligation to the trusting girl who loved him, and whom he *loves—and who subsequently is made his happy wife—whilst Miss Mabel Rose is a charming and s:ympa- thetic heroine, investing her part with much

pathos and winning plaudits at every house. Mr. Alfred Stretton, as Simon Quester, is a villain whom the " gods " delight to hiss, his portrayal of the role of a scheming usurer being extremely well done. Miss Ann Welfitt plays up to him cleverly as his fellow conspirator in the endeavour to wreck the life of the gallant lieutenant; indeed, she is quite one of the most convincing " villainesses " we have " struck " in a long experience of melodrama. All the other parts in the cast are most capably taken, special praise being, perhaps, due to Mr. J. Milne Taylor—who enacts the part of the wronged-and-subsequently-righted girl's father —Miss Nellie Taylor—as her mother—and Miss Maisie 'Weedon as the youthful offspring .of the alliance whose ingenuous efforts result in bring- ing about the reunion of her father and mother. Irrepressible Revile Again !

Patrons of the Royal Court Theatre are next week advised to have some sense and go and see "Ave Some Sense," an Anglo-Oriental musical comedy revue in three scenes, which will be played by an all-British company of known artistes. A real treat is promised, the piece abounding in fun and merry tunes, to say nuthing of the scenery and costumes which are on the lavish scale. The cast includes Sydney 'Bland, Tom E. Ray, Lydia Lee, Rose- mary Lee, the Oolah Girls, Lettie Stone's troupe of dancers, and a whole host of other funny and Charming people.

===== 0002601_19151016_art0301.txt | included=true | included =====
TIP-TOP BILL AT HIPPODROME.

This week's programme at the Hippodrome is, in the vernacular, "just great" ! Crowded "houses" have been duly appreciative. A pair of fine comedians, who style themselves the Two Anartos, top the bill, and, whilst they are "on," keep the audience in roars of laughter; indeed, they are farce personified! Cooper Mitchell, another " is a most ver- satile artiste, for he can sing, tell a good story and mimic—and do all ,three things equally well! He concludes his excellent "show" by rendering a monologue of his own composition and entitled "Our Debt of Honour to Bel- gium." A dramatic-comedy With a most un- expected denouement and entitled The Awakening," is, most decidedly, one of the hits of the programme and calls forth loud and prolonged applause. In the playlet the hus- band dreams that he doubts his wife's honesty and that he shoots his best friend and strangles her—and then he wakes after his nerve- wracking experiences to find that it was only a nightmare. The Ryewodes Trio—the Misses Florrie and Winnie ard Master Tom—present a refined, applause-compelling and tuneful scena, entitled "Music Awakes!" which captivates all listeners, and James Farrow and Zonny Fiske give an admirable eccentric-comedy show abounding in fun. Some so-called comedy " turns " don't! Eric Clifford, who hails from Australia, sings character and chorus songs with great verve, and Lily Flexmore dances gracefully. The Gaumont Graphic goes to complete a most excellent entertainment, the pictures included showing the London Scottish Regiment holding a sports meeting, and the funeral of the late Mr. George Edwardes. • The Red Heads Next Week.

At the Hippodrome next week the manage- ment will (by arrangement with Sidney Blow and Will Collins) present the revue entitled "Red Heads," a piece which is anticipated to meet with the general verdict of being one of the best things seen by a Warrington audience. It is a revue with a reputation, for it has

===== 0002601_19151016_art0302.txt | included=true | included =====
HENRY ADNES.

met with instant success everywhere. There is beautiful singing, charming dancing and stage effects, and a humour which never flags run- ning throughout. The cast includes Mr. Henry Adnes, a clever, capable and versatile per- former whose visit will be welcomed by all lovers of the dramatic art; Robert Layton, Nell Carter, Elsa Sinclair, Ivy Shepherd, and Hanna Hart, supported by the "Red Headed Beauty Chorus."

One of the attractions is a grand dress parade, introducing creations by Madame Ella Burgess, which are announced as the costliest dresses on the stage.

KINETOPHONE AGAIN VISITS THE KINEMA.

During this first week-half at the popular Star Kinema the programme was topped by a welcome return visit of the Edison Kinetophone —speaking pictures. Taking the form of a variety entertainment the film featured a choir of blacksmiths, who sang to the ringing of their hammers. Amateur burlesque theatricals were indulged in by the Nursery Players, and a little ballad, "Shamrocks of Ireland," scene, con- cluded an enjoyable performance. On the pro- gramme other items included "The Cycle Of Hatred," a clever melodrama with a strong love theme. " Pathe's Animated Gazette" gave the latest news from all parts, including German prisoners marching through Southampton. "The Valley of Humiliation" proved a gripping drama of modern life; and a fine scenic, "The Gulf of Togulio," followed. Those celebrated laughter-makers. Fatty and Mabel, raised roars of laughter in Keystone Comedy. During to-clay attractive bills of fare include "The Lion's Cubs," a film which has earned much praise from those who have seen it. Next Week's " Bid Hits."

Star patrons are to have the opportunity next week of seeing two particularly fine films—" The Heart of a Child" on the first three nights, and "The Shulamite " on the second three.

Cage Bird Society's Annual Show. The Warringon and District Cage Bird Society held their twentieth/ annual show in the Cairo- street Schools yesterday. Although not so numerous as in former years, some very fing- birds were staged; the entries this year total- ling 230, as against 490 last year. The judges were Messrs. J. D. Leigh, G. Large and H. W. Bathye. The exhibition will be continued throughout to-day, and will close this evening.

make the sandbags fly in all directions, but still we held on to their trenches. Eventually I and two comrades found ourselves in a Jack John- son' hole half-filled with water. Snipers kept peppering away all round, so we had to stop there for hours, and to make matters worse, it began to rain heavily. . , . But thank God I am alive to tell the tale." Private Rewles, who is twenty-eight years of age, was educated at Ileathside School. Before joining the Territorials in October, he had been employed in the patent house at the Whitecross Co.'s Works. He takes a keen interest in bowl- ing, and has played for the works' team. PRIVATE ALBERT HANKEY. Died of Wounds.

of war, having been previously employed in ' the construction of the new Warrington Bridge. He was a member of "B" Company. MILITARY FUNERAL AT WARRINGTON.' Yesterday the funeral took place with full military 'honours at the Warrington Cemetery. A detachment of wounded Territoria„ls were present, in charge of Captain Eric Fairclough. The Band of the 2/ 4th South Lancashire Regi- ment, in charge of Band-Sergeant R. Sadler, played the Dead March in " Saul " and Chopin's "March Funebre " en route to the Cemetery. The body was borne to the grave by several of the deceased's comrades, while a firing party fired several volleys over the grave and the "Last Post" was sounded.

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

KILLED. LANCE-CORPORAL H. A. FRYER. Comrade's Sympatheq Letter To Bereaved Parents. Up to the middle of the week .Mr. and Mrs. Fryer, of Wilkinson-street, Warrington, had not received an official intimation from the War Office concerning their son, Lance-Corporal Harold A. Fryer, of the King's Own Scottish Borderers, but they had good reason to fear that he had been killed in action, inasmuch as they

_ RIFLEMAN VICTOR COLTER Old Soldier's Son Gives His Life For His Country. An official intimation received 1511 Wedileeday morning from the War Office by Mr. and Mrs. Colter, of Battersby-lane, conveyed to them the news that one of their three soldier sons, Rifle- man Victor Albert Clarence Colter, had bean killed in action while serving at the Front with the King's Royal Rifles. Beyond the bare inti- mation that he had been killed, nothing was added. A single young fellow, Rifleman Colter would, had he lived, have been nineteen years of age next Monday. Indeed, owing to the nearness of

• *.s' Alait*SdkettVdigia:lg:qi.:'.diVieVin, I . • ' I • elgrig,,,r.Nolt.. • , t4144r. • 140..7 • • .

--mrimm.mmuminonnsemomminissioNWNlMMMESCOMMlENEMll ' ..'7;r•P•4' 14.3644 W '`•4 • , ; TVP? , C 6 , t• , , • q.Ol-1-44ePag: 4";,,titi; , 4P44:4. 41,102Y:,*"'• FV44:41,41*,,0, t',.,'"144,-;41*:4410. frit ztr ittl.4*-AV.tb,rs**,:. ditt, t *-4el;l6llo';.t;t4.'l'4li 111*;.';:;4i k 4-1,1 e 4.404.4*44..

had received a letter from Private G. Durham, of the South Wales Borderers, stating that he was one of a burial party who buried the body of Lance-Corporal Fryer. A Very Sad Ordeal. The letter ran as follows: "No doubt you will think it strange for a stranger to write to you, but I am doing so in order to tell you that being one of a burial party I have had the very sad ordeal of laying a number of our brave heroes to rest. It is with very 'deep regret that I have to undertake to tell you that I came across a very dear friend of yours among the slain, viz., Lance-Corporal Fryer. There was in his pocket a small Prayer-book, which con- tained the following words: 13639 Lance-Cor- poral H. A. Fryer.' The wish was also expressed that if anyone found- this book they would re- turn it td Miss D. Eryer, Wilkinson-street, War- rington. . . . I hope the blow will not be too much for you. He did his duty well for his King, home and country. Please accept my very deepest 'sympathy." Enlisted After War Broke Out. Lance-Corporal Fryer, who was twenty years of. age; received his .education at the National School; Hamilton-street School, and, later, at the Secondary School. He was a member of the Church of England Men's Society, and was connected with gt. Ann's Chirrch all his life. He enlisted on September Ist, 1914, . having previously been employed as a clerk by the Warrington Tanning Company, with whom he had been for a matter ,of seven years. MISSING. PRIVATE J. BROADHURST. Wounded Brother Describes The Battle. Private James Broadhurst, of the King's Own Scottish Borderers, is reported missing since the great advance. Private Broadhurst, who is about twenty years of age, is the son of Mr. Frank Broadhurst, 31, Wellington-street, an employe of the St. Helens Cable Co. He attended the Parochial School, Church-street, and worked, before the war, for the Richmond Gas Stove Co. He has two brothers serving—John, whose experiences were reported in "The Ex- aminer" a fortnight ago, and Henry, who was wounded in the Territorial charge in June. Married Germans ask for Mercy. Private John Broadhurst, writing from the County of London NK.ar Hospital, EpsOm, home to inquire whether anything has been heard of Jim, says : "He wouldn't be very far from me when we went over the parapet on Saturday morning, five o'clock. . . . The shrapnel was fairly rain- ing on us, and the two leading battalions must have been nearly wiped out.
